  • 0240 · …confess one will of our Lord Jesus Christ.» — S. Honorius I, First Letter to Sergius (634), as Maximus quotes it here and as it was read at the Sixth Council (681). The sentence began at the end of mo-025 ("having said that because of the ineffable …").
  • 0240 · …nature, not sin, was assumed by the Godhead» — T. ἐκ τῆς θεότητος προσελήφθη, "was assumed by (ἐκ) the Godhead"; Maximus's gloss "that is, not from (ἐκ) sin" plays on the same preposition: what the Godhead assumed was our nature, not sin.
  • 0240 · …the whole nature also is of the Godhead.» — S. [Ps.-]Athanasius, Against Apollinarius (De incarnatione contra Apollinarium); cf. Gal 4:4 ("born of a woman").
  • 0240 · …own conception without seed from the holy Theotokos — T. Θεοτόκος, Theotokos, "God-bearer," the title of Mary affirmed at Ephesus (431); kept apart from Θεομήτωρ, "Mother of God," which follows below.
  • 0241 · …connatural with us—but other in respect of sinning, — T. τῇ ἁμαρτησίᾳ, a rare form, rendered "in respect of sinning"; the Latin has peccandi pronitate. ἀναμαρτησίᾳ ("in sinlessness") would give the same sense.
  • 0241 · …from transgression against the law of the spirit. — S. Rom 7:23.
  • 0241 · …was not assumed by the Saviour,» he says. — S. Honorius, First Letter to Sergius; cf. Rom 7:23 (ἀντιστρατευόμενον τῷ νόμῳ τοῦ νοός μου).
  • 0241 · …what I will, but whatever thou , Father: — S. John 6:38; Matt 26:39 (cf. Mark 14:36), within Honorius's text.
  • 0241 · …example, that we may follow in his footsteps; — S. 1 Pet 2:21, within Honorius's text.
  • 0244 · …at all. It is likely, then, that they — T. Migne prints Δέος οὖν πολλὰ βαίνειν ἐπιβαλεῖν (confirmed on the plate), which does not construe, and its corruption cannot be named from our text. The English follows the sense of the Latin (verisimile est eos mentiri ac subdere, "it is likely that they lie and slip in"), a conjecture; the [?] marks the place.
  • 0244 · …the most holy presbyter, the lord abba Anastasius, — P. Anastasius, monk and Maximus's disciple and companion, later tried and exiled with him (d. 662).
  • 0244 · …the lord abba John, a most holy fellow-labourer, — P. John, abbot, called Symponus, the papal secretary who drafted Honorius's letter; αὐτοῦ, "at his bidding," is Honorius (the Latin names him: jubente Honorio).
  • 0245 · …presides hierarchically over our blameless and orthodox faith, — P. The bishop over Marinus in Cyprus, the archbishop of Cyprus; not named in the Greek or the Latin.
  • 0245 · …to him as the author of our salvation, — S. Heb 2:10 (τὸν ἀρχηγὸν τῆς σωτηρίας).

Chunk 0027 (mo-027)

  • 0245 · …OPUSCULUM 21 — ON QUALITY, PROPERTY AND DIFFERENCE — T. ἰδιότης is rendered "property" by the glossary (§ I: ἰδιότης = property, ἰδίωμα = idiom); the opusculum table's "idiom" is not followed. Combefis's Latin title reads De qualitate, proprietate et differentia.
  • 0245 · …property, and difference, to Theodore, presbyter in Mazarium.* — P. Theodore, a presbyter otherwise unknown; Mazarium (Μαζάριον) is unidentified. The heading's first line reads ΤΟΥ ΑΥΤΟΥ, "Of the same"; a second line is unreadable in our text.
  • 0245 · …do not think that you, most sacred men, — T. Maximus addresses Theodore in the plural (you) through the opening and the close ("your bidding"), as the Latin does (viri sacratissimi), perhaps Theodore with those about him; he turns to the singular (thou) only in the last sentence. The number is kept as printed.
  • 0248 · …property, and difference—that their signification among those outside — T. οἱ ἔξω, "those outside": the pagan philosophers (Latin profanos scriptores).
  • 0249 · …to him alone and to no other , — T. The bracketed words [τῷ περὶ Θεοῦ λέγεται] stand in brackets in Migne (confirmed on the plate) and construe poorly; the Latin omits them. Rendered as printed; logged as a doubt.
  • 0249 · …but simply and in such a way , — T. The main text reads ἀλλ᾿ ἁπλῶς καὶ τοιῶσδε, "but simply and in such a way"; the bracketed alternative, ὁπωσοῦν καὶ ἁπλῶς, ἀλλὰ τοιῶσδε, "[not] in any way whatever and simply, but in such a way," gives the sense the Latin reads (non quovis modo ac simpliciter, sed tali). Both are rendered.
  • 0252 · …Severus alone and beyond all, the deranged sophist, — P. Severus, Miaphysite patriarch of Antioch (512–518, d. 538), the chief theologian of the "one composite nature," against whose doctrine of a difference of qualities only Maximus argues here.
  • 0252 · …and say this as well: if, as you — T. καθ᾿ ὑμᾶς: "you" here are Severus's partisans, whom Maximus now addresses, not Theodore.
  • 0253 · …not of one genus in essence with him.» — S. Cyril of Alexandria, cf. Ep. 44, to Eulogius (on confessing two natures "so far as to know the difference").
  • 0257 · …that thou mayest bring into kinship with God — T. οἰκειώσῃς Θεῷ: the affective sense of οἰκείωσις (§ V, kinship), not the Christological "appropriation"; the Latin has Deo concilies.

Chunk 0028 (mo-028)

  • 0257 · …THE DIVINE MAXIMUS And again the divine Maximus — T. Opusc. 22 has no title of its own: it is an excerpt joined by a compiler, opening Καὶ πάλιν ὁ θεῖος Μάξιμος. Maximus addresses a plural adversary (ὑμεῖς, "you") who rejects Chalcedon as adding to Nicaea; "he says" introduces that adversary's objection.
  • 0257 · …the faith that is true and truly so? — T. Migne prints ὄντως [πίστεως] (confirmed on the plate), the bracket his. Combefis renders veram rectamque fidem, which may point to ὀρθῆς for ὄντως; the printed text construes and is kept.
  • 0257 · …definition introduced by it? «The confession,» he says, — T. The opponent's objection, quoted. φωνῆς is genitive in the Greek; Combefis takes it as a second subject (voxque illa in duabus naturis). The sense is the same.
  • 0257 · …Cyril and the hundred and fifty as well. — P. Cyril of Alexandria, whose authority stands behind Ephesus (431), and the hundred and fifty Fathers of Constantinople (381): each added words to the Nicene definition.
  • 0260 · …was not even being raised at that time»), — S. Gregory Nazianzen, Ep. 102 (the second letter to Cledonius), 2: the Nicene Fathers spoke deficiently about the Spirit, "for the question had not yet been raised".
  • 0260 · …God-chosen Fathers after the synod of the Nicaeans — T. Migne prints κατὰ τὴν Νικαέων (confirmed on the plate), "at that of the Nicaeans"; Combefis has post Nicaenam synodum, and the sense requires it, since these Fathers ratify the definition of the 318, who are the Nicaeans themselves. The English reads μετὰ ("after"), a conjecture.
  • 0260 · …impiety. At once, then, when Eunomius and Macedonius — P. Eunomius, the Anomoean, and Macedonius, whose name was given to those who denied the Godhead of the Spirit: both were condemned at Constantinople (381).
  • 0260 · …piety and against the pious? And so forth. — T. κ. τὸ ἑ., i.e. καὶ τὰ ἑξῆς: the compiler's excerpt breaks off.
  • 0261 · …of the essence. That essence and the enousial — T. ἐνούσιον, rendered "enousial", formed like "enhypostatic" (ἐνυπόστατον): what exists in an essence and not by itself.
  • 0261 · …both those called essential and those called adventitious; — T. ἐπουσιώδεις, "adventitious": qualities that come upon the essence, as against the essential ones (Latin essentiae adventitiae).
  • 0264 · …presbyter of Alexandria, from the work On Providence. — S. Clement of Alexandria, On Providence: the work is lost, and this definition survives as a florilegium fragment. Printed ἁγνωτάτου ("most pure"); Latin sanctissimi*. Read ἁγιωτάτου (see OCR table).
  • 0264 · …natures of our Lord and Saviour Jesus Christ.* — P. Eulogius, patriarch (pope) of Alexandria 580–608, a Chalcedonian polemicist against the Severans. The heading counts seven chapters; no numerals print in the Greek, so the arabic numbers are ours, from that count.
  • 0265 · …how has the Trinity not become a tetrad? — T. Printed τετρὰς ἢ Τριάς ("a tetrad or a Trinity"); Combefis quomodo Trinitas non effecta quaternitas est. Read ἡ Τριάς.
  • 0265 · …say «one incarnate nature of God the Word»; — S. The formula of Cyril (Ep. 46, to Succensus, 2; cf. Ep. 45), which the Severans made their watchword. Here, as everywhere, it is given in the words they use.
  • 0265 · …those outside, a principle of movement and quiescence. — S. Aristotle, Physics II.1, 192b21–22. "Those outside" are the pagan philosophers.
  • 0265 · …(physis) is so called from «having grown» (pephykenai). — T. The etymology depends on the Greek: φύσις from πεφυκέναι, "to have grown, to be so by nature".

Chunk 0029 (mo-029)

  • 0268 · …words «Not as I will, but as thou» — S. Matt 26:39.
  • 0268 · …as God, and shrinks back again as man. — S. This paragraph is close to Chrysostom, In Matthaeum hom. 83.1 (PG 58.746), on the prayer in Gethsemane; Migne prints no quotation marks.
  • 0269 · …thou say that Christ has a theandric will? — S. The «theandric operation» of Dionysius, Ep. 4: here Maximus tests a «theandric will» and finds it unknown to the Father and the Spirit.
  • 0269 · …say «natural»? Thou too wilt confuse, after Severus; — P. Severus of Antioch (d. 538), the chief theologian of the anti-Chalcedonians, whose «one composite nature» Maximus treats as confusion.
  • 0269 · …us moving to pebbles, to two-thirds and thirds — T. διμοιρότριτα, "two-thirds and thirds": the counting-stones (ψῆφοι) of a reckoner apportioning the will into fractions. Combefis tertiasque dimidias.
  • 0269 · …thee, nameless, rank it with the nameless psalms, — T. Printed Ταλμῶν, a non-word; Combefis psalmorum (the untitled, "nameless" psalms of the Psalter). Read ψαλμῶν (OCR table).
  • 0269 · …confess,» according to the great and God-revealing Basil. — S. Basil is named; the passage is not located (cf. Basil, De Spiritu Sancto 18.44–45, on counting and confessing the hypostases).
  • 0269 · …and Saviour Jesus Christ. Written to the orthodox. — T. Combefis's Latin heading has Capita decem*; the Greek heading, damaged at its head, may have lost δέκα.
  • 0272 · …he differs from the Father and the Spirit, — T. The conclusion is condensed: the Word, by the will proper to his flesh, differs from the Father and the Spirit (Combefis carnis voluntate differt a Patre et Spiritu).
  • 0273 · …inclination. 8. If, according to the great Cyril, — S. Cyril of Alexandria: cf. Thesaurus and De sancta Trinitate dialogi on sameness of will following sameness of essence; not located.
  • 0273 · …9. If, according to John the golden mouth, — S. John Chrysostom («golden mouth»): cf. In Matthaeum hom. 83.1, on the flesh's natural shrinking from death.
  • 0273 · …account, fearing a fear where there was none, — S. Ps 13:5 (LXX), cf. Ps 52:6: «they feared a fear where there was no fear».
  • 0273 · …the things signified, to make clear to you — T. σαφηνίσαι ὑμῖν, "to make clear to you"; Combefis reads it as Maximus's offer, ut vobis … explicem. The questions that follow are the ones the defenders of one will must answer.
  • 0273 · …and holds forth about wills, is blind, short-sighted, — S. 2 Pet 1:9, τυφλός ἐστι μυωπάζων.
  • 0273 · …he says nor about what he makes assertion. — S. 1 Tim 1:7, μὴ νοοῦντες μήτε ἃ λέγουσι μήτε περὶ τίνων διαβεβαιοῦνται.
  • 0276 · …questions put to him by Theodore the monk.* — P. Theodore the monk: not otherwise known; the questions survive only in this excerpt. The heading's name is damaged (Οδώρου, read Θεοδώρου).
  • 0276 · …in answer to «of what sort is it?» — T. ἐν τῷ ὁποῖόν τί ἐστι, "in answer to 'of what sort is it?'". Porphyry's formula for species is ἐν τῷ τί ἐστι ("what is it?"); Combefis has in eo quod quid est. The printed ὁποῖον is kept.
  • 0276 · …the Fathers, the true being, and that natural, — T. The line here holds only καῖ before φυσική; whether a word is lost is uncertain. Combefis adds praedicatur. Rendered as printed.
  • 0276 · …discourses on faith to Demetrius, deacon of Vienne, — T. Βιαίνης as printed; Combefis Viennensem. S. Pseudo-Irenaeus: a spurious fragment, known from the monothelite florilegia.
  • 0276 · …appetite of understanding inclining toward what is willed.» — S. Cf. Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics VI.2, 1139b4–5: choice is ὀρεκτικὸς νοῦς or ὄρεξις διανοητική.
  • 0276 · …Clement the Stromatist, from the discourse On Providence. — S. Clement of Alexandria, On Providence* (lost); fragment known from the florilegia.
  • 0277 · …against the Arians to Aeglon, bishop of Cynopolis.* — S. Alexander of Alexandria, letter to Aeglon of Cynopolis: lost, and known only from fragments of this kind; of doubtful authenticity.
  • 0277 · …of Antioch, from the discourse On the Soul. — S. Eustathius of Antioch, De anima*: fragment.
  • 0277 · …of Alexandria, from the greater discourse On Faith. — S. Pseudo-Athanasius, Sermo maior de fide* (fragment).
  • 0277 · …bishop of Nyssa, from the discourse Against Apollinarius. — S. Attributed to Gregory of Nyssa, Against Apollinarius; not found in the Antirrheticus*, not located.
  • 0277 · …Photice, from the sixth chapter of the Ascetica. — S. Diadochus of Photice, Capita centum de perfectione* 5 (the definition of τὸ αὐτεξούσιον). The lemma says "sixth chapter".
  • 0277 · …wishes.» Of the holy Anastasius, patriarch of Antioch. — S. Anastasius I of Antioch (559–570, 593–598): fragment, not located.
  • 0277 · …within.» Of the holy Nemesius, bishop of Emesa. — S. Nemesius, De natura hominis 33, after Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics III.3, 1113a10–11 (there the definition of choice).
  • 0277 · …is the inward word, and what the uttered. — S. Nemesius, De natura hominis* 14.
  • 0280 · …natural will, and what a will of inclination?* — S. Maximus: the "syllogisms on wills" are not extant as a separate work; cf. Opusc. 1 and 16 for the definitions.
  • 0280 · …the presbyter: What is volition, and what choice?* — S. Maximus, Opusc. 1 (to Marinus), PG 91.12–13.
  • 0280 · …and inclination and prudence and opinion and authority. — T. ὄπιθεν, "back, behind"; Combefis renders infra ("below"). The note is the compiler's pointer to the definitions of counsel, inclination, prudence, opinion and authority in the full letter (Opusc. 1).

Chunk 0030 (mo-030)

  • 0280 · …Faith, whose beginning is: «Undefiled is the Word.» — S. Pseudo-Justin, To Euphrasius the sophist, On Providence and Faith*: a lost spurious work, known from florilegium fragments like these.
  • 0280 · …against the Arians to Aeglon, bishop of Cynopolis.* — S. Alexander of Alexandria, letter to Aeglon of Cynopolis (cf. Opusc. 26); a fragment, of doubtful authenticity.
  • 0280 · …indicative of every essence.» Of Gregory of Nyssa. — S. Attributed to Gregory of Nyssa; not located (cf. the definitions of Opusc. 26).
  • 0281 · …is its life according to nature.» Of Chrysostom. — S. Attributed to John Chrysostom; not located.
  • 0281 · …it from the others.» Of the holy Cyril. — S. Cyril of Alexandria: cf. Thesaurus and the Dialogues on the Trinity; not located.
  • 0281 · …suffering any such thing.» Of the holy Basil. — S. Attributed to Basil; cf. Basil, Ep. 189.6–7 (Gregory of Nyssa, Ad Eustathium), on operation leading to the nature.
  • 0281 · …Of the same, from the books against Eunomius. — S. The pseudo-Basilian Adversus Eunomium IV–V (PG 29), not Basil's three genuine books.
  • 0284 · …the same.» Of the holy Gregory of Nyssa. — S. Gregory of Nyssa, Ad Eustathium de sancta Trinitate (= Basil, Ep. 189.6–7), on the identity of operation in the Trinity.
  • 0284 · …commonness of the nature is shown in act, — T. ἐνεργῶς, "in act", the adverb of ἐνέργεια: shown by its operation, not merely in conception.
  • 0284 · …performed by the Father and the Holy Spirit — T. The Son is absent here in the Greek and in Combefis's Latin alike (a Patre ac Spiritu sancto); the source text names Father, Son and Spirit. Rendered as printed.
  • 0284 · …are separated together from one another.» Of Cyril. — S. Cyril of Alexandria: cf. Thesaurus 32 (PG 75), on operation proving sameness of essence; not exactly located.
  • 0285 · …from the second book to Gratian the emperor. — S. Ambrose, De fide* II.8.70, here in Greek dress.
  • 0285 · …the holy Basil, from the syllogisms against Eunomius. — S. Pseudo-Basil, Adversus Eunomium* IV (PG 29.673–676), on ὁ ἐνεργῶν, ἐνέργεια, ἐνέργημα.

Migne's notes not placed in the running text

Migne's foot-of-page apparatus was read for every page (translate/maximus-opuscula/notes/result-*.jsonl). Placed in the text: 120 of 124 numbered Greek notes, and the Scripture notes whose quotation a translator had marked. The rest are listed here by page: notes to Migne's own prefatory notice, his cautions on the Latin, and Scripture references with no marked quotation.

  • p. 0011 (notae, marker illegible): Quod mihi cum Francisco Turriano, viro alioqui erudito probeque in Græcis versato, sequentia qui opuscula Ingolstadii anno 1615 Latine solum edidit, in his Maximi opusculis non ubique convĕnit, eorumque novam ipse adornavi versionem, cum Græco ipso textu ex probatissimis codd. arcessito, producendam (id est, ex rev. Gerasimi Blacchi codice, ex meliori viri et Raph. Dufresnii emendato,) operæ pretium putavi paucula adnotare, quibus et lucis aliquid forte auctori afferam, et mei consilii rationem, ejus judicium omne permittendo lectori, aperiam, uti fere primo sextoque tom. Operum Chrysostomi factitatum Ducæo. — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): What I have in common with Franciscus Turrianus, an erudite man and well versed in Greek, who published the following opuscula in Latin only at Ingolstadt in 1615, is that in these opuscula of Maximus he does not agree everywhere; and I have prepared my own new version of them, to be issued with the Greek text itself drawn from the best manuscripts (that is, from the codex of Rev. Gerasimus Blachus, emended from the better one of Raphael Dufresne). I thought it worth the effort to add a few notes, by which I may perhaps bring some light to the author and open up the reason for my plan, leaving the whole judgment to the reader, as I mostly did in the first and sixth volumes of the works of Chrysostom, in Ducaeus's manner.
  • p. 0011 (notae, a): Τὴν τῶν ὄντων φύσιν περιγράφων. Vis hujus vocis hic est, obliterans, e numero auferens, ac circumscribens; quo sensu etiam Cicero eam usurpavit vocem; nec aliud est quod sequitur περιορίζων. Uti enim ad virtutem superanda natura, ita etiam ad scientiam omnino certam motus omnis temporis ac ævi: quo spectet illud Hesych. Περιοριστόν, περίγραπτον: nec apte Turr. terminans ac definiens. Τὸ ἐνώσεως: plane mendum est, pro γνώσεως: cui uni etiam congruit epithetum ἀπλανοῦς. et quæ contra ἀρετὴν ubique Maximo aliisque distinguitur; scientia scilicet, non unitio vel conjunctio. Optime Schol. Maximi mentem aperit. — keyed to περιγράφων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The sense of this word is: obliterating, removing from the number, and circumscribing; Cicero also used the word in this sense; and what follows, περιορίζων, is no different. For as nature is to be surpassed for virtue, so also for entirely certain knowledge, the motion of all time and age; compare Hesychius: Περιοριστόν, περίγραπτον. Turrianus's 'terminating and defining' is not apt. The word ἐνώσεως is plainly a mistake for γνώσεως, to which alone the epithet ἀπλανοῦς (unerring) is also fitting, and which is everywhere distinguished by Maximus and others from virtue (ἀρετή); that is, knowledge, not union or conjunction. The Scholiast best reveals Maximus's meaning.
  • p. 0012 (numbered, marker illegible): Dufr. φρόνημα.
  • p. 0012 (notae, b): Τοῦ δὲ βίου φύσις. Turrianus: Ratio vero persona vitæ; sive ita habuit ejus cod. sive scribenti obrepsit, loco φύσις, πρόσωπον, manifesto circulo, quem et repræsentat dum in margine ita observat: Ratio sustentatur vita, et vita ratione; s[?]. est ratio hypostasis sive persona vitæ, et vita rationis. Frigida hæc, nec ex Maximi mente, quam Schol. Dufrenii cod. recte aperit; ipseque conatus sum reddere. Rationis vita persona exstitit, qua scilicet repræsentetur et conspicua fiat, uti alibi ipse Maximus; vel etiam ei basis exsistat: vita autem natura seu essentia, ratio, quæ vim omnem vitæ tribuat, id est, virtuti; sitque illi fere, id quod anima corpori. — keyed to πρόσωπον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Of life, the nature.' Turrianus has: 'Reason, but the person of life', whether his codex read so or it slipped in on the writer, with πρόσωπον in place of φύσις, plainly by a circle, which he also reproduces when he notes in the margin: 'Reason is sustained by life, and life by reason'; that is, reason is the hypostasis or person of life, and life of reason. This is cold and not from Maximus's mind, which the Scholiast of Dufresne's codex rightly discloses; and I have myself tried to render it. Life became the person of reason, by which it is represented and made conspicuous, as Maximus himself says elsewhere; or it may even be its base: life, however, is nature or essence, reason that which assigns all force to life, that is, to virtue; and it is to it roughly what the soul is to the body.
  • p. 0013 (lettered, a): Nemes. cap. 33. — keyed to διειληφότες
  • p. 0013 (lettered, marker illegible): Contra potius, quibus voluntate appeti, omnino etiam, eligi; sed ita etiam in Nemesio. — keyed to appeti — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Rather the reverse: for those to whom it belongs to be desired by will, certainly also to be chosen; but so it is also in Nemesius.
  • p. 0013 (notae, c): Ὄρεξις φανταστικὴ τῶν ἐφ' ἡμῖν καὶ οὐκ ἐφ' ἡμῖν. Sensus integer est, nec aliud habent Dufrenii cod. et Venet. in quo etiam, vel certe ejus typographo, deest τὸ, καὶ οὐκ ἐφ' ἡμῖν· non levi mendo: quo tamen ipso ad explendam lacunam ex Turriani codice proclive utor. Explicavit illo Maximus, quid sit τὸ φανταστικόν· esse nimirum τὸ διανοητικόν, vim scilicet cogitandi, ad quam sequitur et cui comes appetitio eorum quæ in nostra sunt facultate, et quæ in ea minime posita sunt; absque scilicet deliberatione rationis, quæ est solum τῶν ἐφ' ἡμῖν, eorum quæ nostræ sunt facultatis: nec bene additur in Turriano, et quæ non sunt. Stultum enim est deliberare de his quæ non sunt in nostra facultate: non autem simplici appetitu ex phantasia ac cogitando ea velle, motu potius brutis affine, quam ex ratione. Lacunæ occasio fuit repetitio τοῦ, τῶν ἐφ' ἡμῖν, quo nihil proclivius, ut aliquid omittatur. — keyed to ἡμῖν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The reading 'Appetite that is imaginative of what is up to us and not up to us': the sense is whole; Dufresne's codex and the Venetian have nothing else, in which (or at least in its printer's copy) the phrase 'and not up to us' is missing, by no slight fault; to fill the gap I readily use Turrianus's codex. Maximus there explains what the imaginative faculty (phantastikon) is: namely the intellectual (dianoetikon), the power of thinking, upon which follows, as companion, the appetite for things that are in our power and for things not at all in it, without the deliberation of reason, which belongs only to what is up to us. Turrianus does not do well to add 'and what are not'; for it would be foolish to deliberate about things that are not in our power, and not by simple appetite to wish them by imagining and thinking, a motion more akin to brutes than from reason. The occasion of the gap was the repetition of 'τῶν ἐφ' ἡμῖν', than which nothing is easier, so that something is omitted.
  • p. 0013 (notae, d): Οὐ πᾶσιν. Hæc ex Nem. cap. 33, totidem vocibus; in quibus aliquid forte luxatum, ut quadrent cum inductione quæ sequitur, eorum, quæ a nobis appeti voluntate possint, sed non eligi, quod non sint nostræ facultatis, nec habeant rationem medii, sed finis. Esset ergo dicendum: Non omnibus quibus convenit voluntate appeti, omnino etiam convenit eligi: nisi excidit vox, καὶ τὸ ἔμπαλιν, vicissimque; ad quam spectet illa inductio. — keyed to πᾶσιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Not for all.' This is from Nemesius, chapter 33, in the same words; in which something is perhaps dislocated, so that they fit the induction that follows, of things that can be desired by will but not chosen, because they are not within our power and do not have the character of a means but of an end. So one should say: 'Not for all to whom it belongs to be desired by will does it also belong to be chosen', unless the phrase 'and conversely' has dropped out, to which that induction would refer.
  • p. 0014 (numbered, marker illegible): Nemes. c. 34.
  • p. 0016 (notae, e): Περὶ φρονήματος. Etiam Cicero laborat reddenda hac voce. Aliis cogitatum, sensus, prudentia, intelligentia, sapientia; sic enim etiam φρονεῖν, sapere: quandoque etiam animus et appetitus. Quam hic Maximus harum vocum proprietatem observat, auctores liberiore earum usu fere negligunt. Quod definit Maximus, φρόνησιν, id est prudentiam, ἕξιν ἀνοίας καὶ ὑπερνοίας ἀντίπαλον: non bene reddit Turr. sive ejus cod. habuit ἀγνοίας, sive fefellit oculus, aut mens præcurrit, Ignorantiæ adversariam, imo, mentis inopiæ, dementiæ, imprudentiæ per defectum; uti ὑπερνοίας, per excessum, quam sufficientiam, dicere possimus; vitium scilicet ὑψινοίᾳ affine, id est superbiæ; qua quis eximie et supra reliquos putat se sapere. — keyed to φρονήματος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): On φρόνημα (mindset). Cicero too has trouble rendering this word. Others render it: thought, sense, prudence, intelligence, wisdom; so also φρονεῖν, to be wise; sometimes also mind and appetite. How carefully Maximus observes the proper sense of these words, while authors almost neglect them in their freer usage. Maximus defines φρόνησις, that is prudence, as 'a state opposed to folly and to over-wisdom': Turrianus (or his codex, which had ἀγνοίας; or his eye failed, or his mind ran ahead) does not render it well as 'opposed to ignorance'; rather, 'to poverty of mind, madness, imprudence by defect', as ὑπερνοία (over-wisdom), by excess, we may call self-sufficiency; a vice akin to ὑψινοία, that is, pride, by which one thinks oneself exceptionally wise and above the rest.
  • p. 0018 (notae, f): Καὶ ἄδηλον ἐχόντων τὸ τέλος· Sic Dufrenii cod. pro quo errore Venet. Εὔδηλον· quod etiam habuit Turriani, dum reddit, et certum finem habent; imo certe incertum: sic enim ipse Maximus, Περὶ ὧν βουλευόμεθα· De quibus deliberamus (quæ ipsa sunt circa quæ electio versatur) non satis esse ait, ut in nostra sint facultate, et per nos fiant; sed necessario addendum, καὶ ἄδηλον ἐχόντων τὸ τέλος· et incertum habent finem: cujus debuit vir doctus non sic facile oblivisci; cum præcipue res sic plana sit, et sic habeat Nem. textus, quem Maximus sublegit. — keyed to τέλος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'And having an uncertain end.' So Dufresne's codex; for which the Venetian has, by error, Εὔδηλον ('quite clear'), which Turrianus also had when he rendered 'and have a certain end'; rather, certainly 'uncertain': for Maximus himself says in 'On what we deliberate' (the very things about which choice is exercised) that it is not enough that they be in our power and come about through us, but one must necessarily add 'and having an uncertain end'. The learned man ought not so easily to have forgotten this, especially as the matter is so plain and Nemesius's text has it, which Maximus follows.
  • p. 0018 (notae, g): Τῆς τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν ὀρεκτοῦ. Venet. ὀρεκτικοῦ, habuitque Turr. cod. plane mendose, nec ipse ullum commodum sensum elicere potuit. Nempe, qui per naturam ὀρεκτικοί sunt (appetendi facultate prædití) ejus nanciscentur mysticam fruitionem seu possessionem, qui per naturam τὸ ὀρεκτὸν est (ipsum natura appetibile) Dei scilicet. — keyed to ὀρεκτοῦ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Of that which is by nature object of desire.' The Venetian has ὀρεκτικοῦ ('of the desiring'), and Turrianus's codex had it too, quite wrongly, nor could he himself draw out any fitting sense. For those who are by nature desiring (endowed with the faculty of desire) will attain the mystical enjoyment or possession of that which is by nature the desirable (that which is itself desirable by nature), namely God.
  • p. 0019 (lettered, c): 1 Tim. ii, 4. — keyed to salus
  • p. 0019 (lettered, d): 1 Cor. xii, 29. — keyed to prophetæ
  • p. 0021 (notae, h): Ὁ κατὰ φύσιν ὑφίσταται λόγος. Huic opponit quod vitii est, παρυφίσταται quasi umbra quadam ac simulacro exsistit. Id enim mali est, quod non tam ens sit, quam ab eo defectus. Sic Basil. in Hexaem. Οἱονεὶ ἀποσκίασμα τῆς δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ ὄντα, αὐτομάτως παρυφίσταται: de illis qui mundum ab æterno ac illi coæternum ponebant. Sic et passim Dionys. Proprie itaque ac delectu verborum locutus est Maximus, etsi Turrianus una voce, nec satis expressa, utraque involvit. Evadit ratio, aut modus. — keyed to λόγος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'The reason according to nature subsists.' To this he opposes what belongs to vice, παρυφίσταται (exists alongside), exists as a kind of shadow and semblance. For that is the character of evil, that it is not so much a being as a falling away from it. So Basil in the Hexaemeron: 'as a shadow of its power, existing of itself it comes to subsist alongside': said of those who posited the world from eternity and coeternal with it. So too Dionysius frequently. Maximus therefore spoke properly and with a choice of words, although Turrianus wraps both in one word, not sufficiently expressed. It comes out as 'reason' or 'manner'.
  • p. 0021 (notae, i): Τὰ τῆς ἡμῶν ἐπιτιμίας. Pœnas scilicet pec- — keyed to ἐπιτιμίας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'The things of our penalty.' That is, the penalties for sins …
  • p. 0022 (notae, i): cati reatu, exque Adæ prævaricatione universo humano generi invectas; quas Maximus illa quæst. 24, alterum peccatum, quod Christus factus est, Paulina phrasi, exponit: nempe τὸ παθητὸν naturæ, citra omnem culpæ reatum, quo et nobis incorruptionem ac immortalitatem paravit ἀδιάβλητα πάθη vocat Damasc., et alii, quibus sponte factum obnoxium Christum fides docet. Non bene Turr. vir alioqui doctus, passiones nostræ honestatis; nec ipse ubique libratissimas viri θεολογικωτάτου voces, qualis est Maximus, assecutus. Τὰ ἐπιτίμια, vox vel tironibus, in re præsertim ecclesiastica, notissima. Sic passim Græci pœnas omnes ecclesiasticas vocitant, ac quas nos dicimus censuras, scita paranomasia in vocibus, τὰ τῆς ἐπιτιμίας, καὶ τὰ τῆς ἀτιμίας: quæ sunt Latinis, pœna et culpa. Primum certe Christus assumpsit, eoque alterum (quod et sibi arrogavit) sanavit, eaque natura sustulit; quomodo nobis τῆς ἑπομένης φυσικῆς ἀφθαρσίας ἀρραβῶνα δεδώρηται, donavit arrhabonem incorruptionis quæ sequitur ac comes est illis in Christo pœnis: quæ ipsa naturæ innovatio est, sic Maximo, Nysseno, Damasceno, aliisque Patribus Græcis celebrata; naturæ scilicet rectitudo, qualis in Adamo condita est, ipsa in Christo præcedens, ut in capite, ac futura in nobis, ut ejus membris; cujus et nobis in eo arrhabo est. Nec bene Turr. fecit munere suo esse pignus naturalis immortalitatis; quam Christus non assumpsit, sed contrarium, τὸ παθητὸν carnis, ejus vim patiendi ac moriendi; ex Adamo scilicet reo unum hoc mutuatus, ad ejus alterum in nobis reparandum; priorem scilicet ipsius rectitudinem, quæ est illa ejus incorruptio naturalis; summa nempe immunitas ab omni eo quod ex peccato nobis inolevit. — keyed to ἐπιτιμίας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ... of the guilt of sin, and brought upon the whole human race from Adam's transgression; which Maximus, in that question 24, explains as 'the other sin', which Christ was made, in Pauline phrase: namely the passible element (to pathēton) of nature, free of any guilt, by which he also procured incorruption and immortality for us; Damascene and others call them 'blameless passions', from which the faith teaches that Christ was voluntarily liable. Turrianus, otherwise learned, does not do well with 'passions of our honesty'; nor has he everywhere grasped the finely balanced words of so theological a man as Maximus. The word ἐπιτίμια is known even to beginners, especially in ecclesiastical matters. So the Greeks commonly call all ecclesiastical penalties, what we call censures, with a clever play on words: 'what belongs to penalty' and 'what belongs to dishonour', which in Latin are 'penalty and guilt'. Christ first assumed the one, and by it healed the other (which he also claimed as his own), and removed the nature of it; whereby he has given us a pledge of the following natural incorruption, he has given a pledge of the incorruption that follows and accompanies those penalties in Christ: which is the very renewal of nature, thus celebrated by Maximus, Nyssen, Damascene and other Greek Fathers; namely the rectitude of nature, as it was established in Adam, going first in Christ as in the head, and to come in us as in his members; of which we too have the pledge in him. Nor did Turrianus do well to make it, by his office, a pledge of natural immortality; which Christ did not assume, but its contrary, the passible element of the flesh, its power of suffering and dying; borrowed from guilty Adam for this one thing, to repair in us his other, namely his original rectitude, which is his natural incorruption; the supreme immunity from everything that has grown in us from sin.
  • p. 0023 (notae, j): Προσεπήγαγον. Non bene Turr. in tertia persona, solius Dei esse adjunxerunt. Ipse enim Maximus est, qui pro se ibi excusat; uti etiam aliis locis, quibus alio modo favere novæ doctrinæ visus sit. Hic clare aperit, quomodo unam solius Dei in beatis operationem, deificationem dixerit; quod nempe actio illa quæ est beatitudo (Dei scilicet visio ac fruitio), ex Deo sit in nobis operante supra totam naturæ ordinem; etsi vere ipsi operemur et vitaliter, at non quasi ipsi nobis actionis principium ac auctores; sed quasi Deo trahente ad id quod divinum est: quo ipso modo dixerit Maximus, non esse illam δικαίων ἔργων τοῖς ἁγίοις ἀντίδοσιν, quod omnino excedat operum merita, quæ ipsa in nobis Dei sint munera, ut loquitur Augustinus. Sed de his theologi. — keyed to προσεπήγαγον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'They added.' Turrianus does not do well with 'in the third person, they added that it is of God alone'. For Maximus himself is the one who there excuses himself; as also in other places where he seemed in some way to favour the new doctrine. Here he plainly shows how he called the single operation of God alone in the blessed deification: namely that the action which is blessedness (the vision and enjoyment of God) comes about in us with God working above the whole order of nature; although we do truly work, and vitally, but not as if we were ourselves the principle and authors of the action, but as drawn by God toward what is divine; in which very manner Maximus says that it is not a requital of the saints for just works, since it wholly exceeds the merits of works, which are themselves God's gifts in us, as Augustine says. But on these things the theologians.
  • p. 0023 (notae, k): Τῶν ἀγαθῶν τὴν ἀπόλαυσιν πάσχουσαν. Quasi scilicet passive, divino quodam Dei experimento, et quo unus ipse agens videatur; veluti se res habet in principali agente motum imprimente, et in instrumento recipiente; cujus ipse motus instrumenti efficitur. Sic alte Maximus altum adeo pertractat θεώρημα, nec quidquam Christi mysterio officere ostendit: quod non ea motione ac afflatu illapsuque explicandum, sed quod Verbum vere homo factum est, ac quidquid hominis erat, una — keyed to πάσχουσαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Experiencing the enjoyment of good things.' As if, that is, passively, through a certain divine experience of God, and in which God alone seems to be the agent; just as the case is with a principal agent imparting motion and an instrument receiving it, whose motion is brought about in the instrument. So Maximus deeply treats so lofty a theorem, and shows that it does nothing against the mystery of Christ: it is not to be explained by that motion, breath and inflow, but because the Word was truly made man, and whatever belonged to man, together ...
  • p. 0024 (notae, k): dempto peccato assumpserit. Turriano desunt una fere aut altera linea; nec quæ habet, ipsa illi satis expedita sunt. Quem ultimo scholio perstringit Nilum, Dufrenii codicis Antiquarius, idem sæpius sugillat quasi non ecclesiastice de Christo ejusque mysterio sentientem. Quis vero hic Nilus sit, ac num cujus exstat libellus adversus primatum papæ, Nilus Thessalonicensis, cui erudite respondit Matthæus Caryophilus archiepiscopus Iconiensis, vir egregie orthodoxus, incertum est. Si tamen is ipse est, et hoc observatu dignum, quod sic Romanæ infensus Ecclesiæ ac schismaticus, nec in exploratis dogmatis sanæ fidei est: videantque nostri novatores quem tanti hominem faciant, suæque velint aciei præstantem ducem. — keyed to πάσχουσαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ... took on everything of man, sin excepted. Turrianus lacks about a line or two here; and what he has is not sufficiently worked out for him. The Nilus whom he touches in the last scholion, the Antiquary of Dufresne's codex, he often criticises as if he did not think ecclesiastically about Christ and his mystery. Who this Nilus is, and whether he is the Nilus of Thessalonica whose book against the primacy of the pope survives, to whom Matthew Caryophilus, archbishop of Iconium, a man outstandingly orthodox, replied learnedly, is uncertain. But if it is the same man, this too is worth noting: that though hostile to the Roman Church and a schismatic, he is not unsound in the examined points of sound faith; and let our innovators see what man they make of him, and whether they wish to have him as a leader at the head of their line.
  • p. 0026 (notae, l): Κεφάλ. ν΄. Sic Dufrenii codex breviate, quæ nota est capitis quinquagesimi, ejus operis, quod Maximus ad Marinum presbyterum scripsit; sicque ex Romanis codd. Caput quinquagesimum, rite ascripsit Lucas Holstenius. At Turriani codex, ipse forsitan quod et apographum a reverendissimo Gerasimo Blaccho acceptum, Κεφάλαια πεντήκοντα, dum reddit, Capita quinquaginta, parum certe accuratum agit interpretem, cui nec sic apertum mendum subolucrit. Quis enim Quinquaginta hic Capita vel somniet, non unum ipsum Quinquagesimum: a quo statim sequitur Quinquagesimum primum; quæ duo ex tanta illa Maximi lucubratione nobis exstant? Ad eumdem Marinum, Πρὸς τὸν αὐτὸν Μαρῖνον, unius Blacchi codicis est. Quodque tantum Maximi opus ad Marinum excidit, cujus nulla dubitatio esse potest, una hac ac altera spica tanti manipuli in his quasi Collectaneis nobis superstite, quid mirum excidisse et epistolam, qua apud eumdem excusat pro diplomate Roma a papa misso Constantinopolim, ut nusquam integra exstet, sed sola quasi excerpta, iis passim relata, qui de Spiritus processione, tuenda aut impugnanda Latinorum hoc argumento fide, scripserunt, ut valde frivolum inde argumentum ducant etiam viri catholici, quasi περὶ νοθείας laborare intelligatur, cum alias Maximi characteres habeat, τῶν σχολαστικῶν, nec veterum, pro Maximi ætate, testimonio careat. Magis miror, quod card. Baronius opus hoc quinquaginta capitibus distinctum, ipsam existimat ad Marinum epistolam, quæ est hic prima inter editas Gretsero ex Turriani versione, cum illa vix excedat duodecim capita: post quam sequitur caput illud 51. ex dicto opere de operationibus et voluntatibus. Nimirum granditas operis prohibuit ill. scriptorem librum adire, alioquin plurimum de re ecclesiastica meritus esset, si nactus integrum ejusmodi opus, saltem indicasset. — keyed to κεφάλ. ν΄ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Chapter 50. So Dufresne's codex gives it in abbreviated form, which is the sign of the fiftieth chapter of that work which Maximus wrote to Marinus the priest; and so, from the Roman codices, Lucas Holstenius rightly wrote 'chapter fiftieth'. But Turrianus's codex (perhaps itself a copy received from the most reverend Gerasimus Blachus), in rendering κεφάλαια πεντήκοντα as 'fifty chapters', acts as a rather inaccurate interpreter, to whom even so the plain error did not occur. For who would imagine 'fifty chapters' here, not the fiftieth alone, which is immediately followed by the fifty-first, the two of that great labour of Maximus that survive for us? 'To the same Marinus' (Πρὸς τὸν αὐτὸν Μαρῖνον) is found in one Blachus codex alone. And that so great a work of Maximus to Marinus has perished, about which there can be no doubt, with only this and one other ear of so great a sheaf surviving to us in these 'collectanea', what wonder that the letter too has perished in which he excuses himself to the same Marinus about the diploma sent from Rome by the pope to Constantinople, so that it exists nowhere entire but only as excerpts, reported here and there by those who wrote on the procession of the Spirit, defending or attacking the argument of the Latins, so that even Catholic men draw a quite frivolous argument from it, as if it were understood to be a question of spuriousness, when otherwise it has Maximus's scholastic characteristics, and is not lacking in the testimony of the ancients, given Maximus's date. I am more astonished that Cardinal Baronius thinks this work, distinguished by fifty chapters, to be the very letter to Marinus which stands here first among those edited by Gretser from Turrianus's version, though that scarcely exceeds twelve chapters; after which follows that chapter 51 from the said work on the operations and wills. Obviously the size of the work prevented the illustrious writer from consulting the book; otherwise he would have deserved very well of the church, had he found the work entire and at least pointed it out.
  • p. 0027 (notae, m): Ποιότητα. Sic quoque Turr. Qualitatem non divisam: sed palam mendum est, ac reponendum, quantitatem, quam aliis passim locis, distinctionis omnino comitem ait, ejusque notam numerum esse, qui ipse minime dividat; sed pro eorum quæ numerata sunt, ratione, vel continuus, vel discretus exsistat. Paulo etiam superius, nihil apte idem Turrianus, ita jungit, διασπίγγων τὴν σύγχυσιν τῶν ἐναντίων, confusionem contrariorum astringat; quasi Maximus contraria vocet naturas in Christo, aut qualitates; non ipsam distinctionem et confusionem; quibus contrariis Nestorius Severusque contraria statuebant, non evertebant, uti ipse Maximus satis clare exponit. Stulta religiositas sic scribæ festinantis manu in præceps agi, ut vel cum luce clarior aberratio est, ipseque perspicuus auctoris sensus, emendare nolis, ac vel indicare quod emendandum videris. — keyed to ποιότητα — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Quality.' So also Turrianus: 'quality not divided'; but the error is plain, and one must restore 'quantity', which in other places he says is always the companion of distinction, and its mark is number, which itself divides not at all, but according to the nature of the things numbered exists either continuous or discrete. A little earlier too, Turrianus joins something not at all aptly, 'confusion of contraries', as if Maximus called the contrary things natures in Christ, or qualities, not the very distinction and confusion; by which contraries Nestorius and Severus established opposites, they did not overturn them, as Maximus himself explains clearly enough. A foolish scrupulousness, thus to be carried headlong by the hand of a hurrying scribe, so that even where the error is clearer than daylight and the author's sense itself is evident, you would not emend, or at least point out what you see needs emending.
  • p. 0028 (notae, n): Συνήγαγε τῇ τῶν γνωμικῶν θελημάτων διαφορᾷ. Clara Maximi sententia; eo quod distinctæ voluntates ex sententia, sequi majoris ac minoris bonitatis esse, quod scilicet vicissim hoc illoque modo procedant, varientque boni prosecutione; a quo proinde nihil vetat deficere: quod est Christo demere τὸ ἀναμάρτητον, quod ab omni immunis peccato est. Turr. Et conjunxit cum differentia voluntatum ex sententia, differentiam sententiæ; eadem voce reddens, quod est Græce διαφορὰ καὶ παραλλαγή: quæ voces longe differunt, ac quarum Maximus pro maximo absurdo alteram infert ex altera in ejusmodi voluntatibus, id est, gnomicis; nempe ex distinctione contrarietatem, contra ac dicit Greg. Τὸ ἐκείνου θέλειν, οὐδὲν ὑπεναντίον Θεῷ, ac vertibilitatem in malum seu minus bonum, humanæ Christi voluntatis ex sententia, ut quæ non Verbi nutu regatur, ut personæ moventis atque utentis, sed ipsa suo. — keyed to διαφορᾷ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'He brought together by the difference of the gnomic wills.' Maximus's meaning is clear: because distinct wills by judgment follow a greater or lesser goodness, namely, that they proceed now in this way, now in that, and differ in the pursuit of the good; whence nothing prevents their falling away from it: which is to take from Christ the 'sinless', which means that he is immune from all sin. Turrianus: 'And he joined with the difference of the wills by judgment the difference of judgment', rendering both with the same word, which in Greek is διαφορά and παραλλαγή: words which differ widely, and of which Maximus, as the greatest absurdity, infers the one from the other in wills of this kind, that is, gnomic ones; namely contrariety from distinction, contrary to what Gregory says: 'His willing is nothing opposed to God'; and (infers) changeability toward evil or lesser good in the human will of Christ by judgment, as one that is not governed by the nod of the Word, as of the person moving and using it, but by its own.
  • p. 0029 (notae, o): (o) Ποσότητα γνωμικῶν θελημάτων. Voluntatum a sententia quantitatem. Id est plures numero ejusmodi voluntates ac γνώμας, quasi caro suo, non Verbi nutu regeretur; quod Nestoriani erroris est. Turr. Quantitatem vocat numerum; bene id quidem: sic enim passim Maximus, at non quod addit: Numerus enim est quantitatis discretæ. Hic enim Severianorum ac Monothelitarum scrupulus, ac vero scopulus, inde negantium duas vel naturas vel voluntates, quod numerum existimarent quantitatis discretæ ac dividere; quod Maximus etiam atque etiam refellit, ostenditque solum notam quantitatis esse, pro ejusdem quantitatis, cui adhibetur, ratione: in discretis, discretum seu divisum; in continuis sive conjunctis, conjunctum ac continuum: cum sola distinctione, sive, ut alii amant loqui, differentia (Græce διαφορά;) eorum quæ numerantur: quorum scilicet unum non sit aliud, nec eorum una essentia, quantumvis sint unita. — keyed to θελημάτων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The Greek 'quantity of gnomic wills' means: the voluntates 'of the sentence/opinion' in quantity, that is, several such wills and opinions, as if the flesh were ruled by its own will and not by the Word's bidding, which is Nestorian error. Turrianus: he calls number 'quantity'; rightly so, for Maximus often does, but not in what Turrianus adds, 'number is of discrete quantity'. For this was the stumbling-block of the Severians and Monothelites, who denied two natures or wills on the ground that they thought number belonged to discrete quantity and divided; Maximus refutes this again and again, showing that number is only a mark of quantity, according to the quantity to which it is applied: in discrete things, the discrete or divided; in continuous or conjoined things, the conjoined and continuous; with distinction alone, or as others like to say, difference, of the things numbered, such that one is not the other, nor is there one essence of them, however united they be.
  • p. 0030 (numbered, marker illegible): Matth. xxvi, 39; Luc. xxii, 42. — keyed to fiat
  • p. 0030 (notae, p): (p) Ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ πεφυκέναι θέλειν καὶ θέλειν. Alterum exemplum est Maximi, ejus quod naturæ, et ejus quod personæ: τὸ πεφυκέναι, esse comparatum, ut loqui, sic et velle, id naturæ: ipsum loqui et velle, quasi utentis personæ: nec unum hæc et idem. Male Turr. Sicut naturæ insitum esse velle et velle: quibus verbis idem significatur, et utrumque natura, contra ipsa Maximi verba et mentem. Nihilque apta nota marg.: Aliquo modo hoc scil. vel illo; quasi insitum naturæ, nempe velle — keyed to θέλειν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Another example from Maximus, of what belongs to nature and what to the person: 'to be naturally disposed' means to be so constituted that, as with speaking, so with willing, this is of nature; the actual speaking and willing is that of the person using it; the two are not one and the same. Turrianus is wrong: 'Just as it is innate to nature to will and to will', words that mean the same thing and make both of nature, against Maximus's own words and mind. The note in the margin is no better: 'In some way this, namely willing, or that'; as if willing were innate to nature.
  • p. 0031 (notae, q): (q) Ἐπεὶ τὸν ποιητὴν αἰτιατέον τῆς μέμψεως. Clara sententia; nempe istud vitii ac labis, tribuendum Auctori, qui sic condidisset, quod cum eo natura pugnaret. Nihil bene Turr. Quia auctor esset causa culpæ et querelæ, qui fecisset sibi, quod cum natura pugnaret. Ipse lector, ne sugillandi avidior videar, recognosce ac cense: majorem certe ἀκρίβειαν voluissem in tractatibus sic theologicis. Dispensationis (Græce οἰκονομίας) rationem scite exponit Maximus, ut modum unitionis personalis, qua certa divini consilii ratione, ut nostræ saluti conducebat, innovatæ sunt naturæ, proprie significet; de qua innovatione ad Greg. orat. in diem Christi Natalem. Id sane acute. Dispensatio enim et οἰκονομία, quamdam velut indulgentiam et accommodationem sonat, prout æquitas videtur exigere, posthabito quod ex debiti jurisque rigore est. — keyed to μέμψεως — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Clear sense: namely that this fault and stain is to be ascribed to the Author, who would so have made things that his own creature fought with him by nature. Turrianus does not render it well: 'because the author would be the cause of fault and complaint, who had made for himself what fought with nature.' Let the reader himself recognise and judge, lest I seem too eager to find fault: I should certainly have wished more exactness in theological treatises of this kind. Maximus expounds skilfully the account of the dispensation (Greek oikonomia), as the manner of personal union, by which, by a fixed rationale of the divine counsel, as was conducive to our salvation, the natures were renewed, as the word properly signifies; on this renewal see Gregory's oration on the Nativity of Christ. This is keenly put. For dispensation and oikonomia suggest a kind of indulgence and accommodation, as equity seems to require, setting aside what is of strict debt and right.
  • p. 0031 (notae, r): (r) Καὶ τῆς Ἀπολιναρίου συγχύσεως. Non ita pronum, ut Apollinarii hæresis confusio nuncupetur. In eo erat, quod Christum ut hominem ἄνουν existimabat; sine mente, cujus vices ac officium præstaret Verbum. Erat igitur illi in Christo velut confusio quædam munerum, uti etiam fere Ario ac Eutychi, substantiarum; humana scilicet natura transeunte in divinam, et quasi ab ea absorpta, ut utraque jam una esset essentia, ambarumque conflatum unum; quod signanter Maximus συνουσίωσιν appellavit; cum tamen etiam ipse paulo post confusionis voce Severi dogma, Eutychiano proximum ejusque prolem, traducat. συγκεχυμένη τῶν ψευδῶν διδασκαλία δογμάτων: ubi Turr. Doctrina infusa falsorum dogmatum: pro confusa; quod malim typorum, quam quod manus mentem præcurrerit: et quod paulo ante est in loco Zachar. Et qui habitatis filia Babylonis, pro filiam. Poterant hæc non tantis sordere per Gretzeri vulgantis diligentiam, cum forte Turriano non satis licuerit abs se scripta relegere. — keyed to συγχύσεως — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Not so obvious that the heresy of Apollinaris should be called 'confusion': it consisted in thinking Christ, as man, to be without mind (anous), the Word supplying its place and office. So for him there was in Christ a kind of confusion of functions, as for Arius and Eutyches, almost, of substances: the human nature passing into the divine and as if absorbed by it, so that both were one essence, a blend of the two, which Maximus pointedly called 'synousiosis'; though he himself a little later, by the word 'confusion', stigmatises the dogma of Severus, next to the Eutychian and its offspring: 'a confused teaching of false doctrines', where Turrianus has 'doctrine infused of false doctrines', for 'confused'; which I would rather put down to the typesetters than to a slip of the hand outrunning the mind; and what is a little earlier in the passage of Zachariah, 'And you who dwell, daughter of Babylon', for 'daughter'. These things could have been less careless had Gretser, in publishing, been diligent, when perhaps Turrianus had not had time enough to reread what he had written.
  • p. 0032 (lettered, i): Psal. cxxxvi, 8. — keyed to Babylonis
  • p. 0032 (notae, s): (s) Τόπος ζοφερός. Locus caliginosus; sic rite dicta Severi mens. Aquilonis typo. Venet. τύπος ζοφερός, quod et Turr. reddit, facta forma caliginosa: quod certe durum, longeque a metaphora; ut et quod illi sequitur, privata divini luminis usu et exercitatione; cum et istud plane aquilonis locum, lucisque in eo absentiam, metaphorice respiciat. — keyed to ζοφερός — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'A gloomy place': a place of darkness; this is rightly said of Severus's mind. [Reading] in the Aquilo print: the Venice edition has 'a gloomy type', which Turrianus also renders 'a gloomy form made': which is certainly harsh and far from the metaphor; as is also what follows, 'deprived of the use and exercise of the divine light'; since that too plainly looks metaphorically to the place of the north and to the absence of light in it.
  • p. 0032 (notae, t): (t) Ἢ πάντως. Legit Turr. ac reddit relative, ᾗ, quæ necessario, quod non satis cohæret: melius disjunctive, ἢ, habent nostri cod. — keyed to πάντως — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Turrianus read and rendered it relatively, 'by which', 'which necessarily', which does not cohere well; better disjunctively, 'or', as our codices have.
  • p. 0033 (notae, u): (u) Τῇ δὲ παραγραφῇ τῆς μιᾶς. Sic et Turr. Et sublata una operatione naturali; de suo addens, ut par est, duas posteriores voces, quasi explicationis causa; nec certe abs re, aut extra Maximi subtilitatem. Quod enim ex Severianorum sententia voluntas ad operationem sequatur, vicissimque se illæ et interimant et ponant, sublata una operationum, Christus ἄβουλος erit, sine voluntate, et ἀνυπόστατος, non vere exsistens et in persona, quod illorum sententia, voluntas inferat personam, vicissimque operationes naturales sublatæ ἀνούσιον faciant; quia quod omni caret operatione et actu ac virtute, ut passim Maximus ex Patrum doctrina, id neque est aut vere substantiam habet. — keyed to μιᾶς — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'By the deletion of the one.' So also Turrianus: 'And with the one natural operation taken away'; adding on his own, as is fitting, the last two words by way of explanation, and surely not beside the point or outside Maximus's subtlety. For since on the Severians' view will follows operation, and the two reciprocally destroy and establish each other, once one of the operations is removed Christ will be 'without will' and 'without hypostasis', not truly existing and in a person, because on their view will brings in person, and the natural operations, once removed, make him 'without essence'; because what lacks all operation and act and power, as Maximus says everywhere from the teaching of the Fathers, neither is nor truly has substance.
  • p. 0034 (notae, v): (v) Μὴ πέφυκε. Ipse nimirum Pater, non γεώργιον, uti Turr. reddit. Nempe id alienum a Deo, ut e[…]ret, et conservet suumque ducat, quidquid alienæ plantationis est; quod unum inductæ auctoritati apte respondet. — keyed to πέφυκε — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Is not by nature': that is, the Father himself, not 'the field' (georgion), as Turrianus rendered it. Namely, what is foreign to God, that he should [eject?] and keep, and count as his own whatever belongs to another's planting; which alone answers aptly to the authority adduced.
  • p. 0035 (notae, x): (x) Τὴν οἰανοῦν περιστολήν. Περιστέλλειν Suid. περικαλύψαι, obtegere, obvelare: nec alio sensu hic verbale Maximo sumitur aut exponitur, quidquid Turr. contractionem reddat, ac si esset συστολήν. Loquitur Maximus de viro excellenter spiritali, cui tribuit, ut nullo spiritalium tædio afficiatur, nihilque tecta ac occulta habeat, quæ vim mentis superant, id est, divina, quæ pro ejus captu, ipsa pene nuda, nullisque figuris ac formis adumbrata, illi patefiant et inferantur. — keyed to περιστολὴν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Any covering whatever.' Suidas: peristellein = to cover over, to veil. The verbal noun is taken in no other sense here by Maximus, whatever Turrianus renders as 'contraction', as if it were sustole. Maximus speaks of the eminently spiritual man, to whom he attributes that he is touched by no weariness of spiritual things and has nothing covered or hidden which surpasses the mind's power, that is, divine things, which are shown and brought to him, almost bare and shadowed by no figures or forms, according to his capacity.
  • p. 0035 (notae, y): (y) Τῇ κενώσει τοῦ Λόγου. Verbi exinanitione, qua ejus nos plenitudinem nancisci rite ait: τὸ κατ' αὐτὸν πλήρωμα. Mendose Turr. ἑνώσει legit, unione Verbi. Infra quoque, τέλος ἱερὸν ἐκομίσω τὴν σωτηρίαν, non satis apte, salutem, quæ extremum est, reportasti. Est certe finis, non quodvis extremum, sed quod intentum, et in quo quiescatur; quod sic sine addito extremum dicere, eaque voce τὸ τέλος reddere, uti non raro Turr. factitat, non satis grammaticum est. — keyed to Λόγου — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'By the emptying of the Word.' By the Word's self-emptying, through which he rightly says we obtain his fulness: 'the fulness according to him'. Turrianus wrongly reads 'union' (henosei), 'by the union of the Word'. Below too, 'telos hieron ekomiso ten soterian', he renders not quite aptly 'you have brought back salvation, which is the extreme'. A telos is certainly an end, not any extremity but what is aimed at and in which one comes to rest; to say 'extreme' so, without addition, and to render the word telos by it, as Turrianus often does, is not quite grammatical.
  • p. 0036 (notae, z): (z) Πᾶσαν θεουργὸν ἀποφήνας. Non satis bene Turr. Reddens totam deificatam: vox enim illa activum quid sonat, ac quam Christi humanitas ex unitione cum Verbo mutuata est, divinorum operum vim ac miraculorum. Sic Budæus in Com. θεουργίας vocata ait Christi opera, quæ divinitatem ejus significabant: ipsumque Novum Testamentum Dionysio θεουργία est, ob eamdem causam. Maximo ipso interprete, θεουργικὴ ἐπιστήμη· Sacerdotalis, pontificia scientia. θεουργικὸν μύρον· divinæ virtutis unguentum nempe sanctificans; θεουργοί, qui sacris operantur. — keyed to ἀποφήνας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Having made the whole theurgic.' Turrianus does not render it well: 'making it wholly deified'; for that word sounds like something active, and means the power of divine works and miracles which Christ's humanity borrowed from union with the Word. So Budaeus in his Commentaries says Christ's works that signified his divinity are called 'theurgies'; and for Dionysius the New Testament itself is 'theurgia', for the same reason. In Maximus himself as interpreter, 'theurgic knowledge' means priestly, pontifical knowledge; 'theurgic ointment' means the ointment sanctifying with divine power; 'theurgoi' are those who work at sacred things.
  • p. 0037 (lettered, l): Marc. x, 30. — keyed to est
  • p. 0040 (notae, a): (a) Καὶ τόπῳ καὶ κόσμῳ ἰδίῳ· pauloque inferius, καὶ κόσμῳ ἰδίῳ καὶ τόπῳ· quo utroque loco Turr. vocem κόσμῳ reddit ornatu. Malui ego mundo reddere, eadem Latinæ vocis Græca amplitudine. Aliis passim locis, Maximus τῷ τόπῳ χρόνον adjungit, quæ sint ea, sine quibus nulla creata possint intelligi, præsertim composita, et quæ constant materia; quibus proinde intelligantur circumscripta. Quid igitur, si Maximus hic mundum pro rei ævo ac tempore accepit, sicque definita exsistentia, quo suum illa mundum constituit, eo circumscripta quasi intrinsece, uti et loco extrinsece? Alius ornatus, qui loco componatur, ad rei circumscriptionem, nullus videtur, nec Græci extra mundum muliebrem ac ejusmodi, facile voce κόσμος utantur: διακόσμησιν proclivius vocant, distinctionis [continues on p-0041: ornatum atque ordinem, qui a creatione accessit.] — keyed to ἰδίῳ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Both in its own place and its own world': and a little below, 'in its own world and place'. In both places Turrianus renders kosmo as 'by ornament'. I preferred 'world', with the same breadth of the Latin word as the Greek has. Elsewhere Maximus adds 'time' to 'place', as the things without which no created thing can be understood, especially composite things and those that consist of matter, by which they are therefore understood as circumscribed. What then if Maximus here has taken 'world' for the age and time of a thing, and so a defined existence by which it constitutes its own world, circumscribed by that as if inwardly, as also by place outwardly? Another 'ornament', one that is arranged by place, seems none for the circumscription of a thing, nor would the Greeks use the word kosmos for a muliebrous world and the like; they would more readily say diakosmesis, of distinction ... [continued next page]
  • p. 0041 (notae, b): (b) Τὸ ποτήριον. Videtur Turr. codex hic habuisse τοῦτο, dum reddit: Transeat a me calix iste; quod etiam habetur cap. illo 51 ex majori lucubratione Maximi ad Marinum supra. Nec forte sit aliud hic ipse tractatus, quam caput aliud ex eadem. Codex Raph. Dufrenii et Turr. subnectunt sequenti tomo dogmatico ad Marinum diaconum, ad quem ipsum scripta major illa lucubratio, etsi in titulo Marinus ille presbyter, hic diaconus prænotatur, nihilque vetat eumdem ex diacono exinde presbyterum initiatum esse, quo utroque gradu vir φιλομαθέστατος ac egregie orthodoxus. sic epistolarum dogmaticarum officio a Maximo cultus sit. Ac forsan major illa lucubratio de operationibus ac voluntatibus, cum titulus solum habeat, πρὸς τὸν αὐτὸν Μαρῖνον in Turr. codice, ex quo una habemus inscriptam Marino, ipsa adhuc diacono scripta sit, tametsi superior epistola, ad quam refertur illud, πρὸς τὸν αὐτόν, diserte Μαρῖνον πρεσβύτερον habeat. — keyed to ποτήριον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'The cup.' Turrianus's codex seems here to have had 'touto' (this), since he renders 'Let this cup pass from me'; which is also found in that chapter 51 of Maximus's longer treatise to Marinus above. Perhaps this very tract is nothing other than another chapter from it. The Raphelengius codex of Dufrenius and Turrianus append it to the following dogmatic volume addressed to Marinus the deacon, to whom that longer treatise itself was written, though in the title that Marinus is called a presbyter and this one a deacon, and nothing forbids that he, having been a deacon, was then made a presbyter, in either rank a most learned and outstandingly orthodox man; so he may have been honoured by Maximus with the office of dogmatic letters. And perhaps that longer treatise on operations and wills, since it has only the title 'to the same Marinus' in Turrianus's codex, from which we have one inscribed to Marinus, was itself written to the deacon, although the earlier letter, to which 'to the same' refers, expressly has Marinus the presbyter.
  • p. 0041 (notae, c): (c) Ἐκ τίνος ταύτην προσδέχῃ. Hanc nimirum συμφυΐαν velut dicat, coalitî animi necessitudinem ac concordiam: sic enim recte Bud. ex Basil. cui adjunxit et συμψυχίαν, ejusdem seu affinis significationis. Turr. quasi explicationis causa: De quo hanc voluntatem accipis? cum Græca vox significans Maximo voluntatem, sit, τὸ θέλειν et θέλημα, uti et Gregorio: quod utrumque neutrius generis est. Potius ergo συμφυΐαν refert ac σύννευσιν, quod ita feminine dictum Maximo, ταύτην. Mihi ipse reddebam: Ex quo tibi ea proficisci videtur? Hæc enim vis τοῦ, ἐκ τίνος, cum illud magis postulet ἐπὶ aut περὶ τίνος; — keyed to προσδέχῃ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'From what do you take this.' This 'symphuia', as if to say, the bond and concord of a united mind: so Budaeus rightly, from Basil, to which he added 'sympsychia', of the same or akin meaning. Turrianus, as if explaining: 'From what do you take this will?', although the Greek word that means 'will' in Maximus is to thelein and thelema, as also in Gregory: both of neuter gender. So it rather refers to symphuia and synneusis, which is why Maximus puts it in the feminine, 'tauten'. I myself rendered: 'From what does it seem to you to proceed?' For this is the force of 'ek tinos', whereas 'will' would rather require 'epi' or 'peri tinos'.
  • p. 0041 (notae, a): ornatum atque ordinem, qui a creatione accessit. Quod item Turr. partium coævarum σύνοδον ἠναγκασμένην, coactam reddit, non placet: nihil certe in naturalibus coactum (quod violentum usus communis vocat) vera admittit philosophia; cujusmodi est partium illa conjunctio; seu magis coitus, ut Græca vox sonat, ac qua potius naturalis quædam mutua propensio designari videatur. Necessaria ergo illa conjunctio, quippe non voluntate quæsita, sed ex naturæ ipsa inclinatione (supposita efficientis actione) secuta; non ipsa coacta aut violenta. — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ... the ornament and order that came with the creation. Turrianus's rendering of the 'union of coeval parts, forced' (synodon enagkasmenen) as 'coactam' does not please me: true philosophy admits nothing forced (what common usage calls violent) in natural things; such is that conjunction of the parts, or rather their coming-together (coitus), as the Greek word sounds, by which a kind of mutual natural inclination seems to be meant. That conjunction is therefore necessary, since it is not sought by will but follows from the very inclination of nature (the action of the efficient cause being supposed); it is not itself forced or violent.
  • p. 0043 (notae, d): (d) Τῆς πολλῆς εὐλαβείας τὸ κόσμιον. Vox εὐλαβείας hoc loco, modestia est, cauta formido; ipsa humilitas et paupertas spiritus, quam Maximus omnibus fere commendat, qui ad eum quæstiones referebant, quibus præfatur ad suas ad eos responsiones. Non satis bene Turr. ornamentum pietatis et reverentiæ tuæ: ut nec cum sequentibus ad illa verba, τῇ ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἱερᾷ συμπλοκῇ, Utriusque cum (illius nempe εὐλαβείας, fortisque zeli seu æmulationis) sacro nexu, ita supplet, disciplinæ scilicet et doctrinæ mysticæ; quorum nihil Maximi textus habet, nec ad ejus intentum attinet. — keyed to κόσμιον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'The decorum of great reverence.' Here the word eulabeia means modesty, a cautious fear; the very humility and poverty of spirit which Maximus commends to nearly all who referred questions to him, to which he prefaces his replies. Turrianus renders it not well: 'the ornament of your piety and reverence', as he also fails with the following words, 'by the sacred union of both', 'Of both together' (that is, of that reverence and of strong zeal or emulation) 'by sacred bond', he thus supplies, 'namely of discipline and mystic doctrine'; of which Maximus's text has nothing, nor does it belong to his intent.
  • p. 0043 (notae, e): (e) Κιβδήλοις λόγοις. Quod habet Dufrenii cod. χενοφωνίαις, Paulina vox est I Tim. vi, 20, nec aliam forte Maximus scripserit, cujus alterum quædam expositio sit. Latinus interpr. in Paulo καινοφωνίας legit, vocum novitates; Græca et Maximus, κενοφωνίας, vocum inanitates, quod utrunique hæreticorum est, nec liquet quid magis Paulus scripserit. — keyed to λόγοις — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Spurious words.' What Dufrenius's codex has, 'kenophonias', is a Pauline word, 1 Tim. vi, 20, and perhaps Maximus wrote no other; the one is, as it were, an exposition of the other. The Latin interpreter in Paul reads 'kainophonias', 'novelties of words'; the Greek and Maximus 'kenophonias', 'emptinesses of words', which is characteristic of both kinds of heretics, nor is it clear which Paul wrote rather.
  • p. 0046 (notae, f): (f) Τῇ πήξει λίαν κατεψυχωμένους ταύτης δὴ τῆς κρυμώδους καὶ ἀφεγγοῦς προβολῆς. Duf. cod. προσβολῆς, ut supra. Haud dubie spectant hæc ad Monothelitarum et Severianorum hæresim, cujus grassationem hac metaphora Maximus exprimit, et cui lucidos sanæ Marini doctrinæ radios concalefacientes opponit. Mihi Turrianus magnopere vagari videtur, dum ita reddit: Qui maximopere frigent in hac frigida et tenebricosa statione fixi. πῆξις, congelatio est. glacies, gelu. Difficilior hic vox προβολή, quam habuit Turr. habetque Venet. cod. vel προβολή, quam cod. Dufr. Nihil felicior Turrianus, dum stationem reddit, quam dum πήξει, voce fixi. Προβολῆς magis arridet, quam vocem optime exponit Budæus in Comment. ut sit quasi pugnæ incursus, aggressio, etc., a cujus significatu nec altera vox multum abest, quod attinet ad propositum: indeque nata earum permutatio, et quod ipsæ parum voces distant. Est ergo hæreticorum ἀφεγγὴς pugna et grassatio, velut andabatarum more, tota in tenebris, ipsa quoque κρυμώδης, quasi gelida, ab Aquilone, quo abest veritatis Sol, nimio gelu constringens, qui eorum afflatum recipiant. [text continues across both columns: forte Græca illa variaverint, aut luxata fuerint. Certe quod hic προβολὴν marg. ascriptum, extra locum est. Erat αἰσθητῶς in ἀπογράφῳ R. Ger. Blacch. sed errore cum feminino puto.] — keyed to προβολῆς — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Dufrenius's codex has 'prosbolés', as above. There is no doubt these words look to the heresy of the Monothelites and Severians, whose raging Maximus expresses by this metaphor, and to which he opposes the bright rays of Marinus's sound doctrine, warming them. Turrianus seems to me to wander widely when he renders it thus: 'Who most of all are chilled, fixed in this cold and gloomy station.' 'Pexis' means congelation, ice, frost. The word 'probole' is the harder here, which Turrianus had and the Venice codex has, or 'probole' as in the Dufrenius codex. Turrianus is no happier when he renders 'station' than when he renders 'pexei' by 'fixed'. 'Probolés' pleases me more, a word Budaeus explains very well in his Commentaries as a kind of assault in battle, an attack, and so on; the other word is not far from its sense as regards the matter in hand, which is why they were interchanged and why the words themselves differ little. So the heretics' lightless attack and raging, in the manner of blindfold gladiators, is wholly in the dark; it is also 'frosty', as if icy from the north, far from the Sun of truth, binding with excessive cold those who take in their breath.
  • p. 0046 (notae, g): (g) Τῶν ἀύλων φημί. Epithetum est ejus quod sequitur, πατρικῶν λόγων. Turr. quasi absolutum ponit; aliaque non satis cohærentia habet, quod forte Græca illa variaverint, aut luxata fuerint. Certe quod hic προβολὴν marg. ascriptum, extra locum est. Erat αἰσθητῶς in ἀπογράφῳ R. Ger. Blacch. sed errore cum feminino puto. — keyed to ἀύλων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Of the immaterial, I say.' This is an epithet of what follows, 'of paternal words'. Turrianus sets it down as if standing alone, and has other things that do not cohere well, perhaps because the Greek there varied or was dislocated. Certainly the 'probolen' written here in the margin is out of place. The copy of R. Ger. Blacch. had 'aisthetos', but I think by an error with a feminine.
  • p. 0046 (notae, h): (h) Πρὸς τὴν ὁλικὴν μετάποιησιν. Ea voce idem Maximus significat, quod voce θεώσεως, ac quod postmodum ἀποκατάστασιν vocat. Fit enim suprema illa animi intelligentis perfectio, quod totus in Deum velut transmutetur, ut jam non aliæ quam divinæ in eo dotes elucescant; uti aliis locis idem luculenter explicat. Non satis placet quod reddit Turr. ad totam possessionem obtinendam, etsi enim quandoque τὸ, μεταποιεῖσθαι, vindicare est, sibique asserere, ἀντιποιεῖσθαι. Hic tamen proclivior ejus vocis acceptio, ἀντὶ τοῦ, μετασκευάζεσθαι· quod beatitudo passionis voce passim Maximo soleat explicari. — keyed to μετάποιησιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Towards the whole transformation.' By this word Maximus signifies the same as by 'theosis', and what he later calls 'apokatastasis'. For the supreme perfection of the understanding soul is that it is wholly transmuted into God, so that now no other than divine gifts shine in it; as he explains clearly in other places. Turrianus's 'to obtain the whole possession' does not much please me; for although 'metapoieisthai' sometimes means to claim, to assert for oneself (like 'antipoieisthai'), here the easier sense of the word is 'to be transformed'; for blessedness is regularly explained by Maximus with the word 'passion'.
  • p. 0046 (notae, i): (i) Τῆς κατὰ σὲ θείας ἱερωσύνης. Videtur hac voce significari Marinum vere jam sacerdotem fuisse et presbyterum, non solum diaconum, quod titulo prænotatur, tametsi ἱερατικὸς ordo, quasi sacerdotalis, etiam diaconos complectatur, qui ipsi sacris muniis fungantur, etsi ut ministri, non ut ipsi offerentes, aliave peragentes quæ sunt omnino presbyteri propria vel episcopi. — keyed to ἱερωσύνης — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): By this word it seems signified that Marinus was already truly a priest and presbyter, and not only a deacon, as the title heads it; although the 'hieratic' order, as if priestly, also embraces deacons, who themselves discharge sacred duties, though as ministers and not as offerers or performers of what belongs strictly to a presbyter or bishop.
  • p. 0047 (lettered, q): I Cor. xiii, 12. — keyed to faciem
  • p. 0049 (notae, j): (j) Τὴν καινὴν ἔκθεσιν ἐνεργήσαντες. De fidei Formula Heraclio edita sermo est, sic peculiarius dicta: quam damnante Joanne IV, acriusque insectante Sophronio, Heraclius in Sergium retulit, ut auctorem; cujus ille suggestione ediderit seu publicarit, ut auctor est ipse Maximus Collat. cum Theodosio Cæsareæ episcopo: quam ipsam tergiversationem, qua alii in alios rem sint amoliti, istis suggillare videtur atque notare. Quod reddit Turr. novam doctrinæ explicationem, communis est. — keyed to ἐνεργήσαντες — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The talk is of the Formula of faith published by Heraclius, so called more particularly: which, John IV condemning it and Sophronius attacking it more sharply, Heraclius laid back on Sergius as its author; at whose suggestion he published it, as Maximus himself says in his Disputation with Theodosius, bishop of Caesarea: this very evasion, by which they shifted the matter on to one another, he seems to brand and mark in these men. Turrianus's rendering 'a new explanation of doctrine' is the common one.
  • p. 0049 (notae, k): (k) Ὁ θεῖος τῶν Νυσσαέων καθηγητὴς καὶ μέγας Γρηγόριος. Eadem verba relata in Sophronii Synodica, ex qua aucta, quæ hic minus plena videbantur lib. iv in Joan. Ἐπιτρέπει γε μήν, ὡς ἐν σαρκὶ γεγονὼς, ὑπομένειν τὰ ἴδια τῆ σαρκί· Sinebat carnem propria sustinere, ut qui eam induisset. Turr. sibi hic duos divinat, Gregorium Nyssenum et Gregorium Magnum, quem velit Gregorium Theologum: sed nihil apte. Itaque sic reddit, sicut Gregorius Nyssenus, et Magnus Gregorius aiunt. Faveret aliquid, si esset καὶ ὁ μέγας Γρηγόριος, quod tamen non est, nec ejus habuerit codex. Res sane plana, ut mirum cespitasse virum doctum, ubi nec tironi facile obrepat. Est et Gregorius Nyssenus Magnus, quanquam eo forte titulo crebrius decoretur Gregorius Theologus; cujus etiam unus hic singularis Græcis est titulus. In Magni titulo valde liberales sunt, ubi præsertim tanta suppetunt nomina, quanta in Gregorio Nysseno, Patrum eloquentissimo ac disertissimo, longeque eruditissimo, præclaroque antistite et fidei probatissimæ; ejusque denique per Orientem instauratore ac egregio vindice, Ecclesiarum in eam rem visitatione. — keyed to Γρηγόριος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The same words are reported in Sophronius's Synodica, from which what seemed less full here is supplied, Book IV on John: 'He allows the flesh to bear its own, as one who has put it on.' Turrianus guesses two persons here, Gregory of Nyssa and Gregory the Great, by whom he means Gregory the Theologian: but nothing aptly. So he renders 'as Gregory of Nyssa and Gregory the Great say'. It would favour something if the text had 'and the great Gregory', which it does not, and his codex would not have. The matter is plain, so that it is a wonder a learned man stumbled where not even a beginner would easily slip. Gregory of Nyssa also is called Great, although by that title Gregory the Theologian is perhaps more often honoured; and this is one title peculiar to him among the Greeks. In the title 'Great' they are generous where so many names attend as in Gregory of Nyssa, most eloquent and learned of the Fathers, far the most erudite, an illustrious bishop and of tried faith; and finally the restorer and distinguished champion of the faith through the East, by his visitation of the Churches on that business.
  • p. 0053 (numbered, 1): Matth. ix, 35. — keyed to languorem
  • p. 0057 (notae, l): Ἴσον ἐπιστολῆς. Venet. erat, τοῦ αὐτοῦ: sed malui ex Dufr. cod. illa addere, παρὰ τοῦ ἐν ἁγίοις Μαξίμου, quod magis cohæreant cum voce γενομένης, pleniorque oratio sit. Reliqua ex Turr. mutuatus sum, περὶ τῶν δύο ἐν Χριστῷ ἐνεργειῶν, quod non habuere alii codd. cum epistolæ tota præfatio, ipsa fusa, illi desideretur. — keyed to Ἐπιστολὴ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Copy of a letter. The Venice manuscript had 'of the same'; but I preferred to add these words 'by Maximus among the saints' from the Dufresne codex, because they cohere better with the word 'sent' and make the sentence fuller.
