The rest I borrowed from Turrianus, 'on the two energies in Christ', which the other manuscripts did not have, since the whole preface of the letter, in its full form, is missing from them.
  • p. 0058 (lettered, y): Luc. xv, 13. — keyed to remotam
  • p. 0058 (numbered, 33): None — keyed to χρόνῳ
  • p. 0059 (notae, m): Τοὺς ἐν αἰῶσι καὶ χρόνοις φυσικῶς περιγράφον. Incipit hic Turr. Tantis desideratis: nec placet, quod vocem περιγράφον, reddit, circumscribit et terminat: altera enim ac prior vox, una sufficit, verumque sensum Græcæ illius reddit, prout a Cicerone, Cæsare, aliisque usurpatur, relatis a Bud. in Comm. Est ergo ἄρρητον τὸ τῆς οἰκονομίας μυστήριον, arcanum nullis verbis explicabile; et ut reddit ipse Turr. nullos explicatus habens; quod ævum omne ac sæculum, et qui eis continentur, natura circumscribat; tanquam scilicet eis eminens, id est, excludat, e numero auferat. Similis lapsus notatus n. 1, in vocibus περιγράφειν et περιορίζειν. — keyed to περιγράφον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Those in ages and times, circumscribed by nature.' Here Turrianus begins. In so much that is missing [in his version]: nor does it please me that he rendered the word περιγράφον as 'circumscribes and limits'; for the first word, being the earlier, is sufficient on its own and gives the true sense of that Greek word, as used by Cicero, Caesar and others, as cited by Budaeus in his Commentaries. So the mystery of the economy is ἄρρητον, a secret that cannot be explained in words; and as Turrianus himself rendered it, 'having no explanations'; because nature circumscribes every age and era and what is contained in them, as though standing above them, that is, excluding them and removing them from the number. A similar slip is noted at no. 1, in the words περιγράφειν and περιορίζειν.
  • p. 0059 (notae, n): Ἀλλ' ἄνωθεν καὶ ἀπ' ἀρχῆς συνουσιωμένην αὐτῷ. Turr. Sed superne et a principio deferens copulavit eam secum secundum substantiam: quasi unum Valentini errorem hæc suffigant, delati e cœlo corporis, cœlestisque materiæ, non ex nobis assumpti; quod toto cœla a Maximi mente abest. Τὸ, ἄνωθεν, ut hic sumendum, ipsa Maximi correctio notat, ἢ οὐδὲ λαβών, quæ nec Turriano defuit, dum reddit, aut neque sumpsit. Sumpsit certe, etsi superne, sive e cœlo devexit. Ἄνωθεν ergo, ipsum est quod ἀπ' ἀρχῆς, a principio, ex ipsa origine ac processione; quomodo usurpavit Greg. in hoc carmine, Ὧν τὸ μὲν ἦεν ἄνωθεν· Quorum hoc quidem erat a principio, ex origine; uti etiam Demosth. τὸ συνουσιωμένην, secundum substantiam unitam; longe est a viro theologo. συνουσιασμός, Eutychianorum est et Severianorum; unitio secundum substantiam, et substantialis, sive etiam naturalis, Christi Ecclesiæ, auctore præsertim Cyrillo. Illud, substantiarum confusio, altera ab altera absorpta, vel etiam ex ambabus una tertia effecta, uti passim Maximus exprimit, et qui eam confundentium hæresim impugnant: istud, vera essentiarum seu naturarum, καθ' ὑπόστασιν, sustinendi ratione (quæ et post unitionem distinctionem retineant, ac quod unaquæque nata est) copulatio. Nec in re sic trita morandum, nisi quantum adegit tanta illa superioris interpretis vere ἀθεόλογος; oscitantia. — keyed to αὐτῷ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'But from above and from the beginning united in substance with him.' Turrianus has: 'But bringing it from above and from the beginning he joined it to himself according to substance', as though these words supported the one error of Valentinus, of a body brought down from heaven and of heavenly matter, not taken from us; which is utterly far from Maximus's mind. That ἄνωθεν must be taken as here [rendered] is shown by Maximus's own correction, 'or not having taken', which Turrianus did not lack either when he renders 'or did not take'. He certainly took it, even if he brought it down from above, that is from heaven. So ἄνωθεν is the same as ἀπ' ἀρχῆς, 'from the beginning', from the very origin and procession, as Gregory uses it in his poem, 'of which one was from above', that is 'from the beginning, from the origin'; so too Demosthenes. The word συνουσιωμένην, 'united in substance', is far from the theologian. συνουσιασμός belongs to the Eutychians and Severians: a union according to substance, and substantial or even natural, of Christ and the Church, especially according to Cyril. The one [sense] is a confusion of substances, one swallowed by the other, or even a third made from both, as Maximus everywhere expresses it and as those who oppose the heresy of the confounders do; the other is a union of true essences or natures, according to hypostasis, on the principle of sustaining them (which retain their distinction even after the union, and what each was born to be). One need not linger over so well-worn a matter, except so far as that great carelessness of the earlier translator, truly lacking sound theology, compelled it.
  • p. 0060 (notae, o): Θέσις. Bene Turr. Positio; cui ἀφαίρεσις opponitur: uti et διὰ θέσεως φιλοσοφεῖν περὶ Θεοῦ, τῷ, δι' ἀφαιρέσεως: affirmando scilicet et negando: qua de re idem Dionys. De mystica theolog. Lansel. ἀφαίρεσις non bene, eminentia, apud Dionys. uti nec Cord. situs. Sed alia in Dionysio et scholiis eorum mihi castiganda σφάλματα. — keyed to ἐστιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Θέσις: Turrianus rightly has 'Positio' (positing), to which ἀφαίρεσις (removal) is opposed, as also 'to do philosophy about God by positing' as against 'by removal', that is by affirming and by denying; on which matter see the same Dionysius, On Mystical Theology, Lansel[ottus]. Ἀφαίρεσις is not rendered well as 'eminentia' in Dionysius, as neither is Cordier's 'situs'. But there are other errors in Dionysius and in their scholia that I must correct.
  • p. 0061 (notae, p): Καλοφροσύνης. Vox paulo rarior. Hesych. τὸ καλόφρον affert, explicanda voce εὔφρων, uti etiam vocem αἰδέσιμος: huncque vel maxime ejus sensum putem hic spectasse Maximum, prout vox αἰδέσιμος active etiam sumitur; quam ejus significationem confirmavi ex Orig. in ejus Catena in Genesim, ubi illi sermo est de Rebecca. Catena hæc exiguis laboribus undecunque licuit ex omnibus Origenianis in Scripturam mihi collecta, notisque illustrata, in Huetii manibus prælum exspectat: nec quia symbolæ sunt, sic elevandæ, ut jam vir doctus insinuavit. Videmus quanta fide in aliis excerptæ ejusmodi symbolæ, ipsoque adeo Origene, in quibus libri integri exstant, ut inde libros aliquot Cyrilli in Joannem supplere licuerit. Breviant plerumque, quandoque etiam, servata sententia, tantisper verba mutant, qui hoc scribendi genus, inter Græcos maxime, secuti sunt: quo fit ut in secundis habenda sint ejus generis scripta, non ut pene nulla; mihi haud raro interpolatis Rufino ejusque affusis furfuribus ad fidem auctorumque censum potiora. Sed hæc obiter. Ad rem quod attinet, prætexebant pietatem Eutychiani et Monothelitæ, quasi vererentur illi quidem, nec admissa distinctione solveretur naturarum unitio; hi vero, ne contrarias in Christo voluntates ac peccatum inducerent, si et humana præditum voluntate dicerent. Quæ maxime Sergii cavillatio fuit, ejusque discipuli Macarii Antiocheni. Reddi itaque possit, animi probitas, verecundia ac pudor, cautio ac timor, et si quid ejusmodi est: Turr. bellam prudentiam reddit: possit et sapientiam. Dictum καλοφροσύνης, quasi ironice et per ἀντίφρασιν, pro κακοφροσύνης, quod est malitia, mentis, perversitas. — keyed to καλοφροσύνης — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Καλοφροσύνης': a somewhat rare word. Hesychius offers τὸ καλόφρον, explained by the word εὔφρων, as also the word αἰδέσιμος; and I think Maximus had chiefly this sense in view here, as αἰδέσιμος is also taken actively; I confirmed that meaning from Origen in his Catena on Genesis, where he speaks of Rebecca. This Catena, gathered by me with modest labour from all the Origenian material on Scripture wherever possible, and annotated, awaits the press in Huet's hands; nor, because they are mere excerpts, should they be belittled, as a learned man has already hinted. We see with what fidelity such excerpts were made elsewhere, even from Origen himself, where whole books survive, so that several books of Cyril on John could be supplied from them. Those who follow this kind of writing, chiefly among the Greeks, often abridge and sometimes, keeping the sense, change the wording for a while; so such writings are to be counted second-rank, not as nearly worthless; to me they are often preferable to texts interpolated by Rufinus and sprinkled with his chaff, as regards faithfulness and the weight of authors. But that is by the way. As to the point at hand: the Eutychians and Monothelites put forward piety as a pretext, as if they feared that, once a distinction was admitted, the union of the natures would be dissolved; and the latter, lest they introduce contrary wills in Christ and sin, if they said he was endowed with a human will as well. This was above all the quibble of Sergius and of his disciple Macarius of Antioch. It can therefore be rendered 'uprightness of mind, modesty and shame, caution and fear', and whatever is of that kind; Turrianus renders it 'bellam prudentiam' ['fine prudence']; 'wisdom' also could be used. [The Greek] was said of καλοφροσύνη as if ironically and by antiphrasis, for κακοφροσύνη, that is malice, malignity of mind, perversity.
  • p. 0065 (notae, q): Μεγίστης ἔχεται διανοίας. Dionysii intelligentiæ cohæreret; eumdem sensum altissimum habet. Ac certe carnis Christi communitatem hanc operationis ac virtutis cum Verbo ac unitione ad ipsum, nedum in propria specie, sed et in mystica benedictione, ut vocat, seu Eucharistia, nemo Patrum plenius Cyrillo altiusque exsecutus est, prorsus consentanee ac proxime Dionysio, sive ipsum Cyrillus legit, sive eamdem ac ille ecclesiasticam doctrinam ac traditionem exposuit: cujus etiam multa mihi relata ac stylo ornata in Biblioth. PP. concionat. ad diem festum Corporis Christi. Non satis expedite Turr., maximam sententiam Patris continet. — keyed to διανοίας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'Holds to a very great thought.' It coheres with the understanding of Dionysius; it has the same most exalted sense. And certainly, as to this communion of Christ's flesh in operation and power with the Word and by union with him, not only in its proper kind but also in 'mystical blessing', as he calls it, that is the Eucharist, no Father has treated it more fully and more deeply than Cyril, in complete agreement and closest accord with Dionysius, whether Cyril read Dionysius himself or set out the same ecclesiastical doctrine and tradition as he; many of Cyril's words on this I have reported, adorned with style, in the Bibliotheca Patrum, in the sermon for the feast of Corpus Christi. Turrianus renders it not very clearly; it contains the Father's greatest thought.
  • p. 0068 (notae, r): Ὧ τὰ ἐκεῖθεν συνεφιλοσόφησε. Exciderunt hæc verba doctissimo interpreti Billio, solita elegantia reddituro. Turr. qua de iis, quæ illic sunt, philosophatus est. Durum mihi, carne philosophari de cœlestibus et æternis; de rebus scilicet ævi futuri. Hæc enim sunt τὰ ἐκεῖ, ut et τὰ ἐνταῦθα, quæ sunt ævi præsentis. Spiritu potius et animo de illo philosophamur, etsi cum carne, illaque ei socia ac subministra, seu instrumento ejus philosophiæ; qua ipsa virtutibus componitur, ac ratione regitur. — keyed to συνεφιλοσόφησεν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'With which he philosophised on the things from there.' These words escaped the very learned translator Billy, who would otherwise have rendered them with his usual elegance. Turrianus has 'by which he philosophised about the things that are there'. It is hard for me [to accept] philosophising with the flesh about heavenly and eternal things, that is, about the things of the age to come. For these are τὰ ἐκεῖ ('the things there'), as τὰ ἐνταῦθα are 'the things here', which belong to the present age. We philosophise about it rather with spirit and mind, even if with the flesh, which is its companion and servant, or the instrument of that philosophy; and the flesh itself is ordered by the virtues and governed by reason.
  • p. 0068 (notae, s): Καὶ ὁ ὑπὸ τοῦ συγγενοῦς μαρτυρούμενος Πνεύματος. Aliam Maximus, aut pleniorem habuit Greg. litteram cum hæc modo solum habeat, ἐκεῖθεν γὰρ ὁ μαρτυρούμενος. Inde enim cui testimonium perhibebatur. — keyed to Πνεύματος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'And he who is attested by the kindred Spirit.' Maximus had a different, or fuller, text of Gregory, since [our text] has only this: 'for from there he who is attested' [i.e. a shorter reading]. 'For from there, he to whom testimony was borne.'
  • p. 0069 (lettered, d): Matth. viii, 3; et ix, 25. — keyed to carnis
  • p. 0070 (notae, t): Ἀλλ' ἀμέτοχος πάντῃ. Legit Turr. ἀλλὰ μέτοχος, quod et habuit Geras. Blacchi cod. Venet. aperto nimis errore. Reddit itaque, sed potius participans omnino secundum substantiam ab omni natura differt. Quæ ipsa aperte pugnant. Non enim differt natura a natura, seu ab ea distinguitur, quod participet, sed quod non participet, sitque quo participent personæ et ὑποστάσεις, ipsa non participans. Quo fit ut, si una Christus natura est, uti operatio, nihil sit eorum ex quibus constitutus est, id est, nec Deus, nec homo, nec cum Patre nec nobiscum communicet. Quod argumentum, uti alibi passim, sic hic quoque Maximus vexat: nec debuit vir theologus re tam proclivi ab ejus deviare sententia, ac quod ab omni abhorret sacra profanaque philosophia, ex nota elisionis male omissa, festinante Antiquarii calamo, deroquere. — keyed to πάντῃ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'But wholly without participation.' Turrianus read ἀλλὰ μέτοχος, 'but participating', which Geras also had, and the Venice codex of Blacchus, by an obvious error. So he renders it, 'but rather participating, wholly according to substance, it differs from every nature'. These things plainly contradict one another. For one nature does not differ from another, or is not distinguished from it, because it participates, but because it does not participate, and it is that in which persons and hypostases participate, itself not participating. Hence, if Christ is one nature, like his operation, he is none of the things from which he is composed, that is, he would be neither God nor man, and would not communicate with the Father or with us. This argument Maximus presses, as elsewhere throughout; the theologian should not have departed from his own meaning in so easy a matter, and from what every sound sacred or profane philosophy abhors, by an omitted elision-mark, [owing to] the hasty pen of the copyist, [to] ... [the last word, 'deroquere', is unclear in the scan].
  • p. 0072 (notae, u): Ἵν' ἐκ τούτου οὐ μεσίτας Θεοῦ μᾶλλον. Addidi negantem particulam, quod necessario sensus exposcit. Quid enim sensus, quod absque illa habent Græca, et reddit Turr. ut hinc vos magis sequestros Dei, quam imitatores præstetis. Imo certe contrarium. Significat enim episcopos pro munere, et qua divinis dotibus eo sublimes fulgent, non tam ad Deum intercedere (quanquam et hoc eorum muneris est) quam ipsos quoque (quod pene divinius videatur) Dei imitatione divina præbere ac perficere; quippe gradu ipso τελειωτικούς, sub summo hierarcha Christo, ea functione ejusque vicarios. Hoc enim est quod sequitur, Non solum ejus in omnem illustrationem ac misericordiam habitu consecuti ἀλλὰ καὶ ἄξια παρέχοντες: verum etiam pro dignitatis munere præbentes. Ubi Turr. ἄξια legit, sed digna etiam ei præbentes: male vero τὸ, ei, quasi eos digna Deo præbere dicat Maximus, quod prorsus abhorret, vel si ἄξια demus eum scripsisse. Præbent certe illi populis ἄξια, in hoc illis comparata dignitate; vel etiam ἄξια, ut quisque dignus est accipere. Sed illud primum longe aptius. Continere habitu, vel ea habitu nactos esse, privatorum potius est. Scripserit et ipse Turr. eis, etsi ita illi prelum erravit. — keyed to μᾶλλον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'So that from this you may be mediators of God rather [than ...]'. I have added the negating particle, which the sense necessarily demands. For what sense is there, since the Greek has it without that, and Turrianus renders it 'that from this you may show yourselves rather as God's go-betweens than as imitators'? On the contrary, surely. For [Maximus] means that bishops, by their office and by the divine gifts by which they shine on high, do not so much intercede with God (though that too is part of their office) as also (which may seem almost more divine) bring about and perfect divine things in others by imitation of God, being in that very rank 'perfecters' under Christ the supreme hierarch, and his vicars in that function. For this is what follows: 'having attained not only the state of illumination and mercy that belongs to him, but also supplying worthy things'; indeed supplying them according to the dignity of their office. Where Turrianus reads ἄξια, but [translates] 'supplying worthy things to him': the 'to him' is wrong, as if Maximus said they supply God with worthy things, which he utterly rejects, even if we grant that he wrote ἄξια. They certainly supply the peoples with ἄξια, a dignity suited to them in this; or else ἄξια, as each is worthy to receive. But the first sense is far more apt. 'Containing by a habit', or 'having obtained them by a habit', is more characteristic of private persons. Turrianus himself may have written 'to them', even if the press erred so.
  • p. 0072 (notae, v): Τὴν δὲ τῶν Σικελῶν. Videtur pronomen — keyed to Σικελῶν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [Heading:] 'The [letter] of the Sicilians.' The pronoun seems [to indicate ...] (continues on the next page).
  • p. 0073 (lettered, k): Orat. p. 2. — keyed to Theologus
  • p. 0073 (notae, v): indicare scriptam epistolam in ipsa Siciliæ insula, cum jam illud diverticulum sibi Monothelitæ quæsissent, ut et unam et duas in Christo operationes ponerent: quam conati sunt Maximo affingere, ac postea in eam illum trahere pro primi ejus exilii tempore, quando et ipsi Eugenii apocrisiarii in eam novam fidem sunt inducti. Videtur itaque Maximus voluisse, ut hanc Siculi ejus illibatæ fidei certum pignus haberent, ac cunctis illius obtrectatoribus opponerent, in qua non perinde cavillari liberum esset, ac in eis quæ ore tenus ad eos locutus esset. Sicque animo insinuat in prooemio. Titulus quem repræsentat Turr. Epistola sancti Maximi ad sanctos Patres præpositos et monachos, et populos catholicos insulæ Siciliæ habitatores, contra eos qui unam in Christo operationem ponunt, ab Antiquario palam adjectus est; quem ex aliis repræsento, ipsa sit Maximo apposita ἐπιγραφή: magis proinde probanda; uti etiam quam repræsento ex Dufrenii Codice ad Quæst. in Scripturam. — keyed to Σικελῶν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [The pronoun] seems to indicate a letter written in Sicily itself, when the Monothelites had already sought that refuge for themselves, so as to posit both one and two operations in Christ: which they tried to attribute to Maximus, and afterwards to drag him into it at the time of his first exile, when even Eugenius's apocrisiaries were led into that new faith. So Maximus seems to have wished that the Sicilians should have this as a sure pledge of his unblemished faith, and set it against all his detractors, since in it there was no such freedom for quibbling as in what he had said to them by word of mouth. And so he hints in the preface. The title Turrianus gives, 'Letter of Saint Maximus to the holy Fathers, superiors and monks, and the Catholic peoples inhabiting the island of Sicily, against those who posit one operation in Christ', was openly added by a copyist; the one I give from others is the very inscription attached by Maximus, and so is the more to be approved; as is also the one I give from the Dufresne codex of the Questions on Scripture.
  • p. 0073 (notae, x): Τῶν ἀντιῤῥόπων καὶ ἐναντίων δογμάτων ὑπόληψις. Sic Maximus novam Monothelitarum opinionem argute vocat, seu illorum damnatæ jam hæresis commentum et evasionem. Una itaque voluntate et operatione, una cum Ecthesi, Joanni IV ac cunctis Occident. necnon Sophronio Hierosolymorum episcopo, reprobata, hoc novatoribus succurrit, ut tanquam ἀντίῤῥοπον (æquivalens, par, æquale) unam, duasque eas dicerent: quod tamen Maximus contrarium esse contendit; ac ejusmodi, ut alterum alterum evertat. Non recte itaque Turr. Nunquam me sensisse aut suspicatum esse de contrariis et repugnantibus dogmatibus, ut et infra, περὶ τῶν ἀντιῤῥόπων οὑτωσὶ καὶ ἐναντίων δογμάτων: de contrariis dogmatibus: ac si ambæ voces essent ejusdem prorsus significationis; quod longe est a vera Gramm. ἀντίῤῥοπον enim, ἰσόῤῥοπον est Suid. ἴσον, ἰσόσταθμον, ἰσόζυγον Hesych. quod perinde lancem trahit, ejusdemque ponderis est; a quo certe procul contrarii ratio est. Nec ii vero longe abesse a Monothelitarum illo genio videantur, qui nuper damnatarum Romæ propositionum acrius defensores, ac cum suo antistite, parum Thomistarum scholæ faventes, atque errantium albo, primo ipso ordine ascribentes, nunc sibi in uno Thoma, velut ei semper militaverint, præsidium quærunt: ac utinam vere quærant, ac quidquid de gratia senserint, ad illius normam, hinc plane erroris ac notæ perpetuo securi, componant. Ineptiunt enim, ni vere ἀσεβοῦντας quis malit dicere, nec magis Angelico præceptori quam ipsi Sedi apostolicæ, inque ea nuper præsidenti Alexandro VII injurii sunt, qui vel scioli vel procaces, vel invidi ac malevoli, obtinente ejus ipsius testimonio, damnatam sancti Thomæ ac Thomistarum in Jansenianam doctrinam deblaterant, aut etiam chartis illinunt atque evulgant. — keyed to ὑπόληψις — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'The supposition of counterbalancing and contrary doctrines.' So Maximus cleverly names the new opinion of the Monothelites, that is, the contrivance and evasion of their already condemned heresy. With one will and one operation rejected, together with the Ecthesis, by John IV and all the West, and by Sophronius, bishop of Jerusalem, this expedient helped the innovators: that they might say 'one' and 'two' of them as though ἀντίῤῥοπον ('equivalent, equal, level'); which Maximus nevertheless contends is contrary, and of such a kind that the one overturns the other. So Turrianus is wrong in 'that I never thought or suspected of contrary and conflicting doctrines', as also below, 'on the ἀντίῤῥοπα, thus and contrary doctrines': as if the two words had the same meaning, which is far from true grammar. For ἀντίῤῥοπον is the same as ἰσόῤῥοπον, Suidas; 'equal, of equal weight, equally yoked', Hesychius: it tips the scale just alike and is of the same weight; the idea of contrary is far from it. Nor do those seem to stand far from that Monothelite temper who, as recent vehement defenders of the propositions condemned at Rome, and little favouring the school of the Thomists, enrol themselves in the list of the erring in the very first rank, and now seek protection in Thomas alone, as though they had always served under him: would that they did truly seek it, and bring whatever they think about grace into line with his norm, thus safe for ever from error and censure. For they talk nonsense, unless one would rather call them truly impious, and they wrong the Angelic Doctor no more than the Apostolic See itself, and in it Alexander VII, then presiding, they who, whether smatterers or insolent or envious and spiteful, with his own testimony in their favour, babble about the condemned doctrine of St Thomas and the Thomists against the Jansenist doctrine, or even smear it on pages and publish it.
  • p. 0074 (notae, y): Φύσιν τε λέγω καὶ ὑπόστασιν. Istud, quod superioris explicatio est, τοῦ καθ' ἑκατέραν, secundum utramque naturam scilicet et personam; Turr. velut absolutum facit relatum ad ὑπογράφοντες;, quo mihi ejus propositio non ferenda videtur, dum sic scribit, quasi Maximi sententiam reddens. Describentes, inquam, Filium esse naturam et hypostasim, quod est ab Ecclesia alienum. Ego non sic didici, nec me docuit Maximus; imo hunc Ecclesiæ catholicæ sensum accepi: nulla enim naturæ et hypostasis in Filio opposi io, ut non Catholice utrumque dici debeat, etsi idem duntaxat ratione naturæ cum Patre communicet, non ratione personæ; unumque hoc alienum dicat Maximus ab Ecclesia catholica, id est, cum eo communicare ratione utriusque, quod est personalem amborum oppositionem, ac proinde distinctionem, Sabelliano errore, tollere. Sic pronum in theologia, ex verbis incaute prolatis, in hæresim incidere, aut certe hæreticam pro Catholica propositionem dare. — keyed to ὑπόστασιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'I say nature and hypostasis.' This, which is an explanation of the preceding, [is of] τοῦ καθ' ἑκατέραν, that is 'according to each, nature and person'; Turrianus makes it as if absolute, relating it to ὑπογράφοντες, so that his proposition seems to me intolerable when he writes thus, as though rendering Maximus's meaning: 'Describing, I say, the Son to be nature and hypostasis', which is foreign to the Church. I did not learn it so, nor did Maximus teach me; on the contrary I took this to be the sense of the Catholic Church: for there is no opposition of nature and hypostasis in the Son, such that each could not be said in a Catholic way, although he communicates with the Father only in respect of nature, not of person; and the one thing Maximus calls foreign to the Catholic Church is to communicate with him in respect of both, which is to remove, by the Sabellian error, the personal opposition of the two and hence the distinction. So it is easy in theology to fall into heresy from incautiously uttered words, or at least to give a heretical proposition for a Catholic one.
  • p. 0074 (notae, z): Τῇ δὲ ἀχράντῳ Μητρί. Turr. quod est adversative, quasi conjunctive ponit, et cum Matre Virgine; quod forte error typorum est, pro, sed; ut et quod paulo post sequitur, impugnando tertiam illam totius seu personæ operationem, ὅπερ ἀδύνατον: quod est possibile, pro impossibile. Potuit Jac. Gretseri edendo sodalis posthumum hoc opus, sedulior opera elucere. — keyed to μητρὶ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): 'But to the immaculate Mother.' Turrianus, which is adversative, puts as though conjunctive, 'and with the Virgin Mother'; which is perhaps a printers' error, for 'but'; as also what follows a little later, in refuting that third operation of the whole or of the person, ὅπερ ἀδύνατον ['which is impossible'], [he renders] 'which is possible', for 'impossible'. The posthumous work of the associate of Jacob Gretser who edited it could have shone forth with more diligent care.
  • p. 0076 (numbered, 1): Joan. xxi, 25. — keyed to sunt
  • p. 0076 (notae, a): (a) Τὴν εἰς ἄλλους γιγνομένην θεοσημείαν. Sic quoque Turrian. Miraculum a Salvatore Deo factum aliis. Mihi tamen non suadeo, quin legam, εἰς ἀνθρώπους. Nam quæ exempla ponit Maximus, τὴν ἀνάστασιν, τὴν ἀνάληψιν: resurrectionem in cœlum receptionem, secundum ejus verum et planum sensum, ipsa vere divina signa et miracula, non εἰς ἄλλους exhibita dici possint in alios; bene tamen εἰς ἀνθρώπους, in homines: nam et ipse vere homo Salvator Deus, sic virtute divinitatis sanci-tatus, ut Paulus testis est, et in cœlum levatus. Decoquit Turrianus. Qui de aliorum suscitatione et curatione exponeret (nam et ἀνάληψις probatis auctoribus et grammaticis θεραπεία est, et ἀνάρρωσις) nihil quidem is peccaret retinendo εἰς ἄλλους, quod et mihi primum placuerat: sed id durius-culum, et remotius a Maximi ecclesiastica styli simplicitate, qua libenter sacris ipsis ac receptis vocibus utatur: alias proclive admodum mutari τὸ ἀνοῦς breviatum, quod est, ἀνθρώπους, in ἄλλους — keyed to θεουργίαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): On ‘the sign [of God] done toward others’ (Τὴν εἰς ἄλλους γιγνομένην θεοσημείαν). So too Turrianus: ‘a miracle done by the Saviour God in [or: for] others.’ I cannot, however, persuade myself not to read εἰς ἀνθρώπους. For the examples Maximus gives, ‘the resurrection, the ascension’ (that is, the resurrection and the reception into heaven), according to their true and plain sense, are themselves truly divine signs and miracles, and could not be said to be displayed ‘toward others’ (εἰς ἄλλους), ‘in others’; but they could well be said εἰς ἀνθρώπους, ‘in men’: for the Saviour God himself is truly man, thus [sancitatus] by the power of divinity, as Paul is witness, and lifted up into heaven. Turrianus waters it down. One who spoke of the raising and healing of others (for ἀνάληψις too, in approved authors and grammarians, is healing, and so is ἀνάρρωσις) would indeed commit no fault by keeping εἰς ἄλλους, which had pleased me too at first; but that is rather harsh, and remoter from the simplicity of Maximus’s ecclesiastical style, in which he gladly uses the sacred and received words themselves; otherwise it is very easy for the abbreviated τὸ ἀνοῦς, that is ἀνθρώπους, to be changed into ἄλλους [the note ends here in print, with no final stop].
  • p. 0080 (notae, b): (b) Ἐξουσιαστικὸς ὑπάρχων, καὶ τῆς τῶν ἁπάντων ἐξουσίας δημιουργός. Ex Dei nativa libertate, suique juris potestate, et quod ejusdem in naturis intelligentibus potestatis auctor ipse sit, recte suadet Maximus, non factum fuisse ἄνουν ἄνθρωπον, hominem mente destitutum; quæ ejus liberæ suique juris potestatis, principium est. Non bene itaque Turr. habens potestatem secundum naturam, et auctor substantiæ omnium; nempe pro ἐξουσίας, legens οὐσίας: quod nec sic ita ejus habuit cod., sic fuit dec[…]dum, ἐξουσιαστής, inter nomina Christi habetur Isa. ix, 6, quod tamen cum aliis a LXX positis, sanctus Hieronymus non exponit. Ejus loco reliqui interpret. δυνατὸς habent. Sonat maxime in potestatem illam, qua quis vel sibi ipse, vel aliis dominatur ac præcipit. Sic ἐξουσιαστικὰ verba appellantur, ἄρχω, κυριεύω, βασιλεύω, et quæ sunt ejus generis. Hinc τὸ αὐτεξούσιον, quod nostri vocant Liberum arbitrium, facultatem scilicet rationis sui dominæ, ac se moventis ex præconcepto fine. — keyed to δημιουργὸς — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ‘Having authority (ἐξουσιαστικός), and maker of the authority of all things.’ From God’s native freedom and his power over his own right, and from the fact that he himself is the author of that same power in intelligent natures, Maximus rightly argues that there was not made an ἄνους ἄνθρωπος, a man bereft of mind; for this is the principle of his free power over his own right. Turrianus therefore does not do well [to render] ‘having power according to nature, and author of the substance of all things’; that is, reading οὐσίας in place of ἐξουσίας; which his codex did not have in that form, so it had to be [dec…dum — word broken in print; probably ‘toned down’]: ἐξουσιαστής is counted among the names of Christ at Isa. 9:6, which however, with the other words set down by the LXX, St Jerome does not explain. In its place the other interpreters have δυνατός. It sounds especially of that power by which a person rules and commands either himself or others. So the verbs ἄρχω, κυριεύω, βασιλεύω and those of that kind are called ἐξουσιαστικά. Hence τὸ αὐτεξούσιον, which our writers call free will, [that is] the faculty of reason as its own mistress, moving itself from a preconceived end.
  • p. 0081 (notae, c): (c) Τῆς πρὸς Μαρῖνον. Non videtur supposita hæc epistola ad Marinum, aliqua esse ex illis quæ ad eum exstant, omnes dogmaticæ: ipsaque prima inter editos Turriano tractatus, serio γνωμικὴν in Christo impugnat voluntatem, et electionem nobis similem, solum admittens quatenus est motus liber, non tamen secundum contraria, aut conjunctam incerti rationem habens; sicque se admisisse Quæst. ad Thalass. excusat ac profitetur. Ubi quoque voluntatem κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν, per arrogantiam et appropriationem ex Patrum sensu, ipsam nostram voluntatem, quam velut caput membrorum, suam quodammodo ducit, recte exponit; cui nihil commune cum tertia illa Monothelitarum voluntate ut totius, etsi eam etiam illi dicerent κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν. Quæ seorsum exstat a reliquis Maximi epistolis ejus ad Marinum epistola, qua satisfacit pro adjecta particula Filioque Litteris apostolicis, num ejusmodi aliquid habuerit incertum est, cum nec illi qui Maximo abrogant, ejus quidquam insimulaverint. vel exemplum illius magis integrum, quam modo habeamus, nacti sint. Potuit sic ab iis Græcis ablegari, qui Phòtiani dogmatis studiosiores, Maximi piam illam expositionem ejusque εἰρηνικὸν non probaverunt. — keyed to Μαρῖνον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): On ‘the one to Marinus’ (Τῆς πρὸς Μαρῖνον). This letter to Marinus does not seem to be spurious, [but] to be some one of those which survive addressed to him, all of them dogmatic; and the first of them, among those printed by Turrianus, seriously opposes a gnomic will (γνωμικήν) in Christ, and an election like ours, admitting it only so far as it is a free motion, yet not one according to contraries, nor having the conjoined notion of uncertainty; and so he excuses and professes that he admitted it in the Questions to Thalassius. Where also he rightly explains the will ‘by appropriation’ (κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν), by arrogation and appropriation in the sense of the Fathers, [as] our own will, which he in a manner counts his own, as the head of the members; with which that third will of the Monothelites, [a will] of the whole, has nothing in common, even if they too called it κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν. As to the letter to Marinus that stands apart from Maximus’s other letters, in which he gives satisfaction about the particle Filioque added to the apostolic letters, whether it contained anything of that kind is uncertain, since not even those who deny it to Maximus have charged him with anything. Or [they] may have obtained a copy of it more complete than the one we now have. It could thus have been got rid of by those Greeks who, more zealous for the Photian teaching, did not approve Maximus’s pious exposition and his peaceable statement (εἰρηνικόν).
  • p. 0083 (numbered, 61): Emissionem lego, ne cum Græcis schismaticis effectum solum temporalem notet, quod longe est a Max. τοῦ δι᾽ Υἱοῦ προϊέναι, omnique Latinorum sensu; ipso ea voce rite expresso ab Anast. in Maximi excerpto. — keyed to missionem — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): I read ‘emissionem’, lest with the schismatic Greeks it denote only a temporal effect, which is far from Maximus’s ‘to proceed through the Son’ and from the whole Latin sense; this very word is correctly rendered by Anastasius in his excerpt from Maximus.
  • p. 0083 (numbered, 62): Et hic ignaros; potuitque vir tantus cautius edere. — keyed to gnaros — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Here too ‘ignaros’; and so great a man might have expressed it more cautiously.
  • p. 0083 (numbered, 70): Anast. Collect. — keyed to igitur — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Anastasius, Collectanea.
  • p. 0084 (numbered, 71): Addit. Anast. et actuali. — keyed to originis — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Anastasius adds ‘and actual’ (i.e. ‘of original and actual sin’).
  • p. 0084 (numbered, 72): Solus Pater causa et auctor, προκαταρκτικῶς αἴτιον, ut Basil. prima origine fons ipse totius Trinitatis. — keyed to auctorem — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Only the Father is cause and author, προκαταρκτικῶς αἴτιον (‘the originating cause’), as Basil says: the first origin, the very fount of the whole Trinity.
  • p. 0086 (notae, marker illegible): Maximi locum ex epistola ad Marinum, de processione Spiritus ex Patre et Filio in Martini primi synodicis, quo offensi sint Constantinopolitani, ipseque Maximus exposuerit, ut non duas causas, sed unam, Patrem, Latini sentiant; nonnulli, etiam Catholici, an sit Maximi addubitant; vereorque ne Leo Allatius liberalior sit, in Vindiciis synodi Ephesinæ Cyrilli ea de re, cap. 72, cum ait plerosque addubitare. Anastasius in Collectaneis ut certæ fidei retulit, ac momentum habere in rem fidei et Ecclesiæ existimavit. In concilio Lugdunensi Gregorio X pontifice, nescio qui dubitarint: non Beccus, non Georgius Metochita, etc. In Florentino ea Maximi distinctione et sententia, Ecclesiarum pax conciliata: non Blemidas aliique Catholici ex schismaticis, Palamas, Marcus Ephesius, etc. Libri antiquissimi servarunt, in quibus codex ille Romanus antiquior vii synodo, ex quo exscriptus Reg. Michaele Duca Angelo Comneno imper. Qui dubitent, parum versatos in Maximi lectione existimo, nec ullam arbitror exstare ejus epistolam aut tomum dogmaticum, in quo ejus styli character clarius eluceat, quibus præsertim præfatur, ut in aliis ad eumdem Marinum dogmaticis, aliosque viros sacros, diversi argumenti epistolis. Nec sic a vero et catholico sensu abhorret Maximi expressio, ut tam facile ejus relinquendum vadimonium viro catholico sit. Una causa Pater absolute, seu auctor: nec Filius auctor, nisi quia a Patre, et ejus quasi virtus, quo cum una causa, et principium de principio: quod Græci libentius particula per exprimunt, ut ordinem significent, et Patri τὸ προκαταρκτικόν, ratione primæ causæ et originalis (a se scilicet) servent. Operæ pretium igitur fecit Maximus, qui et vivus, et vita functus, dissentaneas in specie Doctorum voces Virorumque Ecclesiasticorum eodem sensu, velut olim Athanasius in eadem prope materia (ac περὶ Θεολογίας) commiserit. Locum Maximi eo ipso sensu egregie exponit Joan. Veccus, orat. 2, t. II Græciæ orth. p. 63 et 64, ubi omissa fusiori explicatione, quam Damasceno malit impendere, cujus causa difficilior, et quo adversarii magis glorientur, sic paucis de Maximo: Ὑμῖν δὲ οὐκ ἀγαπητὸν τῷ μεγάλῳ Μαξίμῳ κατακολουθεῖν ὑπερηγορούντι Ῥωμαίων, ἐφ᾽ οἷς καὶ τότε ἐκ Πατρὸς καὶ Υἱοῦ τὸ Πνεῦμα ἔλεγον ἐκπορεύεσθαι· καὶ οὐδὲν διαφέρειν, τὸ ἐκ Πατρὸς δι᾽ Υἱοῦ πρὸς τὸ, ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἐκδιδάσκοντι· δι᾽ ὧν ἡ ἐπιστολὴ τὸ, ἐκ τοῦ Υἱοῦ ἐκπορεύεσθαι, εἰς τὸ, δι᾽ Υἱοῦ προϊέναι σαφῶς ἐκλαμβάνεται; Ubi Leo All. An non volupe vobis fuerit, magnum Maximum sequi, patrocinium Romanorum suscipientem, qui tum ex Patre et Filio Spiritum procedere affirmabant: nihilque differre ex Patre per Filium, et ex utroque, edocentem: quibus in illa epistola, Ex Filio procedere, in illud, Per Filium procedere, assumitur. Omissa Allatio particula καὶ, ipsa non inutilis, qua significatur esse hoc argumento antiquæ traditioni, cum nulla Ecclesiarum scissione sic Romana Ecclesia doceret, nec Græcam lateret. Vexatque egregie hoc argumentum Georgius Metochita in suis de processione Spiritus sancti quinque libris, a me ipsi Leoni Allatio transmissis, ut in tertium suæ Græciæ orthodoxæ volumen conferret; sicque transmissi tum mihi periere, tum rei ecclesiasticæ. Extrema verba 74 sic redderem: Ea ratione quod epistola, ex Filio procedere, palam exponat, ut sit idem ac per Filium prodire. Fuse vero idem ad Constantinum, l. iv, p. 203 et seq., ubi optime quasi utrique parti de processione Spiritus sancti studentem sanctum Maximum, molliendo illa verba junxisse, ut ad assensum traheret, rixamque sedaret: Ἐξ ὧν οὐκ αἰτίαν τοῦ Πνεύματος τὸν Υἱὸν ποιοῦντας σφᾶς ἑαυτοὺς ἀπέδειξαν. Ostenderunt se non causam Spiritus Filium facere: qualem illi sibi videbantur ex ipsorum verbis et modo profitendi, habere: primam scilicet, et qualis est Pater: quod ipsum illis ex contentione provenerat, et quod Latini Monothelitarum hæresis causa ipsos perstringerent; ut optime idem Veccus, p. 47, dicens se deprehendisse ex Maximi epistola; ac facile quisque advertat: nec aliter in Photio et Cerulario, sic privata odia vertentibus in causam fidei. In excerpto, ex epistola quam Regius Cod. laudatus Romæ scriptam dicit, Romanus alter, qui solus habet, cum epistola ad Marinum conjungit, quasi sit illius pars: καὶ μεθ᾽ ἕτερα· quomodo non Romæ, — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [On] the passage of Maximus from the letter to Marinus, about the procession of the Spirit from the Father and the Son, in the synodical letters of Martin I, by which the Constantinopolitans were offended, and which Maximus himself explained so that the Latins hold not two causes but one, the Father: some, even Catholics, doubt whether it is Maximus’s; and I fear that Leo Allatius is rather too liberal, in his Vindication of the Synod of Ephesus of Cyril [Vindiciis synodi Ephesinæ Cyrilli], ch. 72, when he says that most doubt it [plerosque addubitare]. Anastasius in his Collectanea reported it as a matter of certain faith, and judged that it has weight for the faith and the Church. At the Council of Lyons under Pope Gregory X, I do not know who doubted it: not Beccus, not George Metochites, etc. At Florence, by that distinction and judgment of Maximus the peace of the Churches was made: not Blemmydes and other Catholics from the schismatics, [nor] Palamas, Mark of Ephesus, etc. [as printed: ‘non Blemidas aliique Catholici ex schismaticis, Palamas, Marcus Ephesius’]. The most ancient books preserved it, among them that Roman codex older than the Seventh Synod, from which the Regius [codex] was copied under the emperor Michael Doukas Angelos Komnenos. Those who doubt, I judge to be little versed in the reading of Maximus, and I think there exists no letter or dogmatic tome of his in which the character of his style shines out more clearly, especially in those [passages] where he prefaces, as in the other dogmatic letters to the same Marinus, and in letters of different content to other holy men. Nor does Maximus’s expression so far depart from the true and Catholic sense that a Catholic should so easily abandon the pledge [to it]. One cause: the Father absolutely, or author; the Son is not author except in that he is from the Father, and as his power, with which he is one cause, and a principle from a principle; which the Greeks more willingly express by the particle ‘through’, to signify order, and to preserve for the Father the προκαταρκτικόν (‘originating’), by reason of first and original cause (that is, from himself). Maximus therefore did well, who, alive and having ended his life, brought together in one sense voices that in appearance disagree, of Doctors and of Ecclesiastical men, as Athanasius did once on nearly the same subject (also on theology). John Veccus, Oration 2, vol. II of his Græcia orthodoxa, pp. 63 and 64, explains Maximus’s passage splendidly in that very sense, where, omitting a fuller explanation, which he prefers to give to Damascene, whose case is harder and over which the adversaries boast more, he says briefly of Maximus: [Greek, quoted:] ‘It is not agreeable to you to follow the great Maximus, who speaks up for the Romans, on account of which they then said that the Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son; and that there is no difference between “from the Father through the Son” and “from both”, teaching; by which the letter’s “to proceed from the Son” is clearly taken as “to go forth through the Son”?’ [my rendering of the Greek, which is partly broken in print]. Here Leo Allatius [says]: ‘Would it not please you to follow the great Maximus, who undertakes the defence of the Romans, who then affirmed that the Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son: and teaches that there is no difference between from the Father through the Son and from both: in which letter “to proceed from the Son” is taken up into “to proceed through the Son”.’ Allatius omits the particle καί, itself not useless, by which it is shown that this is an argument from ancient tradition, since the Roman Church taught so with no division of the Churches, and it was not hidden from the Greek [Church]. George Metochites vigorously presses this argument in his five books on the procession of the Holy Spirit, which I sent to Leo Allatius himself, to bring into the third volume of his Græcia orthodoxa; and so they, when sent, were lost, both to me and to the ecclesiastical cause. The last words I would render thus: ‘In the sense that the letter says [the Spirit] proceeds from the Son, it plainly explains it so that it is the same as to go forth through the Son.’ At length, too, [Veccus] to Constantine, book 4, p. 203 ff., where [he shows] very well that holy Maximus, as one concerned for both parties on the procession of the Holy Spirit, joined those words by softening them, in order to draw them to assent and calm the quarrel: [Greek:] ‘from which they showed themselves not to be making the Son the cause of the Spirit.’ They showed that they were not making the Son a cause of the Spirit: such as they seemed to themselves, from their own words and manner of speaking, to hold: a first [cause], that is, and such as the Father is; which had arisen for them from the dispute, and because the Latins accused them [the Greeks] of the heresy of the Monothelites; as the same Veccus says very well, p. 47, saying that he gathered this from Maximus’s letter; and anyone may readily notice that it was no otherwise in Photius and Cerularius, who turned private hatreds into a cause of the faith. In the excerpt from the letter which the Regius codex, cited above, says was written at Rome — the other, Roman [codex], which alone has it, joins it to the letter to Marinus, as if it were a part of it: καὶ μεθ᾽ ἕτερα (‘and after other things’) — how not at Rome,
  • p. 0087 (notae, marker illegible): sed Byzacii in Africa scripta esset. Ac num imperitus librarius Byzantium sibi pro Byzacio divinavit, ut eamdem epistolam, eodem loco scriptam sibi finxerit? Anastasius, quod non defloravit, nec habuit: nunquam omissurus sic illustre Romanæ fidei et Ecclesiæ, Petri privilegio, testimonium ac elogium. Nihil autem abhorret a Maximi aliis, quibus per occasionem Romanos laudat, eorumque in fide firmitatem: nusquam non fidei primatum et arcem Ecclesiasticæ communionis, in Romano Antistite ponens, a quo et absolutio ab hæresi impetranda sit, ac cujus arbitratu transigenda fidei negotia. Movere possit, quod sex synodos numerat, cum sexta synodus Maximo jam consummato celebrata sit. Existimo sextam voluisse ipsam Romanam et Lateranensem Martino I pontifice, quæ pro sui celebritate sic quinque generalibus Maximo comnumerari potuit: qua vere Catholicis supremo judicio confossa hydra videretur, cui interfuisset aut communicasset, quidquid esset orthodoxum, reliquis per Orientem sedibus, excepta Hierosolymitana, Monothelitarum hæresi infectis: in qua perinde Sophronio auctore ea damnata fuerat, uti etiam a Joan. IV Romæ: sed non ea pompa et celebritate, qua a Martino e concilio Lateranensi, Maximo ipso illi incentore, cujus acta illustrissima exstarent, in quibus ventilata omnia, adductaque Scripturæ ac Patrum testimonia, quibus veritas catholica lucidissime statueretur, ac quidquid Monothelitarum furfuris esset, exsufflaretur, illorum etiam nominatim proscriptis capitibus: ut jam una Christianitatis tessera viderentur, in sancta una catholica, quo tantum sui desiderium per Orientem veris fidelibus consciverant, quantum Theodosius Gangrensis presbyter testatur in Hypomnestico. Nec ultra trahenda Maximi epistola ad Petrum illustrem, quam nobis servarit Anastasius in Collectaneis excerptam, unaque nobis Græce desideratur: cum alia dogmatica nobis exstent ad eumdem, in epistolis, quas sequentibus repræsento. — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): […how not at Rome,] but written at Byzacium in Africa? And did an unskilled copyist, guessing Byzantium for Byzacium, invent for himself the same letter written in the same place? Anastasius, [for the part] he did not cull, he also did not have: he would never have omitted so illustrious a testimony and praise of the Roman faith and Church, of Peter’s privilege. Nor is it at all at odds with Maximus’s other letters, in which on occasion he praises the Romans and their firmness in the faith: everywhere placing the primacy of faith and the citadel of ecclesiastical communion in the Roman Bishop, from whom absolution from heresy is to be obtained and at whose judgment the business of the faith is to be settled. It might raise a difficulty that he counts six synods, when the sixth synod was held after Maximus had finished his course. I think he meant the Roman synod itself, the Lateran under Pope Martin I, which, for its renown, could be reckoned by Maximus with the five general councils: in which the hydra of the Monothelites was truly pierced by the supreme judgment of Catholics, [and] whoever had taken part or communicated, whatever was orthodox, while the other sees of the East, Jerusalem excepted, were infected with the Monothelite heresy: in which [the heresy] had been condemned likewise at the instigation of Sophronius, as also by John IV at Rome: but not with that pomp and renown with which it was condemned by Martin in the Lateran council, Maximus himself being its instigator, whose most illustrious acts survive, in which everything was debated, and testimonies of Scripture and the Fathers adduced, by which Catholic truth would be most clearly established, and whatever of the Monothelite chaff there was would be blown away, their leaders being proscribed by name, so that they might now appear a single badge of Christianity, in the one holy catholic [Church], for which true believers throughout the East had conceived as great a longing as Theodosius, priest of Gangra, bears witness in his Hypomnesticum. Nor is Maximus’s letter to Peter the Illustrious to be stretched further, which Anastasius preserved for us, excerpted, in his Collectanea, and which alone is lacking to us in Greek: since other dogmatic letters of his to the same man survive, among the letters which I reproduce in what follows.
  • p. 0088 (numbered, 91): Hæc forte post Theodori papæ excommunicationem: vel cum sede ejectus et Urbe in Africa ageret. — keyed to urbis — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): This perhaps after the excommunication by Pope Theodore; or when, driven from the see and the City, he was living in Africa.
  • p. 0089 (notae, d): (d) Ex Regio cod. collato cum nostro Tiliano S. Hilarii et Dufresnii editisque Latine a Turriano. Eadem fere Leontius. Opusculum hoc ipsum breve, quod tam multis codd. exstat, argumento est, pluris Ecclesiæ catholicæ alumnis olim habitum, eisque velut tesseræ loco fuisse adversus hæreticos, qui vel de Deitate male sensissent, vel de dispensatione seu incarnatione; ne omissa quidem Macedonii hæresi, in nostro sancti Hilarii cod., sive ipse sanctus Maximus illam inseruit, sive alius quispiam studiosus adjecit, ut esset plenior doctrina; quod vero similius putem, vel inde quod in aliis ubique codd. desideratur. Placuit vero etiam repræsentare. Titulus apud Turr. paulo auctior; addit enim, Et quod Arius et Nestorius in theologia et incarnatione, contra atque Sabellius et Eutyches sentiant: quod ipsum indicat, unum hoc Maximum spectasse, ut contrarias has hæreses in Trinitate et Incarnatione contenderet, eisque fidem catholicam, ipsam illarum mediam, opponeret. — keyed to φύσεων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): From the Regius codex, collated with our Tilianus of St Hilary [Tiliano S. Hilarii] and with Dufresne’s, and with the Latin editions by Turrianus. Leontius is nearly the same. This very opuscule, short as it is, which survives in so many codices, is proof that it was once highly esteemed by many sons of the Catholic Church and served them as a sort of watchword against heretics who thought wrongly either about the Godhead or about the dispensation, that is the incarnation; nor is the heresy of Macedonius omitted, in our codex of St Hilary, whether Maximus himself inserted it or some zealous person added it to make the teaching fuller; I think the latter more likely, also because it is lacking everywhere in the other codices. I decided to reproduce it as well. The title in Turrianus is somewhat longer, for he adds ‘and that Arius and Nestorius think, in theology and in the incarnation, contrary to Sabellius and Eutyches’; which itself shows that Maximus had this one aim, to set these opposing heresies in the Trinity and the Incarnation against each other and to oppose to them the Catholic faith, which stands midway between them.
  • p. 0089 (notae, e): (e) Καὶ Θεὸν πῶς διαβεβαιοῖ. Sic noster ille cod. non enclitice. Qui velit τὸ, πῶς, ἐμφατικῶς dici, ac velut quadam admiratione, nihil ipse inficias ierim. Sic certe quod altero statim numero sequitur, Πῶς ἡ ἄκρα ἕνωσις, admiratione reddo; quidquid Turrianus interrogatione: Quomodo summa unio et idem habet et diversum? Non id quærit Maximus, sed admiratur, in divinis æque ac in creatis: nisi quod in divinis, non satis aptum, τὸ, diversum et diversitas, quod sic proclive Turr. usurpat: pro eo quod est distinctum et distinctio, sed aliud, vel alterum, vel differens ac differentia; quod nimirum diversus divisionem videatur inducere; sicque reddit Ambros. Calep. diversus, separatus, κεχωρισμένος: quo facit illud Cæsaris De bello Afr.: Cum Numidas diversos dissipatosque in omnes partes fugere vidissent; itemque Plauti in Rud.: Inde suam quisque ibant diversi domum. Ἑτερότητα dicant, si liceret, castigatiores theologi, alietatem; uti etiam alia eis persona atque alia in divinis dicitur: quanquam ἕτερος respectu duorum proprie, ἄλλος etiam plurium. Ad illa verba Hilariani nostri cod. ἢ ταυτότητα οὐσιῶν, addo quæ necessaria sunt, ut sit plenus sensus, καὶ ἑτερότητα προσώπων, καὶ τὸ ἔμπαλιν. Aliter enim se res in Deo habet, aliter in Christo ac nobis, modoque opposito; quod utrumque propositum exponere Maximo, cum utrobique summa unio sit, altera essentialis seu natura, altera personalis ac subsistentia. — keyed to διαβεβαιοῖ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ‘And how he affirms God.’ So our codex, not enclitically. Whoever wants τὸ πῶς (‘how’) said emphatically, and as with a certain wonder, I would not deny. So certainly I render with wonder what follows at once in the next number, ‘How the highest union has both sameness and otherness’ (Πῶς ἡ ἄκρα ἕνωσις); whereas Turrianus [renders it] by a question, ‘How does the highest union have both the same and the diverse?’ Maximus is not asking this but wondering, in divine things as in created; except that in divine matters the words ‘diversum’ and ‘diversitas’ are not quite apt, which Turrianus readily uses for ‘distinctum’ and ‘distinctio’, but [they mean] ‘other’, or ‘another’, or ‘differing’ and ‘difference’; since ‘diversus’ seems to bring in division; and so Ambrosius Calepinus renders ‘diversus’ as ‘separate’ (κεχωρισμένος): to which belongs Caesar’s phrase in the African War, ‘when they saw the Numidians scattered and fleeing in every direction’, and likewise Plautus in the Rudens, ‘thence each went his way, in different directions, home’. The more careful theologians would say ἑτερότης, if it were allowed, ‘alietas’; as also one person and another is said in divine matters: although ἕτερος properly regards two, ἄλλος more than two. To those words of our Hilarian codex, ἢ ταυτότητα οὐσιῶν, I add what is necessary to complete the sense, ‘and the otherness of persons, and the reverse’. For the matter stands one way in God, another in Christ and in us, and in the opposite manner; each proposition Maximus had to set out, since in both there is the highest union, the one essential or of nature, the other personal and of subsistence.
  • p. 0090 (notae, f): (f) Διὰ τὸ μὴ ἰδιοϋπόστατον. Nec Turrianus aliam habuerit vocem, vel Maximus aliam scripserit. Quod ergo reddit: Et propter propriam sententiam dic unam hypostasim, omissa scilicet particula negante, ejus propositio minime sana sit. Quæ enim theologia, ut propter subsistentiam propriam duplicis substantiæ Verbi et carnis, quam scilicet proprie ac seorsim, propriamque sibi quodque horum vindicet, una in Christo dicenda sit hypostasis aut persona? non econtra, quod non sit propria, sed communis per unitionem facta ut omnes nostri codd. repræsentant: debuitque vir doctus ac pius, vel si ita illi laborabat ejus cod. aberatque negatio, non tam facile decoquere. Sic quoque num. 4 et 5. male illi desiderata negatio, cujus absentia, ipsa statim simplex propositio offendit, etsi in vi hypotheticæ verus sensus utcumque elici queat. — keyed to ἰδιοϋπόστατον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ‘Because it is not a hypostasis of its own [Διὰ τὸ μὴ ἰδιοϋπόστατον].’ Neither would Turrianus have had another word, nor Maximus have written another. What he therefore renders, ‘and by reason of a proper sentiment/subsistence say one hypostasis’ [Et propter propriam sententiam dic unam hypostasim], omitting the negating particle, makes his proposition not at all sound. For what theology would it be, that by reason of the proper subsistence of the twofold substance of the Word and of the flesh, which each of them claims as its own, properly and separately, one hypostasis or person should be said in Christ? On the contrary, [it is said] because it is not proper but common, made through the union, as all our codices represent: and the learned and pious man should, even if his own codex had it so and lacked the negation, not so easily have toned it down. So also at nos. 4 and 5 he wrongly missed the negation, whose absence offends the bare proposition at once, though in the force of a hypothetical a true sense can somehow be drawn out.
  • p. 0091 (numbered, 96): Ex Hœsch. Augustana edit. emendata ex codice Raph. Dufr. — keyed to διάφοροι — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): From the Augsburg edition of Hoeschel, emended from the codex of Raphael Dufresne.
  • p. 0093 (notae, a): (a) Πνευματικὸς τόμος. Missa hæc epistola, sive liber dogmaticus ad Stephanum Dorensem episcopum, fidei catholicæ cum archiepiscopo suo Sophronio adversus Monothelitas strenuum defensorem, inque eam rem ab eodem Romam missum, ubi orthodoxorum dogmatum fundamenta exsisterent, inquit idem Sophronius egregia illa suggestione, quæ exstat in concil. Later. sub Martino I, secretar. seu consult. 2. Cumque Maximus hanc Romam mittat, argumento est, sub exordium fere novi dogmatis missam esse, et cum solum edita esset Heracliana, seu magis Sergiana Ecthesis, in quam unam invehitur, nec Constantis Typus adhuc cusus esset, cujus illi Paulus, ipse in eo concilio damnatus, ac a Stephano exagitatus, auctor fuit. Tantæ ergo fidei in Stephano præcipuus incentor videatur Maximus; potuitque per eum sic Theodoro papæ innotescere, ut ei vices suas, et Conservatorium, ut vocabant, in Palæstina ac Hierosolymitana diœcesi concrederet: quo in munere suas strenue exsecutum partes, depositis, qui libellum pœnitentiæ non obtulissent, male promotis per Sergium Joppensem episcopis, ac qui fidei catholicæ scripto assensum non præbuissent, ipse in ea suggestione locuples testis exsistit. Quo et Romani antistitis mire elucet auctoritas, et sollicitudo Ecclesiarum, in causa præsertim fidei, qualis hic vertebatur, ubique terrarum vere orthodoxis spectatissima. Πρωτόθρονον Hierosolymitani throni seu sedis ac diœcesis haud obscure se Stephanus significat, sive id habebat suæ sedis privilegio, quæ esset prima et dignior usu ecclesiastico, cæteris post ipsam Hierosolymitanam sedem, sive etiam quia ipse senior episcopus erat, quomodo Africa primates suos agnoscebat; aut denique quod Conservatoriam a sede apostolica acceperat, vacante sede Hierosolymitana per Sophronii obitum. Hanc porro epistolam habuit etiam cod. Em. card. Barberini, ex qua plurima mutuata sexta synodus, præsertim Agatho in sua suggestione, ex cujus sensu peracta omnia in illo concilio, quæ ad fidei negotium spectabant, ut ejus triumphi præcipuæ partes Maximi fuisse videantur, ac gloria prostratæ Monothelitarum hæresis, tum Romæ Martino papa, tum Constantinopoli Agathone. — keyed to τόμος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (a) "Πνευματικὸς τόμος" (Spiritual Tome). This epistle, or dogmatic book, was sent to Stephen, bishop of Dora, as a stalwart defender of the catholic faith together with his archbishop Sophronius against the Monothelites, and was sent by him to Rome for that purpose, where the foundations of orthodox doctrines were to be found, as the same Sophronius says in that excellent suggestio which is extant in the Lateran council under Martin I, in the secretarium or consultation 2. Since Maximus sends it to Rome, it is evidence that it was composed near the beginning of the new doctrine, and when only the Heraclian, or rather Sergian, Ecthesis had been published, against which alone he inveighs, and Constans's Typos had not yet been struck, of which Paul, himself condemned in that council and attacked by Stephen, was the author. So Maximus seems the chief instigator of such great faith in Stephen; and through him he could thus become known to Pope Theodore, so that he entrusted to him his own vicarial powers and the Conservatorium, as they called it, in Palestine and the diocese of Jerusalem. In that office he vigorously carried out his duties: he deposed those who had not offered a petition of penance and had been wrongly promoted by Sergius of Joppa as bishops, and those who had not given assent to the written statement of the catholic faith; he himself stands as a reliable witness in that suggestio. By this the authority of the Roman pontiff shines out wonderfully, and the care of the Churches, especially in a cause of faith such as was being treated here, most conspicuous everywhere among the truly orthodox. Stephen indicates not obscurely that he is "Πρωτόθρονος" of the throne, or see and diocese, of Jerusalem, whether he had it by the privilege of his see, which was first and more worthy by ecclesiastical custom, the others coming after the see of Jerusalem itself; or also because he was himself the senior bishop, as Africa recognized its primates; or finally because he had received the Conservatoria from the apostolic see, the see of Jerusalem being vacant through Sophronius's death. This epistle, moreover, Cardinal Barberini's codex also contained, from which the sixth synod borrowed very much, especially Agatho in his suggestio, from whose sense everything in that council that concerned the matter of faith was carried out, so that the principal part of its triumph seems to have been Maximus's, and the glory of the overthrow of the Monothelite heresy, both at Rome under Pope Martin and at Constantinople under Agatho.

What stood a little further on, "περιπατεῖ ὁ Ἰησοῦς" (Jesus walks), is emended to "περιεπάτει" (walked): because the sacred text so has it and the sequence of the narrative requires it, "for he did not wish", which alone Pope Agatho cited in his own report (anaphora).

  • p. 0097 (notae, c): (c) Μὴ περὶ τοῦ κατεληλυθότος. In editis Gregorii, ut et in act. illa 4 sextæ synodi, est παρὰ, ut et statim sequentibus, ὡς παρὰ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου. Ipse certe, et de se ipse Christus loquebatur, qui e cœlo descenderit; quo videtur insinuari sensus formalis, ut in sua ipse persona loquatur, non communiter in persona hominum, et solum per arrogationem, tribuendo sibi quæ humani generis communia sunt. Non video quid sibi velit, qui Morelianæ editioni marg. ascripsit, ἴσ. παρὰ σοῦ. Nihil certe, quidquid est, videtur frugi. — keyed to κατεληλυθότος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (c) "Not about the one who had come down." In the editions of Gregory, as also in act 4 of the sixth synod, it reads παρὰ, as also in what follows at once, ὡς παρὰ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου. Surely Christ himself was speaking of himself also, he who had come down from heaven; by which a formal sense seems to be hinted, that he speaks in his own person, not in the common way in the person of men, and only by arrogation, attributing to himself what is common to the human race. I do not see what he means who wrote in the margin of the Morellian edition, 'perhaps παρὰ σοῦ'. Whatever it is, it certainly seems of no use.
  • p. 0097 (notae, d): (d) Τὸ συναφὲς τῆς ἑαυτοῦ πρὸς τὸν Πατέρα θεότητος. Mendose Latina act. illa 4 ejusdem synodi, Ostendit conjunctionem propriæ divinitatis Patrem, pro, cum Patre. Pleraque hujus epistolæ extant in illa suggestione Agathonis, etsi quædam paulo brevius in ea sunt relata: ut in sequenti loco Chrysost. omissum illud, Ὁρᾷς πῶς καὶ τὴν προτέραν αὐτοῦ ἡλικίαν προανεφώνησεν: ubi male Ducæus plurali numero, προανεφώνησαν, prænuntiarint, cum sermo sit de uno Isaia, sic de Christi priore ætate vaticinato. Illud etiam ejus postea, ἐπὶ τῆς δυνάμεως in qua ait Chrysostom. Catholicos ponere, quod Filius sit unum cum Patre, cum hæretici ponerent in voluntate, in majestate, liberius est: non enim ea voce Græci majestatem significant, sed virtutem ac potentiam, quæ ipsa naturæ index est. — keyed to Θεότητος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (d) "The connection of his own divinity with the Father." The Latin in act 4 of the same synod is faulty: 'Ostendit conjunctionem propriae divinitatis Patrem' for 'cum Patre'. Most of this epistle stands in that suggestio of Agatho, although some things are reported there more briefly; as in the following passage of Chrysostom the omitted phrase 'Ὁρᾷς πῶς καὶ τὴν προτέραν αὐτοῦ ἡλικίαν προανεφώνησεν' (see how he also foretold his earlier age); where Ducaeus wrongly puts it in the plural, προανεφώνησαν, 'prenuntiarint', though the discourse is about Isaiah alone, who prophesied thus of Christ's earlier age. Also his later phrase, ἐπὶ τῆς δυνάμεως, in which Chrysostom says Catholics place that the Son is one with the Father, whereas heretics placed it in will: 'in majestate' is too free a rendering, for the Greeks do not mean majesty by that word but virtue and power, which itself is the sign of nature.

  • p. 0099 (notae, e): suggestio, nec male Latina, docebatur a Deo Verbo gestata caro, hoc deinceps minime pati. At certe pessime, nihilque ecclesiastice Bonaventura Vulcan. Considera quod caro, quæ a Verbo gestabatur, non bene ad hoc ferendum affecta erat. Plane docebatur sic humano more indulgere affectibus, ad ostensionem assumptæ humanitatis, ut in eis non diutius moraretur, sed fortiori animo hos compesceret, ut cum infremuit in Lazaro, quasi cohibendo naturalem animi miserantis motum. Sic et in passione, Verumtamen non mea, sed tua voluntas fiat. — keyed to πάσχειν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ...suggestio [of Agatho], and not badly in Latin: 'the flesh borne by God the Word was taught henceforth not to suffer this.' But Bonaventura Vulcanius (renders it) very badly and in no ecclesiastical manner: 'Consider that the flesh, which was borne by the Word, was not well disposed to bear this.' Plainly it was being taught to indulge the affections in a human way, to show the humanity assumed, so as not to linger long in them but to restrain them with a stronger spirit, as when he groaned at Lazarus, as if checking the natural movement of a pitying soul. So too in the passion: 'Nevertheless not my will but thine be done.'
  • p. 0099 (notae, f): (f) Ἡ αὐτὴ ἐνέργεια ἐκ διαφόρου ἐστὶν οὐσίας. Plane luxatio est, reddiditque Maximus, διαφόρου ἐξουσίας, quod Ambrosii textus habet: Quemadmodum eadem operatio diversæ esset potestatis? Ambrosii voce, non admodum propria, motus, ut et Græcam adhiberet, ei respondentem, ipsam non satis ex usu philosophico; meliusque Agathonis suggestionis interpr., ἡ αὐτὴ ἐνέργεια διαφόρου — keyed to οὐσίας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (f) "The same operation is of a different essence." This is plainly a dislocation, and Maximus rendered it "of a different authority" (διαφόρου ἐξουσίας), which is what Ambrose's text has: "How could the same operation be of different power?" Moved by Ambrose's wording, which is not quite proper, to supply a Greek one corresponding to it, itself not sufficiently in philosophical usage; and Agatho's suggestio translates better: "ἡ αὐτὴ ἐνέργεια διαφόρου" [the note breaks off here at the foot of the page and continues on p-0100].
  • p. 0100 (notae, f): ἐστὶν δυνάμεως. Sic quoque sequentibus Maximus vocem ἐξουσίας usurpavit, Μίαν εἶναι τὴν θέλησιν, μίαν τὴν ἐνέργειαν, κατὰ μίαν καὶ τὴν αὐτὴν ἐξουσίαν προαγομένην. Ἐξουσία in illis magis proprie dicatur, quæ γνωμικῶς, ex arbitrio et sententia, quam quæ φυσικῶς (natura scilicet) volumus ac operamur. Quod etiam paulo post sequitur in Leonis auctoritate, ἐνεργεῖ ἐν ἑκατέρᾳ μορφῇ, ipsum quoque emend. ex Latinis, ἐνεργεῖ ἑκατέρα μορφή· agit utraque forma; quo etiam modo in sequentibus habetur, ubi eædem auctoritates breviter perstringuntur. Forte scripserit Maximus ac reddiderit, ἐνεργεῖ γὰρ ἐν ἑκατέρᾳ μορφῇ· quod nihil sensu a Latinis diversum, sed paulo elegantius redditum. In ea Leonis epistola, qualem Græcam repræsentavit Gerardus Vossius, multæ sunt variæ lectiones, quidquid diligentiæ adhibitum, ut ipsa pura, qualis a Leone edita erat, ubique in Ecclesiis haberetur, quasi veræ fidei tessera. Ipsa Leonis locutio visa confundentibus longe durior, quasi divisioni in specie favens: ipsa tamen necessaria ad ipsorum magis elidendam impietatem. Lenius illi habuissent, quod ita hic repræsentatur; ac num illorum aliquis (vel ipse antiquarius) sic lenire voluit? Haud certe, putem, ipse Maximus. Rem probant quæ hic, pluresque in actis sextæ synodi exstant, eorum auctoritates. — keyed to οὐσίας — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): ...[Agatho's reading] ἐστὶν δυνάμεως ('is of power'). So too in what follows Maximus used the word ἐξουσία: "that there is one will, one operation, according to one and the same authority going forth." In those passages ἐξουσία is more properly said of what we will and do γνωμικῶς (by choice and judgment) than of what we do φυσικῶς (naturally). The same appears a little later in the authority of Leo: ἐνεργεῖ ἐν ἑκατέρᾳ μορφῇ, which too is to be emended from the Latin to ἐνεργεῖ ἑκατέρα μορφή, 'each form acts'; it is so also in what follows, where the same authorities are briefly touched on. Perhaps Maximus wrote and rendered ἐνεργεῖ γὰρ ἐν ἑκατέρᾳ μορφῇ, which is in no way different in sense from the Latin, but rendered a little more elegantly. In that epistle of Leo, as Gerardus Vossius presented it in Greek, there are many variant readings, whatever care was applied to keep it pure, as it was issued by Leo, in the Churches everywhere, as the token of the true faith. Leo's very phrasing seemed far harsher to the confounders, as if favouring division in kind; yet it was needed all the more to crush their impiety. They would have had it milder, as it is represented here; and did one of them (or the copyist himself) wish to soften it thus? Surely not, I think, Maximus himself. The matter is proved by what is here, and by more in the acts of the sixth synod, their authorities.
  • p. 0101 (notae, g): (g) Ἐν τῷ ἐπιγεγραμμένῳ, Αὐτὴν ἐπιφάνειαν. In Agathonis suggest. aliter et melius, Εἰς τὰ ἐπιφάνια τῆς ἐνανθρωπήσεως τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγου: qui sit ipse libri seu orationis titulus; quod vero habet Maximi epistola, ejus initium. Plene itaque scripserit Maximus: Ἐν τῷ ἐπιγεγραμμένῳ λόγῳ εἰς τὰ ἐπιφάνια τῆς ἐνανθρωπήσεως τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγου, οὗ ἡ ἀρχή, Αὐτὴν ἐπιφάνειαν. Sic passim breviarium auctorum scripta antiquarii, sæpe etiam nullo judicio. — keyed to ἐπιφάνειαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (g) "In the one entitled: Autên epiphaneian." In Agatho's suggestio, otherwise and better: "Εἰς τὰ ἐπιφάνια τῆς ἐνανθρωπήσεως τοῦ Θεοῦ Λόγου" (On the Epiphany of the Incarnation of God the Word), which is the title itself of the book or oration; what Maximus's epistle has is its beginning. So Maximus will have written in full: "In the discourse entitled On the Epiphany of the Incarnation of God the Word, whose beginning is 'Autên epiphaneian'." Thus throughout the copyist abbreviates the writings of authors, often also with no judgment.
  • p. 0102 (notae, h): (h) Μίαν τὴν γνῶσιν. Sic etiam act. 10 sextæ synodi. Μία γὰρ ἡ γνῶσις ἐπὶ Χριστοῦ, καθάπερ καὶ ἡ ἐνέργεια. Nec pro φύσις obrepsit, cum sequitur, Καὶ γὰρ εἷς ἦν ὁ γινώσκων, ὥσπερ ἀμέλει καὶ ὁ ἐνεργῶν. Sic nempe Themistius, ex una operatione unam Christi inferebat scientiam, uti et unam voluntatem: in qua vel maxime vim faciebant sequaces Monothelitæ, tanquam ipsa duplex admitti non posset, quin esset contraria: quam unam contrarietatem, et qualis nobis inolevit ex Adæ peccato, præferebant velle a Christo excludere: quo et fraudem passus Joannes abbas, ipse auctor Honorio, ut ad eum sensum Orientalibus responderet (quæ ipsa ejus satisfactio est) necdum scilicet satis explorato pro Latinorum ingenuitate, fideique sinceritate, quid iidem Orientales adversum fidei in una illa voluntate, eis asserta, molirentur, donec Sophronius, Stephanus, Maximus, aliique viri egregie catholici, Matri suppetias euntes, detectisque Syrorum insidiis, nobilissimos Romanos antistites Joannem IV, Theodorum, Martinum, ad hydram certis ictibus conficiendam, pro Romanæ sedis suprema in rebus fidei potestate, præclare animarunt; negotio demum felici exitu per Dominum, Agathonem, Leonem II et Constantinum peracto. — keyed to γνῶσιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (h) "Μίαν τὴν γνῶσιν" (the one knowledge). So also in act 10 of the sixth synod: "For there is one knowledge in Christ, as also one operation." Nor has φύσις crept in for it, since there follows, "For he who knows is one, just as, of course, he who operates." So Themistius inferred from one operation one knowledge of Christ, as also one will: on which point the Monothelite followers laid special stress, as though the double could not be admitted without being contrary; and that one contrariety, such as grew in us from Adam's sin, they preferred to exclude from Christ. Abbot John, himself the author for Honorius, was deceived by this fraud, so as to answer the Orientals in that sense (which is his own satisfaction), not having yet, in keeping with the frankness and sincerity of the Latins, sufficiently examined what those same Orientals were contriving against the faith in that one will which they asserted, until Sophronius, Stephen, Maximus and other eminently catholic men, going to the aid of their Mother (Church) and the Syrians' snares having been exposed, spurred the most noble Roman prelates John IV, Theodore and Martin, splendidly, for the Roman see's supreme power in matters of faith, to finish off the hydra with sure blows; the business at last being brought to a happy end through the Lord, Agatho, Leo II and Constantine.
  • p. 0108 (notae, i): (i) Ἀλλὰ αὐτοβουλίαν. Emendo ex Sophronii Synodica, ταυτοβουλίαν· hoc enim est ἓν θέλημα, quod ipse exponit Maximus. Sic Sophronius Christi mysterium explicari dicit a Nestorio, ἰσοτιμίᾳ, ταυτοβουλίᾳ· pari honoris consortio, eadem voluntate, etc. Sic quoque sequenti tractatu de eodem, Ἐξ ἧς καὶ τὴν ταυτοβουλίαν συνίστησι, ἢ τὴν πολυβουλίαν, εἰπεῖν οἰκειότερον. Scite vero id, ταυτοβουλίαν, πολυβουλίαν, quod una illa Nestorii voluntas, solum affectu aut habitudine sit una, σχετικῶς: reipsa vero et natura personaque illi multiplex, uti se res in amicitia, qua sic unum dicimur ac unus, habet. — keyed to αὐτοβουλίαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (i) "Ἀλλὰ αὐτοβουλίαν." I emend, from Sophronius's Synodica, to ταυτοβουλίαν (sameness of will); for this is ἓν θέλημα (one will), which Maximus himself explains. So Sophronius says the mystery of Christ is explained by Nestorius as ἰσοτιμίᾳ, ταυτοβουλίᾳ: by an equal sharing of honour, by the same will, etc. So also in the following treatise on the same subject: "from which he also establishes the ταυτοβουλίαν, or rather the πολυβουλίαν, to speak more fittingly." And you rightly see this, ταυτοβουλίαν, πολυβουλίαν: that Nestorius's one will is one only in affection or habit, relatively (σχετικῶς); but in fact, in nature and person, it is manifold for him, as things stand in friendship, in which we are called one and the same.

  • p. 0110 (notae, j): (j) Περὶ θελημάτων δύο. Hunc tractatum primum refert ill. Annalium Pater, inter eos Maximi tractatus, quos spectare ad superiores illi numeratos non male forsitan conjicit. Videtur is, ad quem scribit, in monachi solum palæstra exercuisse, non vero sacerdotali, seu etiam episcopali dignitate fulsisse. Id certe Maximus ex more insinuasset, sic luculenter ad eum præfatus, ejusque tantis laudata eximia vitæ sanctimonia. Num hæc illa est, quam Stephano ἡγουμένῳ scriptam retulit Photius in ejus Epistolarum catalogo? Tenenda sententia, dum certioris aliquid monumenti prodeat. Luculenta certe epistola est, et inter Maximi dogmaticas non infima. — keyed to θελημάτων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): (j) "Concerning two wills." The illustrious Father of the Annals lists this treatise first among those tracts of Maximus which he conjectures, not badly perhaps, belong to the numbers given above. The man to whom he writes seems to have exercised himself only in the monastic arena, and not to have shone in priestly or even episcopal dignity. Maximus would surely have hinted at this, as is his custom, having addressed him so fully, and praised his outstanding sanctity of life. Is this the letter that Photius reports in his catalogue of Epistles as written to Stephen the hegumen? The opinion is to be held until some more certain monument appears. It is certainly a splendid letter, and by no means the lowest among Maximus's dogmatic ones.
