  • p. 0115 (notae, f): Τὸ μηδὲν ὄντι τὸ σύνολον. Nihil prorsus dicit […]umerum, quod supra res numerata nihil veræ entitatis addat, sed pro earum ratione, vel in rerum natura, vel in sola mente concipiente exsi- […]t; ab illa denique utroque-modo complementum habeat. Formalitates vocant, seu entia metaphysica, ac conceptas rationes: quibus physicos effectus, qualis divisio est, tribuere, merito Maximus absurdum habet. — keyed to σύνολον — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): The phrase Τὸ μηδὲν ὄντι τὸ σύνολον. He says that number adds nothing of true entity over and above the things numbered, but exists according to their nature, either in the nature of things or only in the mind that conceives it, […] and from that, in either way, it has its completion. They call these formalities, that is, metaphysical entities and conceived notions; and to ascribe to them physical effects, such as division is, Maximus rightly holds absurd.
  • p. 0121 (notae, m): Πᾶσαν θείαν καὶ ἀνθρωπίνην. Sic Mono- […]litæ utrinque sibi cavere videbantur scopulum, […] n. 8, dictum est; ut neque cum Severo aliisque […]perioribus confunderent, nec cum Nestorio ipsis- […]e Catholicis (in cujus partes eos male arcesse- […]nt) dividerent. Pu- tem Constantinum papam eorum accepisse satisfa- clionem, nec austerius in ipsos quæsiisse, quos agnosceret catholicam reipsa animo fidem tenuisse, nec quidquam magis quam Ecclesiarum incolumi- tatem in eo negotio spectasse. Ipse Maximus sanum hic sensum deprehendit, si eumdem ipsum Christum secundum aliud et aliud, quod vere erat, alia atque alia, exque ipsorum ratione, pro communis personæ ac compositæ munere, intelligamus operatum esse; quod tamen negat Monothelitas voluisse. Certe si — keyed to ἀνθρωπίνην — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Πᾶσαν θείαν καὶ ἀνθρωπίνην. Thus the Mono[the]lites seemed to guard themselves against a reef on both sides […], as was said in n. 8 […]: so as neither to be confused with Severus and the others […], nor to be divided from Nestorius and the Catholics themselves (towards whose side they were wrongly drawn). Indeed John of Constantinople himself and his synod, under the emperor Bardanes, thought they had made fools of them [the Monothelites], and that these had confessed the catholic faith in the very words of their own party, […] and that he had led the imprudent man [the emperor] to confess it, with only the phrase 'of one and of two' (which seemed to cause disturbances in the Churches) suppressed: […] whatever may be said of this dispensation of John and his fellow bishops (among whom were also men conspicuous for holiness), whether they are to be condemned as having in fact betrayed the faith, as the Baronian censure would have it, or at least judged more mildly, or even praised for the skill of their dispensation, by which they warded off from the Churches as great a storm as they saw threatening them from the tyranny of a heretical prince. I think Pope Constantine accepted their satisfaction and did not press them more severely, since he recognised that they in fact held the catholic faith in their minds and had regarded nothing more in that business than the safety of the Churches. Maximus himself finds a sound sense here if we understand that the same Christ himself operated according to one and another [nature], that is, according to what he truly was, by different [operations], and from their [the natures'] account, in the capacity of a common and composite person; which, however, the Monothelites deny they intended. Surely if […]

  • p. 0125 (notae, n): Ὅροι ἑνώσεων. Brevem hunc tractatum, postremum pene habet Turrianus, etsi non integrum, Græca, Romanus codex superioribus subji- ciebat. Quam 1 ponit, unitio est, ἡ κατ᾽ οὐσίαν· quam debemus accipere ut distinctam contra se- […]uentem, quæ est, ἡ καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν· quarum etiam […]agis conditiones exponit in fine; quod, ἡ κατ᾽ […]σίαν, sit in diversis hypostasibus, seu personis et […]ndividuis; ἡ καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν vero, in diversis sub- […]tantiis seu naturis. Cyrillus tamen et Patres alii, […]σιώδη et φυσικὴν ipsam unionem in Christo ὑπο- […]τατικὴν vocitant, quasi veram, et ut distinguant […]ontra σχετικὴν Nestorii, id est, affectu solo constan- […]em, qualis est in sanctis per gratiam. Hypostasibus […]ndividuorum, non satis apte Turr. quod Maximus, […]πὶ τῶν ὑποστάσεων, τουτέστιν ἀτόμων, nisi aliter […]jus codex habuerit, ac certe aliter habuisse putem, nempe, ἐπὶ τῶν ὑποστάσεων, τῶν ἀτόμων, quæ sic […]lle sibi redenda putaverit, cum esset reddendum: In hypostasibus, quæ sunt individua; qui modus […]oquendi Græcis familiarissimus, ac in illis Maximo. In seq. habuisse videtur, ὡς ἐπὶ ψυχῆς καὶ σώματος, quod et clarius est, ac libens sequor. — keyed to ἑνώσεων — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Ὅροι ἑνώσεων (Definitions of unions). Turrianus has this short treatise almost last, though not complete; the Roman Greek codex appended it to the above. The union which he places first is that κατ᾽ οὐσίαν, which we must take as distinct from the one following, which is ἡ καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν; and he sets out the conditions of both more fully at the end: namely, that the union according to essence is in diverse hypostases, that is persons and individuals, while that according to hypostasis is in diverse substances or natures. Cyril and other Fathers, however, call the union in Christ itself essential and natural, hypostatic, as being true, and to distinguish it from the relational union of Nestorius, that is, one standing in affection alone, such as is in the saints by grace. 'Hypostases of individuals': not quite aptly Turrianus, in that Maximus [says] ἐπὶ τῶν ὑποστάσεων, that is, of individuals, unless his codex read otherwise, and I think it certainly read otherwise, namely ἐπὶ τῶν ὑποστάσεων, τῶν ἀτόμων, which he thought he should render as it ought to have been rendered: In hypostases, which are individuals; a mode of speaking very familiar to the Greeks, and to Maximus among them. In what follows he seems to have had ὡς ἐπὶ ψυχῆς καὶ σώματος (as in the case of soul and body), which is also clearer, and I gladly follow it.
  • p. 0125 (notae, o): Ἡ κατὰ σχέσιν. Turr. secundum habitudinem reddit; sequentia non reddens, ὡς ἐπὶ τῶν γνωμῶν· qu[…] facile erat illi reddere, et ex illis vim τοῦ κατὰ σχέσιν, ex Maximi mente, et theologorum adversus Nestorium, magis perdiscere seu explicare. Est ergo unitio qu[…]e est per affectum, libera voluntate et ex arbitrio, cum plures per concordiam una sententia, uuus animus, una voluntas sunt: Εἷς ἓν θέλημα, ut sit una voluntas, non quod reddit idem Turr. ut una voluntatibus. Hæc nempe una Christi voluntas Nestorio, ejusque ταυτοβουλίας (consensu scilicet in objecto) quæ communis est sanctorum cum Deo, a quorum communi classe ἀνθρωπολάτρης hæreticus Christum non eximebat: nullam substantialem unitionem, ac vere subsistendi ratione, in eo ponens, sed solam affectualem (ut sic loquar) et quæ est per gratiam, in qua solum haberet potiores partes, ut qui reliquis, acceptis a Deo melius usus esset, exque profectu, in Filium Dei evasisset, non a prima exsistentia, natura Filius exstitisset. Hæc sic impura ac Judaica Nestorii, in quem nemo Catholicorum indulgentior esse debeat, et ut malum potius metaphysicum quam scelestissime in Filium Dei blasphemum, ejusque omne dispensationis evertentem mysterium, ingerat. Qui de hara Calvini Ministellus in Septimania tentavit haud ita pridem Nestorium levare, Cyrillo detrahere, is suas in se contineat tenebras, nec orci novam fuliginem (cui satis sit Calvinianus candor hactenus Christianissimo orbe tantum non repressus) in claram ejus pubem, celebri orbis Lycæo procul aspergat. — keyed to σχέσιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Ἡ κατὰ σχέσιν (the union by relation). Turrianus renders it 'according to habit', not rendering what follows, ὡς ἐπὶ τῶν γνωμῶν (as in the case of judgements); which it was easy for him to render, and from these to learn more thoroughly, or to explain, the force of κατὰ σχέσιν, according to Maximus's mind and that of the theologians against Nestorius. So it is the union which is through affection, by free will and choice, when many through agreement are of one opinion, one mind, one will: Εἷς ἓν θέλημα, 'that there be one will', not as Turrianus renders it, 'that there be one in wills'. This one will of Christ, then, [was held] by Nestorius, and his ταυτοβουλία (agreement of will in the object) which is common to the saints with God; from whose common class the man-worshipping heretic did not exempt Christ, placing in him no substantial union and none by true subsistence, but only an affective one (so to speak), which is through grace, in which he would only have the better parts, so that as one who had made better use of what he had received from God, and from progress, he came to be the Son of God, and was not Son by nature from the first existence. This impure and Jewish doctrine of Nestorius, against whom no Catholic should be too indulgent, he brings in as a metaphysical evil rather than as most wicked blasphemy against the Son of God, overturning the whole mystery of the dispensation. Let him who of late, the little minister from the Calvinist brood in Septimania, tried to lighten Nestorius and to disparage Cyril, keep his own darkness within himself, and not sprinkle a new soot of hell (for whom the Calvinist candour, hitherto scarcely restrained in the most Christian world, should suffice) upon the clear face of the famous Lyceum of the world.
  • p. 0125 (notae, p): Π καθ᾽ ἁρμονίαν. Idem atque ἁρμός, commissura, compago. In animalibus proprie dicitur; hincque adeo in ædificiis, quorum structura sic maxime alii aliis lapides committuntur. Ἁρμὸς λ[…]-θου, Lapidis commissura, citatur ex Schol. Aristophanis. — keyed to ἁρμονίαν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Ἡ καθ᾽ ἁρμονίαν (the union by fitting). The same as ἁρμός, a joint, a fastening. It is properly said of animals; and hence also of buildings, whose structure is such that stones are joined most closely to one another. Ἁρμὸς λίθου, 'a joint of stone', is cited from the scholiast on Aristophanes.
  • p. 0125 (notae, q): Ἡ κατὰ […]ναλοιφήν. Unionem hanc, quæ maximi sit Græcis theologis, acutissime exponit. — keyed to συναλοιφήν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Ἡ κατὰ […]ναλοιφήν (the union by […]). He explains most acutely this union, which is of the greatest account to Greek theologians.
  • p. 0126 (notae, q): Maximus ex eorum mente. Nempe advertit vir divinus in theologia maxime usurpari hanc unitionem, et ab illa, uno omnium Patrum consensu removeri: ita nimirum ut veras in Deo intelligamus emanationes, quibus reipsa personæ procedant, Filius et Spiritus, qui tamen cum Patre auctore non coalescant ac confundantur, sive etiam inter se, sed suam quæque hypostasim, et quod vere in persona exsistunt, habeant. Næ igitur frigide Turr. unio secundum conjunctionem. Certe quæ secundum conjunctionem unitio est, haud ita abhorret a Trinitate. Nam quæ major conjunctio, quam qua una persona in altera est, et quasi illi immeat, per συμπεριχώρησιν, quam theologi Græci vocant. Est ergo conjunctio (non enim divisio et separatio), sed sine confusione, veraque eorum quæ sic conjuncta sunt, distinctione. Greg. orat. De theol. ἑνοῦμεν δὲ τὴν θεότητα, καὶ οὐκ εἰς ἓν τὰ τρία συναλείφομεν. Chrysost. in I ad Cor. Οὐ τὰς ὑποστάσεις συναλεί- […]ων, ἀλλὰ τῆς οὐσίας τὴν ὁμοτιμίαν ἐμφαίνων. Damasc. De Trin. Ἑνοῦνται γὰρ αἱ ὑποστάσεις, καὶ τὴν ἐν ἀλλήλαις περιχώρησιν ἔχουσι, δίχα πάσης σ[…]ναλοιφῆς καὶ συμφύρσεως. Reddat Turr. Uniuntur et inter se commeant absque ulla conjunctione. Ergo summo divortio. Implicat certe in adjecto. Mihi, absque ulla coalitione, concretione et quasi conflato, et confusione. Συναλείφειν Gregorio, τὸ συναιρεῖν· et συναλοιφή, συναίρεσις, Sabelli propria; conjunctio quasi elisione, et quasi ad unum medium redactis quæ uniuntur, extremisque compressis, velut fere apud grammaticos fit in syllabis. More nostro loquimur in divinis, in quibus tamen non licet communibus Patrumque vocibus resilire, vel ita detorquere. Hæc satis pro opusculi parvitate. — keyed to συναλοιφήν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Maximus, from their mind. The divine man notes that this 'union' is chiefly used in theology, and is removed from it by the one consent of all the Fathers: so that we understand true emanations in God, by which the persons actually proceed, the Son and the Spirit, who however with the Father as source do not grow together and are not confounded, or even among themselves, but each has its own hypostasis, and what truly exists in a person. So Turrianus is flat in [rendering] 'union according to conjunction'. Surely a union that is according to conjunction does not so much shrink from the Trinity. For what greater conjunction than that by which one person is in another and as it were dwells in him, through περιχώρησις, as the Greek theologians call it. There is therefore a conjunction (for it is not division and separation), but without confusion, and with a true distinction of what is thus conjoined. Gregory, orat. De theol.: ἑνοῦμεν δὲ τὴν θεότητα, καὶ οὐκ εἰς ἓν τὰ τρία συναλείφομεν. Chrysostom on 1 Cor.: Οὐ τὰς ὑποστάσεις συναλεί-[…]ων, ἀλλὰ τῆς οὐσίας τὴν ὁμοτιμίαν ἐμφαίνων. Damascene, De Trin.: Ἑνοῦνται γὰρ αἱ ὑποστάσεις, καὶ τὴν ἐν ἀλλήλαις περιχώρησιν ἔχουσι, δίχα πάσης σ[…]ναλοιφῆς καὶ συμφύρσεως. Let Turrianus render: 'They are united and dwell in one another without any conjunction.' Therefore [it is] at the greatest divorce. It certainly implies a contradiction in the adjective. To me: without any coalition, concretion and as it were fusion, and confusion. Συναλείφειν in Gregory is τὸ συναιρεῖν (to contract); and συναλοιφή, συναίρεσις is proper to Sabellius; a conjunction as if by elision, with the things united brought to one middle and their extremes squeezed together, as nearly happens in syllables among grammarians. We speak in our usual way in things divine, in which however it is not lawful to recoil from the common words of the Fathers, nor so to distort them. This is enough for the smallness of the opusculum.
  • p. 0126 (notae, r): Καὶ συνοδικαρίου. Sit forte qui judiciis ecclesiasticis præerat, vel qui ab epistolis quasi synodalibus Paulo archiepiscopo esset. Num quasi ἔκδικος seu συνέκδικος, defensor, vindex? Quid si ambæ jungendæ voces, Ῥήτορος καὶ συνοδικαρίου, ut altera alteram exponat, sitque orator, cujus partium erat ut pontificias sanctiones ac decreta exponeret? seu etiam ex illorum ratione subditis jus diceret; qualis fere quem episcopi officialem vocant. Vellem certiora monumenta, quibus ejus munus totum explicari posset. Valde certe inficetus homo, qui nugas ejusmodi, magis quam subtile aliquid habentes quæstiones, obtruderet. — keyed to συνοδικαρίου — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Καὶ συνοδικαρίου ('and synodical official'). Perhaps he who presided over ecclesiastical courts, or who was in charge of what we might call synodal letters under Paul the archbishop. Is he as it were an ἔκδικος or συνέκδικος, a defender, an avenger? What if the two words are to be joined, Ῥήτορος καὶ συνοδικαρίου, so that one explains the other, and he be an orator whose part it was to expound pontifical sanctions and decrees? or also, according to their reckoning, to dispense justice to subjects; such as the person whom bishops call an official. I would like surer records by which his whole function might be explained. A very uncouth man, to press such trifles, which hold questions rather than anything subtle.
  • p. 0127 (lettered, o): Joan. xx, 17. — keyed to vestrum
  • p. 0127 (lettered, p): Joan. xiv, 28. — keyed to major
  • p. 0127 (lettered, q): Prov. viii, 22. — keyed to creavit
  • p. 0127 (notae, s): Τὰ τῆς ἀγ[…]ωμ[…]σύνης ῥ[…]ματα. Nempe Eunomii, qui a Christo ac de Christo dicta ad ejus astruendam humanitatem, ac quibus ipsius in humanum genus commendetur dignatio ac benignitas, in illius deitatis injuriam improbe summaque ingratitudine trahebat, uti etiam Billius observavit. Chrysostomo passim εὐγνωμοσύνης verba, relata scilicet ad Christum, qui illa verborum expressione Patri deferat, et se illi minime contrarium ostenda[…], suamque cum originem agnoscat. — keyed to ῥήματα — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Τὰ τῆς ἀγνωμοσύνης ῥήματα (the words of ingratitude). Namely, of Eunomius, who dragged the things said by Christ and about Christ, to establish his humanity, and by which his condescension and kindness toward mankind are commended, wickedly and with the greatest ingratitude into an insult to his deity, as Billius also observed. In Chrysostom, words of gratitude (εὐγνωμοσύνης) occur everywhere, referred to Christ, who by that expression of words defers to the Father, shows himself not at all contrary to him, and acknowledges his own origin.
  • p. 0128 (notae, t): Οἰκείωσις δέ. Scite Maximus arrogationem distinguit, qua sibi quis arrogat, quæ reipsa habet, et qua ea, quæ sunt aliorum, ut se unum quid cum illis reputat. Sic in Christo præter naturalia, ipsa vere et in unitione, in ipso exsistentia, κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν proinde naturalem, ut idem Maximus loquitur; quæ sibi vere ac natura Christus vindicaret ac tribueret; erant ἐν τῇ κατ᾽ ἐπίνοιαν διαστολῇ, ut subtili mentis cogitatu a deitate quasi sejuncta caro intelligebatur, ac unum nobiscum ratione hominis, velut præcisione a Verbo sustentante, atque eam habente; κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν σχετικήν, arrogatione illa quæ est secundum affectum, ac quadam necessitudine, etiam illa quæ nobis ex peccato inoleverunt; ignorantia, non subjectio, et similia: ipsa vere nunquam in ipso, quod neque in illa præcisione, sed vere semper in unitione: at neque in nobis ut συστατικά, quasi ad naturam pertinentia, illamque constituentia, sed pravæ voluntatis labe invecta. Idem clarius sequenti ad Marinum tomo dogmatico. — keyed to δὲ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Οἰκείωσις δέ (appropriation). Maximus distinguishes rightly between the arrogation by which one claims for oneself what one really has, and that by which one counts what belongs to others as one thing with oneself. So in Christ, besides the natural things, those truly in him and in the union, existing in him, [there is] a κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν [appropriation] hence natural, as Maximus himself says; things which Christ would claim and attribute to himself truly and by nature; they were in the division κατ᾽ ἐπίνοιαν (by thought), as the flesh was understood by the subtle thought of the mind as sundered from the deity, and one with us in the account of man, as by a precision from the sustaining Word who has it; κατ᾽ οἰκείωσιν σχετικήν (by relational appropriation), by that arrogation which is according to affection and a certain necessity, even those things which have grown in us from sin: ignorance, not subjection, and the like: these are never truly in him, since not even in that precision but truly always in the union; but neither are they in us as constitutive, as if belonging to nature and constituting it, but as brought in by the stain of a depraved will. The same more clearly in the following dogmatic tome to Marinus.
  • p. 0130 (notae, u): Κατάγνωσις φύσεως. Nempe velle mori, quasi naturæ abdicatio est ac damnatio; cum ipsa esse et vivere appetat. Nec qui altiori proposito mortem contemnunt, ipsi mortem ambiunt, aut vitam defugiunt, sed meliorem vitam deteriori præferunt. — keyed to φύσεώς — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): Κατάγνωσις φύσεως (condemnation of nature). Namely, to wish to die is as it were a renunciation and condemnation of nature, since nature itself desires to be and to live. Nor do those who, for a higher purpose, despise death themselves seek death, or flee life, but prefer a better life to a worse one.

Sic puto exesam pene vocem rite restitui ac suppleri; nec in ea totoque Maximi discursu hæret aliquid scrupuli. — keyed to δυσωπούμενοι — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Τὰ φυσικὰ δυσωπούμενοι". Thus I think the almost worn-away word has been properly restored and supplied; and neither in it nor in the whole course of Maximus's argument does any scruple remain.
  • p. 0132 (numbered, 1): Philipp. iii, 13. — keyed to sufficis
  • p. 0132 (notae, x): Τόμος δογματικός. Hunc magna parte sive librum, sive epistolam, Anastasius Bibliothecarius in sua Collectanea retulit, ea deflorans ac sublegens, quæ Honorii papæ defensionem contineant. Ac sane gratulandum tantum monumentum et cimelium in pontificia Romæ asservatum gaza, vel nunc demum qua productum est lingua, Gallicani cleri sedulitate et ope; inque eo splendidissimi surgentis jubaris, pari nobilitate, vita, doctrina, serenissimi principis ducis d'Abret Em. card. propensissima liberalitate, ac episcopi suprema arce Palatini, in lucem prodire: ut eo, ac reliquis Maximi, filia suppetias matri eat, arcemque vel maxime Romanam, quo tempore tantis turbinibus quatitur, fulciat, et sibi ipsa in ejus salute incolumitatem atque gloriam, avita in sedem apostolicam pietate, quærat. Nec una Honorii hic defensio, sed et Anastasii Sinaitæ, et Gregorii Theologi. — keyed to ΔΟΓΜΑΤΙΚΟΣ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Τόμος δογματικός" (Dogmatic Tome). Anastasius the Librarian reported this work, whether a book or a letter, for the most part in his own Collectanea, culling and gleaning those parts which contain the defence of Pope Honorius. And indeed congratulation is due that so great a monument and treasure, kept in the pontifical store-house of Rome, has now at last been brought forth in the French language, through the diligence and aid of the Gallican clergy; and in it, by the most generous liberality of the most serene prince, Duke d'Abret, the Eminent Cardinal, with equal nobility, life and learning, a rising splendid light, and of the bishop, from the supreme Palatine citadel: so that by it and by the rest of Maximus's works, a daughter may come to the aid of her mother, and may support the Roman citadel above all, at a time when it is shaken by such great storms, and may seek for herself, in the Roman See's safety, security and glory, out of her ancestral piety toward the Apostolic See. Nor is this the sole defence of Honorius here, but also that of Anastasius the Sinaite and of Gregory the Theologian.
  • p. 0133 (lettered, u): Hebr. vi, 20. — keyed to nobis
  • p. 0133 (lettered, v): II Cor. iii, 18. — keyed to cultu
  • p. 0133 (notae, y): Τοῦ τῆς ἀναστάσεως φερωνύμως. Circumlocutio est, qua Anastasius Sinaita Antiochenus archiepiscopus Justino imperatore, describitur, libro adversus Joannem Philoponum; qui se fidei quasi indicem atque arbitrum, Catholicos inter et Severianos constituens, suæ illi lucubrationi Διαιτητὴν titulum fecerat, ut auctor est Nicephorus lib. xviii, c. 47, sive etiam περὶ ἑνώσεως, De unione. Lemmation ejus libri Anastasii refert Gretserus in Odegi proœmio, repertum inter adversaria Turriani. Ex quibus etiam τοῦ Διαιτητοῦ τεμάχιον, ex illius operis libro iv, pollicitus, cum quinque Anastasii orationes de rectis fidei dogmatibus editurus esset, fidem liberasse non videtur. — keyed to φερωνύμως — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Τοῦ τῆς ἀναστάσεως φερωνύμως" (of him who is rightly named from the Resurrection). It is a periphrasis by which Anastasius the Sinaite, archbishop of Antioch under the emperor Justin, is described, in the book against John Philoponus; who, setting himself up as a sort of judge and arbiter of the faith between the Catholics and the Severians, had given his own work the title 'Diaitetes' (the Arbiter), as Nicephorus attests, book 18, ch. 47, or also 'On Union', De unione. Gretser reports the heading of this book of Anastasius in the preface of the Hodegos, found among Turrianus's papers. Out of these he promised also a fragment of the Diaitetes, from book 4 of that work, when he was about to publish five orations of Anastasius on the right doctrines of the faith; he does not seem to have kept his promise.
  • p. 0135 (notae, z): Ὑπὲρ ἧς καὶ ἐξ ἧς ἑσπουδάσθη τῷ φιλοπόνῳ. Nempe Anastasio; quasi alludat ad ipsum adversarii nomen Φιλοπόνος · quo ipso Patres ægre eum indigitant, sæpiusque cum illis exciderit, velut emendando adjungunt, ἢ μᾶλλον ματαιοπόνος, ne sic ominato nominantes videantur aliquid indulgere Tritheitæ, aliisque erroribus nefasto hæretico. Paulo obscuriora videantur hæc Maximi, præsertim quod utriusque nos auctoris lucubratio deficiat; tum scilicet Anastasii pie hærentis menti Gregorii, nec tamen ea explicanda satis facilis, Philoponi depravantis, ac, si mavis, Matæoponi, non viri studiosi, sed hominis stulte laborantis. — keyed to φιλοπόνῳ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Ὑπὲρ ἧς καὶ ἐξ ἧς ἑσπουδάσθη τῷ φιλοπόνῳ" (for which and from which the diligent one was zealous). Namely, for Anastasius; as though he were alluding to the very name of his adversary, Philoponos ("lover of toil"); the Fathers reproach him with this name with distress, and, since it fell out with them often, add as a correction, "or rather Mataiopónos" (vain-toiler), so that, by thus pronouncing a bad omen, they may not seem to indulge Tritheism or other errors with an ill-starred heretic. These words of Maximus may seem somewhat obscure, especially because the writing of both authors is lacking to us; on the one hand, because of Anastasius piously clinging to the mind of Gregory, yet not easy enough to explain, and on the other, of Philoponus, who distorts it, or, if you prefer, Matæoponus, not a man of study, but of one foolishly toiling.
  • p. 0138 (notae, a): Προσελήφθη ἡμετέρα φύσις, οὐχ ἁμαρτία. Sana hæc admodum, unaque forte Honorio, perinde ac Magno Athanasio, cogitata: nec tamen Macarius, quanquam Honorium sequi auctorem sibi videbatur, unamque illam ex Adæ culpa nobis inolitam, exscindere voluntatem, damnationem evasit; quod nempe homo dementissimus, sic abesse a Christo, ipsoque Adamo, vitium illud voluntatis humanæ contendebat, ut nec ipsa voluntas in eis humana esset, sed solum divina: quod certe in sani hominis mentem ne quidem venire possit, nec in Honorii, sapientissimi viri præstantissimique antistitis venisse putandum sit; nisi forte, eo duntaxat sensu, quod et humana, ipsa divina diceretur, quod ex divinis rationibus, et pro eo ac naturæ a Deo inditum erat a creatione moveretur; seu immobili rectitudine, ut in Christo ob unitionem, seu etiam in malum vertibili ex ipsa potestate arbitrii, ut in Adamo: quæ humanæ voluntatis non abolitio est, aut cum divina confusio; sed nativa, ut a Deo est, eique subjecta, illius perfectio. Paulo ante τὸ, συνδρομῆς, quod legit Anastasius, aptius est: et paulo post idem prope repetitur, ascripta Patri incarnationis effectione per Filium ac Spiritus cooperationem. Nihil bene Anastasius, jungendo τοῦ γένους cum ἡδονήν et reddendo generis desiderium; cum sit libido generi dominans (nobis scilicet ac mortalibus) a seductione. — keyed to ἁμαρτία — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Προσελήφθη ἡμετέρα φύσις, οὐχ ἁμαρτία" (our nature was assumed, not sin). These words are quite sound, and were perhaps conceived by Honorius just as by Athanasius the Great; yet Macarius, although he seemed to himself to follow Honorius as his authority, did not escape condemnation in cutting out that one will inherited by us from Adam's fault; for that most senseless man contended that it was so absent from Christ, and from Adam himself, the vice of the human will, that not even the human will itself was in them, but only the divine; which surely cannot enter the mind of a sane man, nor is it to be thought to have entered that of Honorius, a most wise man and most excellent prelate; unless perhaps in this sense alone, that the will would be called human, indeed divine, because it was moved from the divine reasons and, in accordance with what had been bestowed on nature by God, from creation; either with immovable rectitude, as in Christ by reason of the union, or also with a power of the will capable of turning to evil, as in Adam: which is not the abolition of the human will, nor its confusion with the divine, but a native will, as it is from God, and subject to him, the perfection of that will. A little before the word 'συνδρομῆς', which Anastasius read, is more apt; and a little later the same is almost repeated, the effecting of the Incarnation being ascribed to the Father through the Son and the co-operation of the Spirit. Anastasius is wrong in joining 'τοῦ γένους' with 'ἡδονήν' and rendering 'desire of the race', since the pleasure is a lust ruling over the race (that is, over us mortals), arising from seduction.
  • p. 0139 (lettered, d): Marc. xiv, 36. — keyed to Pater
  • p. 0139 (notae, b): Πρὸς ὑποτύπωσιν ἡμῖν ἐκφαινόμενος. Ad nostram informationem elucens, ut nobis formula et exemplar esset: uti etiam Paulus II Tim. 1: Ὑποτύπωσιν ἔσχε ὑγιαινόντων λόγων, ὧν παρ' ἐμοῦ ἤκουσας: ubi Chrysost. metaphoram ait desumptam ex arte pictoria, qua pictores rem pingendam sibi delincant atque informant. Fulg., Formam habe sanorum verborum, quæ a me audisti. Anast. prorsus barbare, ad imaginationem nobis ostensa est; cujus et superiora, nullo sano sensu reddita; sed parcendum viro opera strenuo, sed non satis in Maximi stylo versato. — keyed to ἐκφαινόμενος — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Πρὸς ὑποτύπωσιν ἡμῖν ἐκφαινόμενος" (shining forth for our outline/pattern). Shining forth for our instruction, so that there might be a formula and example for us: as Paul too uses it in II Tim. 1: 'Ὑποτύπωσιν ἔσχε ὑγιαινόντων λόγων, ὧν παρ' ἐμοῦ ἤκουσας' (Hold the pattern of sound words which you heard from me); where Chrysostom says the metaphor is taken from the art of painting, in which painters outline and form for themselves the thing to be painted. Fulgentius: 'Hold the form of sound words which you have heard from me.' Anastasius: utterly barbarous: 'ad imaginationem nobis ostensa est' (was shown to us for the imagination); of which the earlier parts too are rendered without any sound sense; but one must spare a man diligent in his work, but not sufficiently versed in Maximus's style.
  • p. 0140 (lettered, f): Joan. vi, 38. — keyed to meam
  • p. 0140 (notae, c): Δέος οὖν πολλὰ βαίνειν. Istud erroris scribæ palam est. Anast. quibus duriuscule reddit, Timor ergo est opinari, Maximi litteram velut digito ostendit; nempe scripsisse, Δέος οὖν ἐπιβάλλειν, seu ἐπιβαλεῖν, quod etiam sentire, intelligere, opinari reddi quandoque queat: hic autem aptior ipsi ejus vocis communior acceptio, nempe adjicere, subdere. Sic certe Latinos Romæ excusare ait Maximus non scripsisse unius vocem in epistola, quæ tamen in Græcis exstaret: sicque subdititiam eam esse, ἐπιβεβλημένην, illorum fraude adjectam, qui tanti tantæque sedis antistitis auctoritate, novitati suæ præsidium quærerent: cum præsertim qua sic obscure simpliciusque conscripta erat, non videtur iis deesse vocis illius inserendæ occasio, ipsamque totam sibi rapiendi epistolam, quasi illa Honorius Sergio reipsa astipulatus esset. Hincque forsitan ejus tandem et Romæ relictum vadimonium, ne, dum satis purgari non poterat, labis aliquid per ejus defensionem videretur prima ipsa sedes in Honorii successoribus contrahere, viris scilicet tanto fidei zelo clarioribus, quanto Honorius vel certe leniori in eam opera, ejus ipso ortu, videbatur seipsum obscurasse. — keyed to βαίνειν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Δέος οὖν πολλὰ βαίνειν." This is plainly an error of the scribe. Anastasius, who renders it rather harshly, 'Timor ergo est opinari' (so it is fear to suppose), points to Maximus's letter as with a finger; namely, that he wrote 'Δέος οὖν ἐπιβάλλειν', or 'ἐπιβαλεῖν', which can also be rendered 'to feel, to understand, to suppose' at times; but the more apt, and more usual, sense of this word here is 'to add, to put under'. In this way the Latins at Rome, Maximus says, did not write the word 'one' in the letter, though it stands in the Greek; and so it is a spurious word, 'inserted' (ἐπιβεβλημένην), added by their fraud, who sought support for their novelty in the authority of so great a see: since especially in what was written so obscurely and simply, there seems no lack of occasion for inserting that word, and for seizing the whole letter for themselves, as though Honorius had in fact agreed with Sergius. And hence perhaps his summons and the pledge left at Rome in the end, lest, while it could not be sufficiently cleared, the first See itself might seem to contract some stain through his defence in Honorius's successors, men indeed as much the more distinguished for zeal of faith as Honorius, or at least by a milder effort in this regard, from his very origin, seemed to have obscured himself.
  • p. 0140 (notae, d): Τὴν οἰανοῦν κυρίωσιν. Quin Maximus ἀκύρωσιν scripserit, vix dubito, eamque vocem altera illi subjecta exposuerit, ἢ ἐκβολήν. Sic certe ambæ unæ voces ejusdem sensus ac probe Græcæ, sibique — keyed to κυρίωσιν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Τὴν οἰανοῦν κυρίωσιν" (any ratification whatever). That Maximus wrote 'ἀκύρωσιν' (annulment), I scarcely doubt, and that he explained that word by another subjoined to it, 'ἢ ἐκβολήν' (or rejection). So certainly both words are of the same sense and properly Greek, and to themselves [the note breaks off at the foot of the page and continues on p-0141: 'optime respondentes …'].
  • p. 0141 (notae, marker illegible): optime respondentes, non quod Anastasius videtur legisse, nempe κένωσιν, dum reddit exinanitionem; nisi et illi istud obrepsit vel scribæ vel typorum mendo, pro abolitionem. Sic passim Latinis depravantur voces, tum maxime, cum ex vetustis membranis, vel fallente oculo, vel manu præ[…]urre[…]te, exscribuntur, exque apographo vulgantur. — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [continuing note (d) of p-0140] ... answering excellently to each other, not because Anastasius seems to have read 'κένωσιν', in that he renders it 'exinanitionem' (emptying); unless this too slipped upon him, whether by the scribe or a printer's error, for 'abolitionem'. So everywhere words are corrupted by the Latins, most of all when they are copied out from old parchments, whether the eye deceives or the hand runs ahead, and are published from a transcript.
  • p. 0141 (notae, e): Πρεσβυτέρῳ τῷ ἐν Μαζαρίῳ. Mazara u[…]bs — keyed to Μαζαρίῳ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Πρεσβυτέρῳ τῷ ἐν Μαζαρίῳ" (to the presbyter at Mazarion). Mazara, a city [the note breaks off at the foot of the page].
  • p. 0142 (notae, e): Siciliæ littoralis in ora australi inter Thermas. Philipp. Ferrar. Libens Maximus scribit, his, qui Romanæ diœcesis erant; vel qui paulo remotiores ab urbe Constantinopolitana liberius et doceri fidem, et docere poterant, in ea urbe potissimum, longa præsulum Monothelitarum serie exagitatam ac pene excisam, donec tandem per Seniorem Romam, novæ fidei lux nova affulsit: nec tamen animorum omnem rancorem, etiam ei Honorio litans, eximere potuit, quin se ille brevi post explicaverit, in eo sacerdotum cœtu, qui iterum in Palatii Trullo, Justiniano II imperatore, convenientes, per absentiam aliarum omnium sedium patriarchalium, canones edidere, nec paucos apostolicæ sedi injurios; quo ausu schismati radicem posuere; ut bene Matthæus Caryophilus archiepiscopus Iconiensis, vir Græcorum catholicissimus, in Confutatione Nili Thessalon. De primatu papæ. Placetque illa ejus observatio: In summa, quidquid Orientales in conciliis egerunt clam papa, non sacris conciliis tribuenda sunt, sed artificiosis quorumdam machinamentis. De Honorio vero, Non ferebant quatuor Constantinopolitanos patriarchas anathematizari ut hæreticos Monothelitas, et non inseri Honorium Romanum. Idque adeo sit, quod ait Adrianus papa in viii synodo act. 7, dictum anathema Honorio post mortem ab Orientalibus, ὅτι ἐπὶ αἱρέσει κατηγορήθη· nec tamen sententiam ferre quemquam patriarcharum aut præsulum potuisse, εἰ μὴ ἡ τῆς αὐτῆς πρώτης καθέδρας αὐθεντία συνήνεσεν ἐπὶ τούτῳ. Reddebat Radd., Nisi ejusdem primariæ sedis accedente ad eam rem auctoritate. Tulisse itaque hanc injuriam Romanam Ecclesiam hæc significant, ut vel ea exulceratos Orientalium animos patriarcharum maxime ex nomine proscriptione e synodo Lateranensi, quovis modo liniret; uniusque periculo capitis, tot annorum schisma sopiret, ac Christi gregis tantam sibi partem, sicque illustrem (Senior Roma, novam; mater, filiam) adjungeret. Hæc Honorii antiquis […]ota defensio; hæc probata; hæc incolumis per tot sæcula, ante novitiam Sirleti ac sequacium cusam, steterat, stetitque ac stabit; ut sibi unius veritatis candor ecclesiasticis probe fultus monumentis, non conjectura nutans, semper sufficit: uno hoc Ecclesia feliciter vincit, nec ullam τ[…]ῖς ἔξω sua traducendi copiam facit; quin eorum imminet jugulo, hac vere armatura velut acies ordinata, illis terribilis — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [Continuation of note (e) on 'Mazara'.] ...of coastal Sicily, on the southern shore between Thermae. Philippus Ferrarius. Maximus writes it with pleasure, for those who were of the Roman diocese; or who, being a little farther from the city of Constantinople, were freer both to be taught the faith and able to teach it, in that city above all, which had been long harassed by a series of Monothelite prelates and almost cut down, until at last through Elder Rome a new light of faith shone upon it; yet he could not remove all rancour of mind, even in pleasing Honorius, but that he explained himself shortly after, in that assembly of priests who, meeting again in the Trullan hall of the Palace under the emperor Justinian II, in the absence of all the other patriarchal sees, issued canons, and not a few injurious to the apostolic see; by which daring act they laid the root of schism; as Matthaeus Caryophilus, archbishop of Iconium, a most Catholic man among the Greeks, rightly observes in his Confutation of Nilus of Thessalonica, On the primacy of the pope. And that observation of his is pleasing: 'In sum, whatever the Orientals did in councils without the pope is not to be attributed to the sacred councils, but to the artificial contrivances of certain men.' And of Honorius: 'They would not bear that four Constantinopolitan patriarchs be anathematized as Monothelite heretics, and that Honorius of Rome not be inserted.' And this is so, as Pope Adrian says in the eighth synod, act 7, that anathema was spoken against Honorius after death by the Orientals, 'because he was accused of heresy'; yet that no one of the patriarchs or prelates could pass sentence, unless the authority of the same first see agreed to it. Raddus rendered it: 'unless with the authority of the same primary see added to the matter.' That the Roman Church bore this injury these things signify: that she might soothe the embittered minds of the Orientals, chiefly by the proscription of the patriarchs by name in the Lateran synod; and by the risk of one head lull to sleep the schism of so many years, and join to herself so great a part of Christ's flock, and so an illustrious one (Elder Rome adding the new; the mother, the daughter). This defence of Honorius, known to the ancients [word blotted]; this was approved; this stood unharmed through so many centuries, before the novelty of Sirletus and his followers, stood and stands; so that the candour of one truth, well supported by ecclesiastical monuments, not wavering on conjecture, always suffices: by this one thing the Church happily conquers, and allows no opportunity of traducing her to those outside; nay, she hangs over their throat with this armature, like a battle-line drawn up, terrible to them.
  • p. 0143 (notae, f): Τῷ, περὶ Θεοῦ λέγεται. Videntur hæc verba ex margine in textum irrepsisse, nec aliud esse quam scholion ac indicationem Maximo non insuetam, qua signific[…]t de Deo solum illi sermonem esse; idcirco clausa visum est repræsentare. — keyed to ἄλλῳ — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Τῷ, περὶ Θεοῦ λέγεται" (it is said of God). These words seem to have crept from the margin into the text, and to be nothing other than a scholion and indication not unusual in Maximus, by which he signifies that his discourse there concerns God alone; therefore I thought it right to represent them enclosed in brackets.
  • p. 0143 (notae, g): Ὡς πρὸς ἐκείνην. Luxatam litteram, et laborantem in voce ἁπασῶν (quam unam mutavi ὁπωσοῦν) mihi videor restituisse, ut clarus sensus sit, et differentiæ explicatio tradita. Varin. certe vocem ὁπωσοῦν, voce ἁπλῶς explicat; nec aliter Maximus: ut ὁπωσοῦν et ἁπλῶς qualitati assignet, τὸ τοιῶσδε differentiæ; quomodo sic se habent, ut commune et proprium; et quod absolute, et certo aliquo modo; de pluribus, deque uno dicitur. — keyed to ἐκείνην — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): "Ὡς πρὸς ἐκείνην" (as in relation to that one). A dislocated reading, and one labouring in the word 'ἁπασῶν' (the one word I changed to 'ὁπωσοῦν'), I think I have restored, so that the sense is clear and the explanation of the difference given. Varin at any rate explains the word 'ὁπωσοῦν' by the word 'ἁπλῶς'; and Maximus not otherwise: so that he assigns 'ὁπωσοῦν' and 'ἁπλῶς' to quality, 'τὸ τοιῶσδε' to difference; in the way they stand thus, as common and proper; and what is said absolutely, and in some definite way; of several, and of one.
  • p. 0149 (lettered, h): Τὸ μὴ ἐξ ἀτελῶν. Variæ Græcis terminorum ac vocum acceptiones, quæ res in theologia difficultates parit, erroresque incautis innectit, uti quandoque ipse admonet Maximus. Exemplum istud quod adhibet τοῦ ἐνυποστάτου, paulo φυσικώτερον est. Ita sane in naturalibus, hypostasis ex imperfectis substantiis conflatur, ex quibus unum totum ac per se exsistens consurgit: in Christo autem utraque perfecta natura est; unde nec unum totum ut natura ex eis conflatur, sed unus perfectus Deus, perfectus homo in utraque exsistit; ipsasque non hypostases, sed ἐνυπόστατα alibi Maximus admittit. Hic vero quod ponit exemplum, de imperfecto quod perfecto utitur unione secundum confusionem, hoc forte velit. Partes, quæ sic accedunt perfecto, ex illa imperfecti cum eo mistione et confusione, ut sic ἐνυπόστατα esse, quæ vere in hypostasi cui adveniunt, exsistant, non ut accidentia, sed ut quid substantiale, nec extra hypostasim, etsi non sint ipsa hypostasis, quæ est totius ratio, ac per se seorsum a quocunque alio, exsistentis. Paulo post τὸ ἐνυπόστατον, ad ipsa trahit communia accidentia seu adventitia, quæ et ipsa in hypostasi exsistant: sed id remotius est, quod illa esse valde debile et secundum quid in ea habeant. Sequentia, excerpta potius sunt, ad sequentem usque epistolam ad Constantinum Sacellarium, quam justa aliqua lucubratio vel epistola; cumque ipsius Maximi nomine prænotati loci afferantur, conjectura sit ejus studiosum aliquem hæc potius quam ipsum Maximum digessisse; vel si primum Maximus digessit adversus Severianos et Monothelitas, sic illi digesta alius auxerit, uti fere aliqui exsistimant de quæst. ad Antiochum Athanasii nomine, deque Anastasii Sinaitæ in Scripturam, aliis Nicæni. — keyed to ἀτελῶν — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): On 'not from imperfect [parts]': Greek terms and the senses of words vary, and this causes difficulty in theology and ensnares the unwary, as Maximus himself sometimes warns. The example he uses of 'enhypostaton' is rather natural. Thus in natural things a hypostasis is compounded from imperfect substances, out of which one whole, self-existing thing arises; but in Christ each nature is perfect, so that neither is one whole formed from them as a nature, but one perfect God, perfect man, exists in both; and Maximus elsewhere admits them to be 'enhypostata', not hypostases. Here, however, the example he sets down, of the imperfect that is used by the perfect by union according to confusion, he perhaps means this: parts that thus join the perfect, from the mixture and confusion of the imperfect with it, so as to be enhypostata, which exist truly in the hypostasis to which they come, not as accidents but as something substantial, and not outside the hypostasis, though they are not the hypostasis itself, which is that of the whole and exists separately by itself from anything else. A little later he draws 'enhypostaton' to the common accidents or adventitious things, which also exist in the hypostasis; but that is more remote, since they have it only very weakly and in a qualified way. What follows is rather excerpts, as far as the next letter to Constantine the Sacellarius, than a proper treatise or letter; and since the passages are cited under Maximus's name, the conjecture is that some student composed these rather than Maximus himself, or, if Maximus first composed them against the Severians and Monothelites, that another enlarged his compilations, as some commonly suppose of the question to Antiochus under Athanasius's name, and of Anastasius of Sinai on Scripture, and others of the Nicene.
  • p. 0154 (lettered, i): Εἰ διὰ τὸ ἓν θέλημα. Non b[…]e Turr., inter cujus Maximi opuscula istud undecimum est, istam sententiam superiori jungit; quo cogitur decimam numerare, quod epilogi rationem habet. Habuerit ejus cod. εἶτα, εἰ, quod ille reddit, Deinde, si: nihil tamen id favet, ut non sit nova sententia seu argumentum, quæ alia aliis subnectere solent Græci ejusmodi particulis, uti fere etiam Latini. Titulus in Turr. cod. et Romano paulo diversus: illius est, Γνῶμαι ἀποδεικτικαὶ περὶ τῶν δύο τοῦ Χριστοῦ θελημάτων, Romani, qualem repræsentavi. — keyed to θέλημα — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): On "Εἰ διὰ τὸ ἓν θέλημα": [one or two letters of the third word lost] in the Turin codex, in which this opusculum of Maximus is the eleventh, it joins this sentence to the one above; hence it is forced to count a tenth, which has the force of an epilogue. His codex may have had εἶτα (then) for εἰ (if), which he rendered "Then, if": yet that does not prevent this from being a new sentence or argument, such as Greeks, and Latins too, commonly append with such particles. The title in the Turin codex and in the Roman differs a little: that of the former is "Γνῶμαι ἀποδεικτικαὶ περὶ τῶν δύο τοῦ Χριστοῦ θελημάτων" (demonstrative sentences on the two wills of Christ), that of the Roman is the one I have given.

  • p. 0155 (lettered, j): Διαβεβαιοῦνται.   • p. 0156 (lettered, j): desiderant Turr. codex, ipsæ videntur, quæ hic subjectæ sunt, post breve illud excerptum Responsionum ad Theodorum presbyterum; uti etiam censuit vir cl. Lucas Holstenius, ac Roma scripsit: Imo ipsæ sunt quibus Maximus abunde fidem liberat, Patrumque doctrinam ac traditionem de naturali voluntate et operatione sic enucleate, tanta vi ac copia promit, ut ad ejus operam nihil desiderari videatur; plenissimeque ea retundantur impudentissima Sergii, Pauli, aliorumque Monothelitarum hæresis antistitum ora, qui vel Patres illius non meminisse jactabant, vel ἀδιάφορον habendum volebant, sive una, sive duplex operatio atque voluntas dicetur, quasi neutrum ad fidem spectaret, satisque ei consultum esset, dissidentium parti[…] silentio; quod et Honorio primum sancitum, tum[…] circumscribente Sergio, ut loquitur Christianu[…] Lupus, tum quod ea indulgentia Theodosiano[…] aliosque Severianos adducendos speraret. Eju[…] tamen decretum nemini successorum probatum[…] excusatus animos, ne pari cum Ecthesi ac Typo[…] synodo Lateranensi calculo notaretur. Longa seri[…] refert laudatus Lupus c. 5 Notarum ad vi synodum, eos qui eadem locuti cum hæreticis, ipsaque[…] in illis proscripta, ipsi tamen hæreticis minime adscripti sunt, ac quæ sic locuti sunt, benignius habita. — working translation (a first reading by the apparatus reader, not yet reviewed): [Continuing the note on "Διαβεβαιοῦνται" from the previous page:] ...and which the Turin codex lacks, seem to be those which are subjoined here, after the short excerpt of the Replies to Theodore the presbyter, as the distinguished Lucas Holstenius judged, and wrote from Rome. Nay, rather they are the very ones by which Maximus abundantly frees his credit, setting out the doctrine and tradition of the Fathers on natural will and operation with such force and richness that nothing seems wanting to his work; and by them the most shameless mouths of Sergius, Paul and the other leaders of the Monothelite heresy are most fully refuted, who either boasted that the Fathers made no mention of it, or wished it held a matter of indifference whether one or two operations and wills be spoken of, as though neither bore on the faith, and enough had been provided for him by silence of the dissenting parties[…]; which was first decreed by Honorius, and then, as Christian Lupus says, by Sergius's circumvention, and because by that indulgence he hoped the Theodosians and other Severians could be brought over. Yet his decree was approved by none of his successors; [and he is] excused in intention, lest he be marked by the judgment of the Lateran synod as equal with the Ecthesis and Typus. The cited Lupus, ch. 5 of his Notes, relates at length, on the sixth synod, that those who said the same things as the heretics, and whose [words] were proscribed among them, were nevertheless by no means counted among the heretics, and that what they thus said was taken more kindly.

Opusculum 12 (Latin only): readings checked at the plate

Opusculum 12 survives only in Anastasius's Latin, which Migne prints without a Greek column (cols. 141-146); the Latin column is therefore the source and is translated as it stands. Every reading the translator flagged (108) was looked up on the page image (2026-11-06): 104 were slips in our transcription of the scan and are corrected without comment; 2 are readings where the plate prints the form the translator doubted (Migne's text, kept); 2 were not settled at the plate (a blotted word, and the line that carries Combefis's footnote 91, which is apparatus and not text). The unsettled and kept rows:

page.linethe scaffold readreadingverdictnote
p0087.L.20sine successu?Pplate prints sine successu receptionem
p0087.L.34wshoc??first word blotted (…mus,)
p0087.R.06laudatissimilaudatissime?Pplate prints laudatissimi (voc. pl.)
p0088.R.58vel eum sede cjectus eT Urbe in. Africavel cum sede ejectus et Urbe in Africa …?sentence ends ageret. gap before …em: line lost?
